PREFACE 

HIS is a very dull book, as the authors are well aware. They hope, however, that historical accuracy may atone somewhat for the dulness; that the fidelity with which they have endeavoured to set forth the mainly uneventful chronicle of a remote country parish may draw attention to some aspects of American ecclesiastical history of which we have been too generally heedless.

They wish also to thank all who have contributed such information as they possessed, and they especially desire to record their indebtedness to the records of the Venerable Society for the Propagation of the Gospel; to sundry data. collected by the late Ven. Jules Louis Prevost, M. D.; to the work of the late Henry Pleasants, Esquire, in connection with the history of St. David's Church at Radnor; to the manuscripts of the late Dr. Julius F. Sachse and others, now in the collections of the Church Historical Society; and their appreciation of many courtesies extended by Dr. St. George 

L. Sioussat and the staff of the Manuscripts Division of the 

Library of Congress, and by the staffs of the Historical Society of Pennsylvania, the Library of the Lutheran Seminary at Mount Airy, and the Chester County Historical 

Society at West Chester. Philadelphia, February, 1944.

HAROLD DONALDSON EBERLEIN CORTLANDT VAN DYKE HUBBARD,

INTRODUCTION By the Bishop of Pennsylvania, THE RIGHT REVEREND OLIVER JAMES HART, D.D., LL.D., S.T.D. 

✰✰✰ARISHES, like individuals, may be old in years P and young in spirit, possessing, as it were, the power of perennial youth. 

From the days of the Founders the Christian Church has played its part in the development of our national life. "Freedom of Worship" are words which have a modern sound but they are, in reality, an age-long expression of an ancient quest. We must ever remember that with the laity who came to seek freedom to worship God there were also the ordained ministers of the Christian Church who established Missions and Schools and supervised the religious life of many communities. 

To what is now our own Diocese the Mother Church of England sent courageous leaders who shared the daily hardships of primitive life and inspired the colonists to carry on in spite of difficulties which it is almost impossible for us to understand and appreciate.

St. Peter's-in-the-Great Valley has witnessed to the Faith since the turn of the eighteenth century when a little log Church was erected. During these long years the Sacraments have been administered and the Gospel preached. 

Two hundred years ago-1744-the stone Church was built. What memories hallow its plain and simple walls! 

In its well-kept "God's Acre" sleep the valiant-hearted men. and women who knew and loved it. They are not dead. 

Across the years they speak-if we are wise enough to hear. 

In this Bi-centennial year it is peculiarly fitting that the colonial simplicity of the Church should be restored, not that it may become merely a lovely "Shrine" but rather that

iv 

Introduction all who enter may feel the pervading influence of the holiness of beauty.

So, to all who maintain the high traditions of a noble Past and who are planning for the years to come I am happy to send this message of sincere congratulation and good wishes. That the spirit of the Eternal God may continue to guide and bless this Parish Church is my earnest prayer. 

OLIVER J. HART.

CHAPTER I Parent Mission and Sister Churches 

COR nearly half a century the two Churches of St Peter-in-the-Great Valley and St. David at 

Radnor formed one and the same parish. Like a sturdy elm whose trunk divides in two, when it has reached a certain height, with twin trunks thereafter continuing upward in co-equal and almost parallel growth, this venerable parish in the fulness of time became two independent cures of souls.

But from an early stage of the joint parish history, there were always two separate church buildings as a matter of physical necessity. The territory covered by the mission that afterwards became two distinct parishes was of such broad extent, reckoned even by modern standards, that it was evident almost from the first that one single gathering place was impracticable. With roads that were bad at the best, and sometimes well-nigh impassable, even on horseback, there had to be two separate places of worship to accommodate the widely-scattered and increasingly numerous parishioners, many of whom found it impossible to traverse the long distances to reach any one more or less central point of this still wild and difficult area. And, indeed, after there were two separate church buildings, they were still quite as far apart as most of the Colonial churches in 

Virginia and Maryland.

When the parish began life as a single mission, without any dedicatory designation-full parochial organisation and definite dedications evolved gradually with the passage of years the people worshipped in a log chapel. Seemingly trustworthy tradition places it in the neighbourhood of the

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley present Berwyn. This log chapel, as tradition, confirmatory allusions and sundry demonstrable facts all indicate, was built about 1700, perhaps a little earlier. 

The colonists in the Welsh Barony lived at the very edge of civilisation when they reared this humble place of worship. To the west and north was the untamed wilderness; to the south and southeast, until they came to the Delaware, there were only sparse settlements of Swedes and English; twenty miles to the east lay Penn's infant Philadelphia, but communication with the tiny city was so discouraging to travel that for years to come the Welsh Barony with the adjacent country, where Welsh was the common (and official) tongue, was to remain an imperium in imperio and was, for the most part, sufficient unto itself of necessity as well as by choice.

The log chapel near Berwyn served the Church of England congregation of Welshmen until 1710 or 1711 when, tradition says, it was destroyed by fire. Tradition also adds that it was "burned by design," whatever sinister action that may imply. Just what happened for several years after this is not recorded. Church services were probably held at different farmhouses.

At all events, in 1715, the people at the lower or southeastern end of the parish built a fine stone church, St. 

David's at Radnor. The people at the upper or northwestern end of the parish built a log church, the predecessor of the present stone Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley, a structure finally completed in 1744; this stone church was originally very similar in design to St. David's, though somewhat larger. 

In what year the people of Tredyffrin, East Whiteland and Charlestown reared the log church is not recorded. Nor can we certainly identify its site. In all likelihood, it stood somewhere in the neighbourhood of the present church. 

There is a tradition, which seems reasonable, that the old stone steps and mounting block north of the church, now standing alone and unrelated to anything else, once stood beside the door of the log church. The earliest gravestone in the churchyard-it has long since disappeared-bore the

Parent Mission and Sister Churches 

3 date 1703; it would be natural to build the church in ground that had already begun to be used as a burial place. Finally, since a grist mill was always a convenient, necessary and accessible focal point in early Colonial times, the proximity of the Great Valley Mill (dating from shortly after 1700) would probably be a factor in determining the position of the log church somewhere in the vicinity. 

As to the date of its building, persistent tradition has it that in 1744, when the new stone church was at last finished, the log church had already been standing a long time, some say forty-odd years-long enough, in any case, to need replacement or drastic repairs. From this tradition (which, be it noted, was handed down nearly a century ago by people then very old, whose memories went well back into the eighteenth century) we can only conjecture that it may have. been built either before or soon after the burning of the original log chapel near Berwyn, although its building may equally well have been some years later, for aught we know. 

Be the story of the log church what it may-it is not likely we shall ever definitely know much more of its site and date-the present stone church that succeeded it in 1744 inaugurated a new era in the life of the parish. In 1745 the new structure was dedicated as the Church of St. Peter-inthe-Great Valley and a duly constituted Vestry was elected,* some of whose members had previously served in St. 

David's Vestry. From that time St. Peter's and St. David's may date their existence as two separate and distinct churches, although both continued under the ministrations of the same successive clergymen until 1836 and the final separation of interests and clerical responsibility did not come until 1845-a hundred years subsequently. 

Prior to 1745, in all his correspondence with the Venerable Society for the Propagation of the Gospel in Foreign Parts, Parson Currie always referred to the neighbourhood around the log church as the "upper part of my parish." 

After the stone church was finished and dedicated, and the "upper part of my parish" had achieved individual identity The first Vestry may have been elected in 1744. A torn margin in the old minute book of the Vestry has left the last figure missing; it was April 15th, either 1744 or 1745. 

The second entry of a Vestry meeting is unquestionably dated December 4th, 1745.

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley as St. Peter's, with a complete Vestry of its own, and no longer suffered under the anonymity of the "log church," 

Parson Currie always wrote of "St. Peter's" or "the Valley" -incidentally, he deplored the difficulty of getting the two churches to pull together in the matter of providing a common glebe and glebe house for his residence as parson of both St. Peter's and St. David's.

So much for a short preliminary outline of the physical beginnings of St. Peter's. Let us now turn to a more explicit study of the parish history, from its inception in 1700 down to the present day.

CHAPTER II  

I had in less than three years after my Arrivall a very numerous Congregation Consisting for the most part of persons brought over from the Quakers and other Sectaries to the Church of England; and the true Religion (by the Frequent Resort of Persons from Remote Parts to Philadelphia), did soe spread, and the number of Converts did increase so fast, that I was obliged to divide my selfe among them as often and as Equally as I cou'd, till they were Formed into proper Districts, & had Ministers sent over to them by the Venerable Society.

For this Reason I went frequently to Chichester [now Marcus 

Hook], which is 25, Chester or Upland 20, Maidenhead 40 (where I baptised 19 Children at one time), Concord 20, Evesham, in West 

Jersey, 15, Montgomery 20, and Radnor 15 miles distant from Philadelphia; All which, tho' Equally Fatigueing, and Expensive I frequently went to, & preached in, being by all meanes determined to lose none of those, whom I had gain'd, but rather add to them till the Society otherwise Provided for them. 

But Montgomery and Radnor next to my owne beloved Philadelphia had the most considerable share in my Labours, where I Preached in Welch once a fortnight for 4 years, till the Arrival of Mr. Nichols Minister of Chester in 1704, about which time also the 

Revd Mr. John Thomas my late Assistant came for England……….

There is another Welch settlemt called Montgomery in the County of Philadelphia, 20 miles distant from the City; where are considerable numbers of Welch People, Formerly in their native Country of the Communion of the Church of England, but about 1698 two years before my arrival in ye Countrey most of them joyned with the

Quakers, but by God's blessing some of 'em were Reduced, and I have baptized their Children and preached often to 'em, especially while my late Assistant Mr. Thomas continued wth me; I visited them since & prevailed upon them to meet every Lord's day abt 40 in number, where One that can understand the Language well, & is a sober discreet man, reads the Prayers of the Church every Lord's day, ye proper Psalms and Lessons, omitting only the Absolution, and what properly belongs to the Priest's Office & then Reads some Portion, in a Booke of Devotion to the People; I mett with Severall good Bookes translated into the Welch Language among my Countrey People, particularly the whole Duty of man in Welch, & ye Practise of Piety, as for the Christian Monitor; Dorrington's Familiar Guide to the Lord's Supper, the Advice of a Minister to his Parishioners, all in Welch, wt I received, were Faithfully Dispersed, but were so few, yt a greater number is stil wanting."

The State of the Church in Pensylvania, most humbly offered to ye Venerable Society for the Propagation of the Gospel in Foreign Parts, a memorial presented to the Society by the Revd Evan Evans, London, September 18, 1707.

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley "Within land lies Radnor or Welshtown, finely situate and well watered, containing about fifty families; in this place is a congregation of Church of England-Men; but no settled minister." 

-British Empire in America: Oldmixon, 1708. 

HE long quotation at the head of this chapter is not put there for decorative purposes, though some readers seem to fancy that such is the purpose of prefatory excerpts. Dr. Evans's memorial to the Venerable Society contains the clue to the early history of the parish and will be referred to again and again until all its available testimony is exhausted. 

There is no available record extant that tells in so many words the exact date and place at which the mission had its beginning, or the names of those in attendance. Lacking such a specific record, we have to "put two and two together" to get the story. But there are enough two's and two's which, put together in logical relation, give a fairly clear and consistent picture. And the main features of this picture closely coincide with long-standing local tradition. 

Sent from England in 1700 by Bishop Compton, of London, to succeed the deceased Reverend Thomas Clayton in the cure of Christ Church, Philadelphia, the Reverend Evan Evans-an indefatigable, clear-headed Welshmanwas aggressively evangelistic, especially with regard to his fellow-countrymen in the Province. His initiative and untiring exertions brought about the establishment of Church organisation in the Welsh Barony. To gain some idea of the field into which he directed his labours, one must grasp the religious complexion of the Province at the time of his coming to Philadelphia.

By 1689, the Friends had reported that nearly all the Welsh in Pennsylvania, of whom there were many, were either professing Quakers and members of Meeting, or else were "Quakerly affected" and ready to join the Society of Friends. Then, in 1691, came the Keithian schism and split. 

Quakerdom wide open. The followers of George Keith*- *Born c. 1638, George Keith had been closely associated with Fox and Barclay in

England and was one of the weightiest members of the Quaker Society. In 1685, he was appointed Surveyor-General of New Jersey. In 1689, he became Head Master of the 

William Penn Charter School in Philadelphia. Coming into sharp doctrinal variance with

an acute-minded, aggressive and combative Scot, of distinguished mental attainments-condemned the Quaker doctrine that what they termed the "Light within" was allsufficient for man's salvation without need of any other means of grace; the Foxian Quakers, led by Thomas Lloyd, the Deputy-Governour, hotly and stubbornly resented the charge that they were in error. So serious was the rift between the two factions that their Meetings were rent asunder into bitterly hostile camps fairly bursting with animosity. The Quakers had always been jealous of their politico-religious ascendancy and tenacious of their control over the Provincial government; by the same token, they cordially disliked the recognition of any other religious body, in spite of the express provision in Penn's charter (for which we may thank Bishop Compton) "that if any of the inhabitants of the said pvince, to the number of Twenty, shall att any time hereafter be desirous, and shall by any writeing or by any person deputed for them, signify such their desire to the Bishop of London, that any preacher or preachers to be approved of by the said Bishop, may be sent vnto them for their instruccon, that then such preacher or preachers shall and may be and reside within the said pvince, without any Deniall or molestaccon whatsoever."

The wholesale defection of the Keithians made the Foxian Quakers-who retained their domination of the Provincial Legislature-more anxious than ever to gather within their fold (at least nominally or passively) all the inhabitants of the Province they could muster; it led them likewise to put whatever obstacles they could in the way of them that held other religious convictions. Enter Parson Evans, in 1700, to take charge of Christ Church, when the acrimonious atmosphere engendered by the Quaker dissensions had but partially cleared; many of the Keithians were in receptive mood to heed enlightened religious leadership. 

While it is true that great numbers of the early Welsh colonists were Quakers when they left Wales, and so remained when they seated in the Welsh Barony or other the other Quaker leaders in Pennsylvania, he led off a large following of "Christian" or "Separatist" Quakers. In 1692, the Philadelphia and Burlington Yearly Meetings issued a declaration of "disunity" with him; in 1695, the London Meeting "disowned" him. In 

1700, he joined the Anglican Church and took Holy Orders, ordered deacon and priested by the Bishop of London.

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley parts of Pennsylvania, it is also equally true that there were many more who were not Quakers-by birthright, subsequent "convincement," or inclination-although in certain instances they may have paid nominal adherence to Quakerism after settling here.

These non-Quaker Welshmen had migrated to Pennsylvania, "not to avoid any Violence at home, but to improve their Fortunes in those Parts." Some of them, "bred up Members of the Church of England," had "here unhappily fallen into Quakerism, for want of a Minister; as being disposed to follow that, rather than to have no Form of Religion, and were ready to return back to the Church of England." 

Others had never even made any pretense of being "Quakerly affected," as George Keith put it, and clung loyally to their Church of England allegiance, although they had neither church nor pastor in the new land. 

It was these latter, and perhaps some of the Keithians too, who had occasionally availed themselves of the ministrations of the Swedish clergy and, as circumstances permitted, resorted to the Swedish church at Weccacoe. To discourage them and prevent this "pernicious" practice, the Foxian Quakers sought to hinder their crossing the Schuylkill on Sundays. Of this "small potatoes" incident in our

Colonial history, Dr. Sachse writes: 

"Many of the Welsh, however, yearned for Church services and, from the time of their arrival, even before the lay services were held within the bounds of the Welsh Tract, were wont to attend the Swedish services in the old Blockhouse on the banks of the Delaware at Weccacoe, stating that although they could not understand what was said, still they could bend their knees, offer up their silent prayer and receive the benediction on their departure. 

When the practice of the Welsh became known to the Quaker authorities, fearing that it would ere long generate the desire for Church services of their own, in their native tongue, they placed every obstacle in the way of the Churchmen possible. Among others, they prevented them from crossing the Schuylkill on Sundays and holy days; this led to a protest being sent to Governour Fletcher of 

New York, in 1693, who then ruled the Province under the Crown in place of Penn." 

The Quakers reckoned the Swedes hopeless subjects for "convincement" or conversion and did not care whether they got to their church or not, but they hit at the "backsliding" Welsh through the Swedes, and the petition to the Gover-

nour and Council is filed under the name of the Swedes as complainants. Under date of May 11th, 1693, the Minutes of Council read: "Upon reading the petition of sundrie Swedes, Inhabitants upon the river Scuilkill, wherein they sett forth that their meeting hous is on the other side of the river: That they live three miles distant from the ferry, and that they are restrained from passing the nearest way to their worship on Sundays and Holy days by philip England, keeper of the ferry att Schuilkill.

His Excell. did offer his Inclinations to remove any obstruction that might be given to the worship of God, and his regard to the Interest of the proprietarie in the ferry, desiring the Councill's advice. 

The Council are of opinion, That the petitioners may have Libertie granted them to transport themselves over the river to & from their worship, provided they doe not abuse this Libertie to other ends, to the prejudice of the ferry." 

In the light of foregoing explanations about religious affairs among the Welsh colonists, Parson Evans's statement is apposite when he writes some years later in his memorial. to the Venerable Society that "true Religion (by the Frequent Resort of Persons from Remote Parts to Philadelphia [to Christ Church]), did soe spread, and the number of Converts did increase so fast, that I was obliged to divide my selfe among them as often and as Equally as I cou'd, till they were Formed into proper Districts, & had Ministers sent over to them by the Venerable Society."

This accession of converts to the Church of England doubtless received not a little impetus from the missionary journey of the Reverend George Keith and the Reverend John Talbot, sent by the Venerable Society in 1702 to report on religious conditions in the American Colonies. In 1700, several years after his return to England, (as already noted) 

Keith had abjured Quakerism and taken Holy Orders in the Anglican Church, made deacon and priested by the Bishop of London. Now, when his erstwhile "Keithian," "Christian," or "Separatist" Quaker followers saw their former leader come back amongst them as a priest of the Church, not a few of them were ready without more ado to put in to the same ecclesiastical haven. The numerous additions to the Church's fold were not an undetermined number computed merely by loose guesswork. In his Journal, meticulous in recording detailed facts, Keith says that Parson Evans had baptised "above 500 Men, Women and

ΙΟ The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley 

Children Quakers, in Pennsylvania and West Jersey;" during his own "Continuance in those Parts," Keith and Talbot together baptised many more. Keith's Journal is very dull reading, but it does give much valuable information found nowhere else.

Although Christ Church, in Philadelphia, was the charge committed to him by the Bishop of London, Parson Evans, in his ardent missionary zeal (as hitherto pointed out), devoted unflagging efforts in ministering to the surrounding country and especially to his fellow-Welshmen to whom he could preach in their mother tongue. Without neglecting his duties as incumbent of Christ Church, he contrived to go "frequently to Chichester, which is 25, Chester or Upland 20, Maidenhead 40 (where I baptized 19 Children at one time), Concord 20. 

Evesham, in West Jersey, 15, Montgomery 20, and Radnor 15 miles distant from Philadelphia; All which, tho' Equally Fatigueing, and 

Expensive I frequently went to, & preached in, being by all meanes determined, to lose none of those, whom I had gain'd, but rather add to them till the Society otherwise Provided for them."

These missionary journeys to the country he made chiefly on week-days. At Christ Church, in addition to the regular 

Sunday morning and afternoon services, incessantly-busy Parson Evans had special Sunday evening services for young men. These proved highly efficacious for "conversion by eavesdropping"; in his memorial to the Venerable Society, Evans writes: "we Discover'd a visible Benefitt from Our evening Lectures; For those Quakers, that Durst not appear in the day at the Publiq Service of the Church, for fear of disobligeing their Parents or Masters wou'd stand under the Church windows att night, till many of them pluck't up so much Courage, as to come to ye Church it selfe, and at last by the blessing of God upon ye word preached, Submitted to the Holy Ordinance of Baptizm, & continue Stedfast in the Communion of the Church of England." 

After enumerating the outlying fields of his missionary endeavours, and their several distances from the city, Parson Evans continues in his memorial, "But Montgomery and Radnor next to my owne beloved Philadelphia had the most considerable share in my Labours, where I Preached in Welch once a fortnight for 4 years, till the Arrivall of 

Mr. Nichols Minister of Chester in 1704."

This passage unmistakably and beyond all peradventure dates the beginning of his organised mission work, from

II which common root the sister churches of St. Peter's and 

St. David's ultimately grew. Parish history began in 1700. 

Here, however, must follow a note of identification about the "Montgomery" linked with Radnor in the memorial as a recipient of "the most considerable share in my Labours." "Montgomery," if not at the actual site, was in the immediate neighbourhood of where St. Peter's now stands.* 

Still further confirmatory of missionary activity, encouraging response from the people, and regularity of services at "Montgomery" (Parson Currie's "upper part of my parish"), is the later-quoted passage from Parson Evans's memorial of 1707: 

"There is another Welch settlemt called Montgomery in the

County of Philadelphia, 20 miles distant from the City; where are considerable numbers of Welch People, Formerly in their native Countrey of the Communion of the Church of England, but about 1698 two years before my arrival in ye Countrey most of them joyned with the Quakers, but by God's blessing some of 'em were Reduced [brought back], and I have baptized their Children and preached often to 'em...; I visited them since & prevailed upon them to meet every Lord's day abt 40 in number, where One that can understand the Language well, & is a sober discreet man, reads the Prayers of the Church every Lord's day, ye proper Psalms and Lessons, omitting only the Absolution, and what properly belongs to the Priest's Office & then Reads some Portion, in a Booke of Devotion to the People;

This custom of regular services with a lay reader, when the clergyman in charge could come only once or twice a month, obtained perforce in many of the early churches, and for a long time after their firm establishment, because the Colonial clergy were few in number and oftentimes one priest had to carry the burden of two, three or four widely-separated congregations.

In his solicitude for his flock at "Montgomery," Parson *In a footnote on p. 43 of his History of Old St. David's Church: John C. Winston 

Co., Philadelphia, 1915, Mr. Henry Pleasants writes: "The location of Montgomery,' so particularly referred to by Mr. Evans in the foregoing letter, is difficult to establish, but the following collection of data on the subject for which the writer is wholly indebted to Rev. Jules L. Prevost-presents very strong reasons for believing that the reference is to a very early settlement about Old St. Peters Church, Great Valley, and that the establishment of Episcopal services there was at least coincident with their establishment at Radnor. "In Humphreys's 'Historical Account of the Society for the Propagation of the Gospel in Foreign Parts,' published in 1730, is appended a 'Map of N.Eng. N.York, N.Jersey &

Penna.-1730,' indicating towns to which missionaries are sent and showing 'Montgomery' in the general location of St. Peter's Church, Great Valley, in addition to 'Radnor' and 

Whitemarsh' evidently corresponding to the sections of Radnor, Merion and Montgomery in Wales; 

In 1938, Dr. Prevost and Dr. A. W. Baugh again closely examined this map, carefully figured the bearing and distance of Montgomery from St. David's, and came to the fully convinced conclusion that Montgomery and St. Peter's were one and the same.

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley 

Evans did not confine himself to holding services there and preaching in Welsh as often as he could; making occasional visits to parishioners; or arranging to have a lay reader carry on the Church services regularly during the intervals. between clerical attendance. The teaching he could impart only by sermons when present himself, or by the selections he appointed the lay reader to read, he sought to supplement by distributing books among the people who, it seems, already had some doctrinal or devotional literature in their possession. That explains the allusion in his memorial, "I mett with Severall good Bookes translated into the Welch Language among my Countrey people. particularly the whole Duty of man in Welch, & ye Practice of Piety, as for the Christian Monitor."

It explains also his allusion to books already distributed by him, and his plea for more to distribute: "Dorrington's Familiar Guide to the Lord's Supper, the Advice of a

Minister to his Parishioners, all in Welch, wt I received, were Faithfully Dispersed, but were so few, yt a greater number is stil wanting." 

No one was more keenly alive than Parson Evans to the absolute need of ministering to the Welsh colonists in their own tongue, if the Church was to grow and flourish amongst them. In the memorial to the Society, just before he speaks at length of the mission at Montgomery, he writes: "The Welch at Radnor and Merioneth in the Province of Pensylvania have addressed my Lord of London (having a hundred hands to their Petition), for a minister to be settled amongst them, that understands the British Language, there being many ancient People among those Inhabitants, that doe not understand the English, and cou'd a Sober and discreet person be procured to undertake that mission, He might be capable by the blessing of God to bring in a Plentifull Harvest of Welch Quakers, that were Originally bred in the Church of England, but were unhappily perverted, before any minister in Holy Orders, that cou'd preach to 'em in their own Language, was sent into Pensylvania, But I believe they are not irrecoverable, had they an Itinerant Missionary, who wou'd use application & diligence to reduce 'em to ye Communion of the Church." 

Since Dr. Evans speaks of this petition as having already (1707) been sent, it seems not at all unlikely that he himself had prompted its sending. In mentioning "Radnor," in all likelihood he was including "Montgomery" as well, in a general way, for they were so near together geographically. 

Because of this comparative proximity, it was apparently

becoming the habit-the habit had certainly become fixed at a later date to refer rather loosely to "Radnor" and "Montgomery" together merely as "Radnor," as though they were one and the same mission or parish as, indeed, they were, although spread out in two separate neighbourhoods. At any rate, the passage just quoted plainly shows that Dr. Evans's efforts had already borne fruit amongst the 

Welsh to the extent of getting them to petition the Bishop of London (according to their charter rights, as previously pointed out) for a resident clergyman, either to be sent directly by his Lordship, as Dr. Evans had been sent to 

Christ Church, or to be appointed by the Venerable Society as one of their regular missionaries. The Oldmixon statement of 1708 confirms the existence of an organised body of Churchmen at that date in the Welsh Barony. His allusion to "fifty families" would seem to indicate the inclusion of Montgomery with Radnor, and to accord with the number of signatures on the petition to the Bishop of London since, as Parson Evans says, the Churchmen of Merion joined with those of Radnor in their memorial to his Lordship. 

The Venerable Society for the Propagation of the Gospel in Foreign Parts, although anticipatory mention has necessarily been made of it, was not founded until 1701. In that year also it received its charter from the Crown. From the very outset of its organisation its leading spirits showed themselves level-headed and used sound common-sense in their planning. In evidence of their vision, one of their first acts was to send John Talbot and the converted George Keith in 1702 on their missionary-surveying errand to the American Colonies, as previously stated. Not depending on this source of information alone to guide them in their future appointment of missionaries, they also wrote the Colonial Governours and other persons of recognised weight and position in the conduct of Colonial affairs desiring them to communicate to the Society their opinions respecting the religious state of the several Colonies. Their replies to the 

Society give a rather gloomy picture of the virtually unchurched condition of a large part of the British Colonial territory. One of the worst showings of the time came

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley from Colonel Caleb Heathcote,* first Lord of the Manor of Scarsdale in the Province of New York. In his letter to the Society in 1704 he writes: "Being favoured with this Opportunity, I cannot omit giving you the State of this Country, in relation to the Church, and I shall begin the History thereof, from the Time I first came among them, which was about 12 Years agoe. I found it the most rude and 

Heathenish Country I ever saw in my whole Life, which called themselves Christians, there being not so much as the least Marks or Footsteps of Religion of any Sort. Sundays were only Times set apart by them for all manner of vain Sports and lewd Diversions, and they were grown to such a Degree of Rudeness that it was intolerable. I having then the Command of the Militia, sent an 

Order to all the Captains, requiring them to call their Men under Arms, and to acquaint them, that in Case they would not in every Town agree among themselves to appoint Readers, and to pass the Sabbath in the best Manner they could, till such Times as they could be better provided; that the Captains should every Sunday call their 

Companies under Arms, and spend the Day in Exercise; whereupon it was unanimously agreed on thro' the Country, to make Choice of Readers; which they accordingly did, and continued in those Methods for some Time."

In his History of the Society, Humphreys adds, "This Description given by that worthy Person, who proved afterwards highly instrumental in settling Religion, both here and in the Neighbouring Countries, was confirmed by many Accounts from other Hands."

Theocratic New England, though in a condition by no means to warrant complacency, was apparently not so rampantly "rude and Heathenish" as to prompt commanders of the Militia to tell people they must either say their prayers or else spend Sunday in military drilling. In summing up the report gathered from the replies to the Society's letters of enquiry, Humphreys says of South Carolina that "besides Negroes and Indians, living without any Minister of the Church of England, . . . above half the People" lived "regardless of any Religion." In North Carolina, the summary *Colonel Caleb Heathcote, born in the Hundred of Scarsdale in Derbyshire, after which he named his Manor of Scarsdale in Westchester County at its creation in 1701, married the daughter of Chief-Justice William Smith, of St. George's Manor, Long Island. Colonel

Heathcote was "Surveyor-General of His Majesty's Customs for the Eastern District of 

North America; Judge of the Court of Admiralty for the Provinces of New York, New

Jersey and Connecticut; and a member of His Majesty's Council for the Province of New 

York. He was Mayor of the City of New York from 1711 o 1714, during a part of which time his brother, Sir Gilbert Heathcote, was Lord Mayor of London. "Besides all his many other interests, he was deeply devoted to the welfare of the Church of England, of which he was a staunch and faithful adherent. To him, more than to any other one man, was the Church of England in New York City and in Westchester County indebted for its support and growth, so long as he lived." 

-Manors and Historic Homes of the Hudson Valley: H. D. Eberlein, 1924.

notes "above 5000 Souls without any Minister, any religious Administrations used; no Publick Worship celebrated, neither the Children Baptized, nor the Dead buried in any

Christian Form." Maryland and Virginia were rather better off, though understaffed with clergy. Conditions were a little better still, perhaps, in Pennsylvania and the "Country of 

Newcastle," as the Three Lower Counties (now Delaware) were sometimes called, but not enough so to create any particularly bright spot in the generally sorry prospect. The 

Society were solicitous, too, about the Negro slaves and Indians. They were shocked to learn, as Humphreys relates, that "Besides, the Negroes were much discouraged from embracing the 

Christian Religion, upon Account of the very little Regard shewed them in any religious Respect. Their Marriages were performed by mutual Consent only, without the Blessing of the Church; they were buried by those of their own Country or Complexion, in the common 

Field, without any Christian Office; perhaps some ridiculous Heathen

Rites were performed at the Grave, by some of their own People. 

No Notice was given of their being sick, that they might be visited; on the contrary, frequent Discourses were made in Conversation, that they had no Souls, and perished as the Beasts."

The letters the most responsible and representative people in the Colonies wrote the Venerable Society about the prevailing religious conditions disclose an aspect of Colonial history we are too prone to overlook. These reports convinced the Society of the need for prompt missionary action to avert the menace of a demoralisation affecting the whole Atlantic seaboard. The missionaries sent out and supported by the Society, from the early years of the eighteenth century up to the outbreak of the Revolutionary War-some of their benefactions, indeed, continued even later-exerted an effect upon American life which it is impossible to overestimate. For the many and lasting benefits conferred by the 

Society's steadily maintained programme of missionary support, American Churchmen owe an immeasurable debt of gratitude.

Of exactly what clerical ministrations the people of Montgomery and Radnor enjoyed, after Parson Nichols came to Chester in 1704 and Parson Evans went to England in 1707, we have no record. We have already seen that Parson

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley 

Evans provided for Sunday services at St. Peter's to be conducted by a lay-reader when no clergyman could be present. Such an arrangement, in all likelihood, continued at both St. Peter's and St. David's, with occasional Sunday visits from the clergy as opportunity served and, it would seem, more or less stated visits and preaching from the clergy during the week. Although the letter is somewhat later in date (1718), a communication from the Churchwardens of Trinity Church, Oxford, to the Secretary of the Venerable Society, in describing conditions in that parish probably gives an equally true picture of what was then also taking place and had been taking place in preceding years -at St. Peter's and St. David's, especially since Oxford at that time was coupled with the two-church parish in the Welsh Barony as a single missionary cure or unit assigned to the pastoral care of one missionary priest (when one was available), sent out by the Society for the Propagation of the Gospel. The letter of the Oxford Churchwardens says: It may not be unacceptable to their Honours to let them know what we are doing to keep ourselves together in this our necessity until we have a minister which will be good news to us when he does come.

We humbly let their Honours know after this manner we having no minister on a Sunday (except by chance), agree among ourselves to meet at the House of God every Sunday where one Nathaniel Walton our school master one zealous for the Church and of good report among us takes due pains every Lord's day to read unto us the

Holy Scriptures as they are appointed to be read, also the Prayers and Psalms in their Order and Course, and a Homily or Sermon he reads every Sunday where also he Catechises the Children that are of the Church every Sunday, duly being 20 in Number and more often comes to be Catechis'd in the Church The Revd Dr. Evans gives us his benediction in doing what we do, & the Learned Revd Mr. John Talbot highly commends us in our 

Zeal for the Church."

Not every parish was blessed with a zealous "Nathaniel 

Walton our schoolmaster," but wherever there was a schoolmaster he appears now and again doing Sunday duty as a lay-reader. If the earliest-planted missions or parishes in the Province of Pennsylvania had depended solely upon continuity and regularity of clerical ministrations, they would have perished in short order. There simply were not enough parsons to go round. Though most of them, like 1

Evan Evans, in the face of manifest difficulties offered by great distances and wretched roads, did all that was humanly possible to 'divide themselves . . . as often and as Equally as they cou'd' over a formidable stretch of territory, it was the determination and loyalty of the laity that maintained parochial existence in most places. 

In the ten years between 1704 and 1714, then, it would seem that St. Peter's and St. David's had to depend mainly on lay-readers for regular Sunday services, with intermittent and irregular clerical visits (as previously noted) on Sundays or, more usually, on week-days. Who the clerical visitors were during this period we can only surmise, basing conjectures on probabilities and the knowledge that such clergymen as were in the Province at this time gladly responded to calls for ministrations outside their immediate charges, so far as they could. Among these possible or probable visitors, the first in point of time would be the 

Reverend Henry Nichols, sent to Chester in 1703 and remaining there till 1708. He was the first resident missionary appointed in Pennsylvania by the Society for the Propagation of the Gospel. Beyond his known energy and missionary zeal, and the comparative nearness of Chester, however, there is nothing to indicate definitely that he pastorally visited Montgomery or Radnor. In 1707 the

Venerable Society appointed the Reverend Thomas Jenkins to Appoquiminy. Parson Evans says of him (in his 1707 memorial to the Society) that he "has a Competent knowledge in ye Welch tongue; and if ye most honble Society wou'd be pleas'd to give it him in charge to visit those people [a Welsh settlement between Appoquiminy and New Castle] as oft as may be, It would be a meanes by God's help to keep those in ye Communion of the Church, that are allready Joyned wth it, and to Reduce others that have been seduced."

Parson Jenkins had scarcely reached Appoquiminy before the Reverend George Ross, the missionary at New Castle, inveigled him to leave his charge (without any authorisation from the Society) and transfer his labours to Chester, vacant by the removal of Parson Nichols to Maryland. No sooner had he taken up the work at Chester than Mr. Ross

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley (again without any authorisation whatever from the Society) further tempted him to take charge of New Castle, while he himself removed to Philadelphia to take the mastership of the school there, from which he had succeeded in edging-out the Reverend John Clubb-all this to the great annoyance of Parson Evans at his return from England to the charge of Christ Church in 1709. For this unsanctioned departure from his post at Appoquiminy the Society reprimanded Parson Jenkins and he straightway went back and continued his labours in his designated cure. He seems to have been hypnotised into this dereliction by Mr. Ross's persuasive powers.

Whatever Parson Evans thought of Mr. Ross's meddling and its consequences in various quarters, he had an high opinion of Parson Jenkins and his earnestness. While he was at Chester and New Castle, it is not at all unlikely that Parson Jenkins may occasionally have visited and preached at Montgomery and Radnor; the people there were always clamorous to have someone preach to them in their own language, and neither Chester nor New Castle were distant enough to offer a serious obstacle to his going. He could not have gone after 1709, however, for in July of that year the chronicles of the Society record that he "died of a calenture caused by the Musketoes." Apparently this means that he was a victim of either malaria or yellow-fever, more likely the former.

As to the other clerical visitors during the period from 1704 to 1714 it is possible to speak with somewhat more certainty, although there was still no missionary definitely assigned to the charge. The Society appointed the Reverend 

John Humphreys to Trinity Church, Oxford, in 1711.

There he remained until 1713 or 1714, when they transferred him to Chester to succeed Parson Ross, whom they had sent back to his old post at New Castle. John Humphreys was energetic, determined, full of missionary zeal and very much to the fore in all local matters where the interests of the Church were concerned. Judging from the character of the man, and what we know in general of his activities (from the records of the Society), it is highly likely that he

visited the congregation of Montgomery and Radnor when he could and preached to them in their own tongue, in the period prior to 1714. We know that he did afterwards, when he was stationed at Chester, and seemed no stranger to the field, for in a letter to the Society's Secretary, in 

October, 1718, he says: "Since Dr. Evans left us to go to Maryland, which was in the summer, I observe his turn to supply the Church at Radnor,* which is 20 miles from home, once a fortnight upon Thursday, likewise the Church at Oxford which is 28 miles, so that I preach every Lord'sday, and every Thursday . . . .

When Parson Evans came back from England to Christ Church in 1709, it is perfectly reasonable to assume that he continued his former deep interest in the people of the 

Welsh Barony and visited them whenever he could—in all likelihood, with considerable regularity. Furthermore, in a certificate from the Churchwardens and Vestry of St. 

David's Church, enclosed in Dr. Evans's letter of June 29th, 1719, to the Venerable Society, they say: "This is to certifie that the Revd Dr. Evans hath preached the 

Gosepl at Radnor at the House of Mr. Wm Davist one of the Subscribers once a Fortnight from Nov in ye year one thousand seven hundred all ye time he was residt. at Philadelphia without any reward from us, and since his return from England which was on the twenty-second day of March 171 untill the latter end of June last past he preached at St. David's Church at Radnor and at Trinity 

Church at Oxford alternate every Thursday (Vizt) one Thursday here and another at Oxford aforesd. during which time he deported himself with prudence in all respects becoming a Person of his sacred Character. He laid ye foundation of ye Church of England first in these parts as well as other places in this province and we have great reason to lament his departure for Maryland. Mr.

Humphrey Preacheth here when the affairs of his other Church will permit."

It is well, at this point, to remind the reader of a matter to which attention has already been called that the parish in the Welsh Barony was commonly designated (rather carelessly, perhaps) by the title of that lower end of the parish that had the substantial stone church with a dedicatory name, while the log-chapel upper end remained anonymous, though very vigorously existent. In most of the Society's records of the first half of the eighteenth century, if one reads "Montgomery and Radnor" for "Radnor," they will not be far wrong historically with respect to the one parish with two separate church buildings. †William Davies or Davis, on coming from Wales in 1685 seems first to have settled in Philadelphia, removing soon afterwards to the Welsh Barony. Thomas Allen Glenn says (Pennsylvania Magazine of History and Biography: Vol. xxxvii, p. 247) that the log cabin or chapel near the present Berwyn, already mentioned, p. 1, was built on his property. 

This might mean that a congregation gathered at William Davies's house even before 1700. 

In any event, there is nothing to conflict with the tradition about the original log chapel already noted in Chap. I. William Davies, it is thought, may have been a "Keithian" 

Quaker before turning to the Church. Unfortunately, Mr. Glenn gives no authorities for his statements relative to William Davies and the "church in his house."

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley 

The mention of Parson Humphreys coincides with the statement in his own letter previously quoted. 

The Reverend John Clubb* who, as Schoolmaster, became associated with Christ Church in Philadelphia as early as 1705 or 1706 and, as a Welshman, was able to assist

Parson (Evans in his ministrations to the Welsh people of the Barony, almost certainly came to St. Peter's and St. 

David's at an early date. Even when he took charge of 

Trinity Church, Oxford, after the death of the Reverend Andreas Rudman (the Swedish pastor of the Gloria Dei at Weccacoe, who had ministered at Oxford for several years), there is good inferential reason to believe that he continued his visits to the Welsh Barony at every possible opportunity. 

Certain it is that the people there were deeply attached to him. In 1712, the Venerable Society sent him from Oxford to Appoquiminy whither, obedient to orders, he went. However, to judge from his letter to the Secretary (quoted by 

Henry Pleasants: History of Old St. David's Church; John 

C. Winston Co., 1915. p. 43), he was not happy and asks the Society to restore him to Oxford: "Or if it seem not good to them to grant me this my request yet it would please them to place me among my countrymen the Welch at Radnor (being not above 17 or 18 miles distant from Oxford). among whome likewise I have great hopes of being usefull and doing good, haveing been to preach to them of late in our Native Language as often as my attendance in the church (wherewith I am more immediately concerned) would give me leave. I find a numerous 

Body of them all desirous of the Benefit of a Minister duly ordained to Read and Preach the Word of God and to Administer the ordinances of Christ to them as you'll find by their Addresses to my Lord of London and the Honble Board."

An address of the Clergy of the Province of Pennsylvania to the Venerable Society, in 1712, sets forth the cause of

Parson Clubb's discontent at Appoquiminy and prays that he may be stationed at Radnor: "The case of Mr. Clubb stands thus: He was, about 15 months ago, ordered by you to Apoquinomy, whither, in submission to your determination, He immediately repaired, tho' both contrary to his own inclinations, and without any conveniency to accommodate his family in that place, and he being still destitute of any such accom- *In a letter of August, 1705, Governor John Evans, referring to Mr. Clubb's connection with Christ Church, writes: "He has been Assisting in some Measure to our 

Minister Mr. Evans, whose Charge is very considerable, and requires a great deal of Service."

modation, and very much impaired in his health since he went to reside there, we beg leave to lay his request before your Hon'ble Board, viz: That he may either be appointed Missionary for his 

Countrymen, the Welsh, at Radnor, who have sent several addresses for a Minister, and will, we are sure, kindly receive him, they being by inclination as well as experience of his good services already done them, extremely desirous of his settling among them Multi- tudes of the Welch people in these parts, who are generally very ignorant, have fallen away to the Quakers, Anabaptists, Sabbatarians, &c. But these at Radnor have been preserved steady to the Church by the means of Mr. Evans and Mr. Clubb, who preach and converse with them in their own Language. We therefore submit it to your pious consideration whether you will not out of your usual charity to the Souls of Men be pleased to furnish these poor people with a minister which if you think fit to do we are of opinion Mr. Clubb will be a very proper person for them."

At long last Parson Clubb's own personal appeal, the unanimous hearty endorsement of his fellow-clergy in the Province, and the repeated pleas of the Welsh people in the 

Barony bore fruit. The Venerable Society, after due consideration and talks with Parson Clubb in London (whither he seems to have gone at the summons of the Society), finally appointed him to be missionary at Radnor and also, according to a minute of April 23rd, 1714, "Agreed that Mr. Clubb do supply the cure at Oxford in manner proposed by him to the Committee, and that Mr. Humphrey the 

Society's Missionary at Oxford in Pennsylvania be removed to 

Chester in the same province . . ."

Thus after fourteen years, during which time staunch and loyal laymen had kept the parish or mission organisation alive, with such aid and encouragement as hard-pressed and devoted Parsons Evans, Clubb and other provincial clergy could give, did the Welsh people of Montgomery and Radnor receive a definitely-assigned missionary priest of their own, their first "proprietary pastor," who could minister to them in their native language.

While recording the names of our own clergy-both those whom we know to have laboured in this field during the fourteen-year formative period, and those who more. than likely gave occasional help-it is fitting to acknowledge the many valuable services to our own Church performed by the Swedish clergy, priests with valid orders, especially 

Andreas Rudman, Eric Bjorck and Andreas Sandel. The

Society for the Propagation of the Gospel were fully aware

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley of the timely and unselfish aid afforded by the Swedish clergy and, so far as it was possible, recognised their good offices by granting rewards. The Swedes were indefatigable missionary workers and constantly penetrated far into the wilderness to minister to scattered colonists. While we have no proof that any of them ever exercised their pastoral office amongst the Welsh of the Barony, it is well within the bounds of possibility that they may occasionally have administered the sacraments during these destitute years of clerical dearth.

Parson Clubb's deep satisfaction and happiness at entering upon his new duties are evident in his letter of October 

6th, 1714, to the Secretary of the Society: "My safe arrival at Port Philadelphia was (thanks be to God), upon the 24th of Augst after a long passage of 13 weeks and the bearing of several difficulties which the tediousness of our Voyage and the number of our hands aboard was in a great measure the occasion of. During which time I faithfully performed my duty dayly in reading prayers, morning and evening, and preaching every Lord's day in which offices of devotion I found those Dissenters (that were on board wth us), always willing and ready to joyn and I believe would do so every where else if they were not prevented by those that are their leaders.

In a few days after, when my effects were delivered me, I entered upon my charge at Radnor and Oxford, where the people were well satisfied and ready to receive me, and Mr. Humphreys as willing to go to Chester as the Society were pleased to appoint him their 

Missionary in that place.

The people of Radnor are very thankful to ye Honble Corporation that you were pleased at last to consider them and send them the

Minister they desired for ye welfare of their Souls. Their promise is now much after the same rate wth that mentioned in their address by me, vizt, of making what Provision they are able for their Minister tho' no certain yearly stipend. 

They met me unanimously upon the day appointed, vizt, the 7th of the last month, and at the same time heartily engaged themselves to build a handsome stone Church. They subscribed that day a tolerable sum to the carrying of it on and obliged themselves to make it good and for the rest I shall use all the means I am able to effect it by collection. Indeed they are a large Congregation of well affected people to the principles of the Church and deserved your charitable consideration long before. .

I humbly presume (that as I understood some small time before I came away Mr. Lasinby had given some books, viz: Bibles and of them that are titled 'The Whole Duty of Man,' to be disposed by the Society in the Plantations where their Missionaries are), to beg of your Honble Body that you will be pleased to bestow upon us what portion you think fit of such, there being here a great many youth wholly unprovided of Bibles and non to be purchased but at such excessive rates as they are not able to comply with . 99

Parson Clubb took up his residence at Oxford and from there administered both his cures. The distance between the two was great, but Oxford was nearer the city and it was presumably easier to arrange living conditions there than at Radnor where there was no glebe. The coupling of Oxford with Radnor as a single missionary assignment, under the care of one priest, was a topographical blunder and misfortune; from the outset it created insuperable difficulties that at last resulted in Parson Clubb's death. This illadvised arrangement came about more by chance, perhaps, than design. Parson Clubb had already had a long connection with Oxford parish; he was attached to the people and they to him. During his former years of service there he had, it would seem, been largely instrumental in getting the present brick church built. Naturally he had been loath to leave Oxford and go to Appoquiminy. He was happy at the thought of a return. On the other hand, he had likewise had long and intimate connection with the congregation in the Welsh Barony; his zeal for working amongst his fellow- 

Welshmen, who had so long and so persistently petitioned for a missionary of their own blood and tongue, drew him strongly towards the field at Radnor. In his representations to the Society while he was in London, he let his enthusiasm. override good judgement; with the besom of enthusiasm he could brush aside obstacles of space and see Radnor and Oxford only 18 miles apart. The actual distance was nearer 

28 miles, and the roads were bad. He failed to reckon the physical difficulties in the way of filling both cures, and he overrated his own strength. The Society, quite understandably, having no accurate topographical knowledge of the Province, relied upon Parson Clubb's familiarity with local conditions in assenting to this utterly impracticable arrangement.

True to their promise, the congregation at Radnor set about building the "handsome stone Church" and, on May 9th, 1715, assisted by the Reverend Andreas Sandel, pastor of the Gloria Dei at Weccacoe, the Right Reverend Non- 

Juror John Talbot, and several other clergy, Parson Clubb laid the cornerstone. The demands of the two parishes,

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley along with the great distances he had to travel in all weather, inevitably overtaxed Parson Clubb's strength. 

Nevertheless, he worked so assiduously and so incessantly that he failed to make semi-annual reports to the Society or keep other explicit records. That he worked to good purpose, subsequent communications of the Vestry and contemporary letters attest, but the exposure and exacting labours to which he subjected himself soon ended his life. 

By all accounts he was a saintly and self-effacing priest who never stopped to count the cost to himself when there was a missionary task to perform. In referring to his death, a letter from the Churchwardens and Vestry of Radnor to the 

Venerable Society, in 1720, says: "Mr. Clubb our late Minister was ye first that undertook ye Care of Radnor & Oxford & he payed dear for it for ye great fatigue of riding between ye two Churches in such Dismall wayes & weather as we generally have for four months in Winter soon put a period to his life." 

He died on Christmas Day, 1715, before the new church was fully finished. That the then "upper part of the parish" at Montgomery also received its share of Parson Clubb's ministrations there can be little doubt. Although there is no specific statement to that effect, in a letter of October 3rd, 1715, to the Secretary, Priest Humphreys, at Chester, writes: "Mr. Clubb has likewise his hands full as well as we, preaching frequently to 2 or 3 congregations besides those immediately committed to his charge by the honble Society." 

From our knowledge of the man himself, his intense sense of duty to minister especially to his fellow-Welshmen, and the local circumstances, we can interpret this in only one way. 

Parson Clubb's untimely death left the Churchmen of the Welsh Barony and Oxford again dependent upon such occasional clerical attention as the few neighbouring priests could manage to give them. For some years thereafter the records disclose no communication to the Society from the Welsh Churchmen, but the Churchwardens and Vestry of 

Oxford sent a letter (May 21st, 1716) to the Secretary

that throws some light on the conditions affecting both parishes. It says, "To the most Revd Rt Revd & the other Revd and worthy members of the honble Society for promoting the Gospel in Yse parts it hath pleased Almighty God to take unto himself, our loveing & beloved Pastor, Mr. John Clubb, yr Mission' who departed this life, in Decr last past, and ever since we have been destitute of a minister: only a certain minister who lately came from England, upon account of the oaths & other impositions hath sometimes preached to us, as he hath had an opportunity, but we hear he is not like to continue in these parts, being threatened also here wth the same abjuring oath [evidently the Non-Juror John Talbot] and so we are like to be as sheep without a Shepherd. Therefore in this case we most humbly implore your help, that as we have a Church wherein to meet and worship God, so we may be accommodated wth a minister to officiate for us in the things that pertain unto his worship, and we hope that such yr Charity will redound to your own reward & to the increase of religion in these parts, & to the glory & praise of God." 

A letter (of August 28th, 1716) from the Reverend 

George Ross, of New Castle, to the Secretary says: "There are so many places in the province of Pennsylvania, and territories, that want your ministry of the word, and so few Missionaries to supply them (and now fewer than ever) that except the honble Society will please to send us help, we who endure so long the heat of the day must needs give out, while we are spent so much in journeying often, & travelling far from our several places of residence and habitation." In a postscript he adds . . . "There are but two Missionaries in Pennsylvania, Mr. Humphreys [of Chester], and myself, for as for Mr. Talbot, we look upon him as of a distinct government, tho' he resides now mostly at Philada. Now as to our affection to the Government of King George, our demeanour we think has been such at all times and in all places, that our Loyalty to & love for his Majesty King George cannot be questioned or complained of. If it is we are ready to answer whatever may be alleged to the contrary. We have never presumed to vary from the prayers of the Church by adding or curtailing in one jot or tittle; and if any complaints have been made they are false & groundless." 

A letter to the Secretary from the Reverend John Humphreys of Chester (August 4th, 1716) carries the same lament about the dearth of clergy. He says, "Mr. Talbot indeed assists us very much by takeing upon him the care of the Church of Philade of wch some care must be taken by us here," since the unsavoury Phillips scandal. Then he goes on to bewail "how easily are poor simple people destitute of teachers imposed upon and seduced by crafty smooth tongued deceivers. Great are the murmurings of these naked congregations against the Venerable Society for their neglecting to send them Missionaries, and all our

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley arguments wont prevent their complaints wch proceed from the bitterness of their Souls; and this is it wch renders many of them flexible to hear any Dissenter that offers himself unto them, wch is frequently done when we are at too great a distance to prevent it." 

Priest Humphreys's report of the discontent and complaints amongst the "naked congregations" reflected the general condition, a condition for which, however, the Venerable Society can scarcely be blamed on the score of negligence or lack of interest. The recent advent of the

Hanoverian Succession in England explains much of the delay in sending the badly-needed missionaries. With the "15" fresh in mind, the Society, as a Crown corporation, had to be careful that the new missionaries they sent were loyal in their sentiments towards the new dynasty and that those already in the field were not in any way disaffected and imbued with smouldering Jacobite hostility to King George. 

The anxiety to maintain an attitude correct in every particular respecting the recently-established régime plainly appears in the Secretary's letter of May 14th, 1716, to Governour

Gookin, requesting his Excellency that he would inform the Society "if any of their Missionaries within your governments are disaffected to the government of his Majesty King George and if there be any such that your Excellency would be pleased to specify the matters of fact upon wch the charge of such disaffection is grounded, and cause a copy of such accusation to be delivered to the persons accused before it is transmitted hither, that if they have anything to offer in defence of themselves they may have a speedy opportunity of transmitting it to the Society."

Under the same date, the Secretary writes the Reverend Messrs. Ross, Clubb (news of whose death had not yet come to them), and Humphreys saying: "The Society have ordered me to acquaint you and the rest of your brethren the Clergy with their resolution, at your next meeting pursuant to the standing orders of the Society (see order 12 page 22d) you would after enquiry made, inform the Society if any of your brethren are disaffected to the government of his Majesty King George and if there be any such that you would specify the matters of fact upon which such charges of disaffection are grounded, and that before it is transmitted hither you would cause a copy of such accusation to be delivered to the persons accused that if they have anything to offer in defence of themselves, they may have a speedy opportunity of transmitting it to the Society, the Society have wrote to the several governours of the plantations to the same effect as you will observe by the inclosed which is a copy of that letter."

This issue of political loyalty amongst the clergy explains the allusion, in the letter of the Oxford Churchwardens and 

Vestry (of May 21st, 1716) to the Society, to "a certain minister" who "upon account of the oaths & other impositions is not like to continue in these parts"; it also explains the reluctance to name him. The same issue likewise explains Parson Ross's emphatic protestations of loyalty to the House of Hanover and his evasive attribution of 

John Talbot to "a distinct government." In 1716, after his return from a second visit to England, by the appointment of the Venerable Society Parson Evans took charge of Oxford and Radnor with a renewal of his former faithful services. But his pastorate was of short duration for, in the summer of 1718, worn and tired with long years of missionary service, he accepted a rectorship in Maryland where he remained until his death in 1721. 

In 1719 the Radnor Churchwardens and Vestry desire of the Society "your pious and charitable Consideration & that you would be pleased to send us a minister to Preach the Gospel in the British 

Language . . . .”

Later in this same year the Society appointed the Reverend Robert Weyman to the cure of Oxford and Radnor and he seems to have entered upon his duties shortly afterward. 

Parson Weyman was a faithful pastor, so far as he could be with the serious limitations under which he laboured. It was becoming increasingly evident, alike to the people of both parishes and to the incumbent, that the coupling of Oxford and Radnor together as a single missionary field, under the care of only one priest, was an impossible combination. It was utterly futile even to pretend that one missionary could do justice to both. Indeed, with modern motor facilities, it would present a situation equally impossible. It would call for one person to be actually in two distant places at the same time-not trying to be as simultaneously present as might be.

As early as September, 1720, in a memorial to the Vener-

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley able Society from the Radnor Churchwardens and Vestry, in which they "beg leave to express ye gratefull sence we have of your goodness in your pious and early care of the Church in these parts & particularly to return our humblest thanks for sending us a pastuer so assiduous in ye work of his Calling & so well qualifyed & inclined to Instruct us as ye Revd Mr. Robt Weyman," they go on to point out that "It is our misfortune to be surrounded with Sectaries who constantly hold their meetings every Lord's Day & your Honrs having been pleased to Comit ye care of another very distant Church to our Minister his Unconstant attendance on us gives a very little satisfaction to a great part of our Congregation & renders his labour of small effect to many others who for want of being better guarded are often led away by seducing & Corrupt Teachers. 

There is," [they continue], "another Congregation* within a few miles of us who are altogether destitute of a Minister & Oxford being a large & growing Congregation wch requires Constant Service, If in your great goodness you would condessend & settle ye Revd Mr.

Robt Weyman or any other Welsh Minister among us & ye People of Parkyeomant who are now building a Church he might with greater ease to himself & benefit to us serve these two. 

The following spring one of the Radnor Wardens wrote 

Sir John Philippus that 'Radnor Church was likely to be in a deplorable state as Mr. 

Weyman is likely to leave on account of it being too great a fatigue to supply that and Oxford 28 miles distant, and as he did not well understand Welsh, and not one-fourth of that congregation understanding English and many are in danger of sectaries who are very numerous.'

The several protests from Parson Weyman and his people seem to have had some effect upon the deliberations of the Society for, in April, 1723, the Missionary to Oxford and Radnor writes them anent his gratification that "The Honble Society are enclined . . . to make some provision for the people at Radnor who require a person of years and well conversant in the Welch tongue to reside amongst them and to visit them from house to house as well as to preach to them, for frequent conference with them in their own language is the most likely method that I can think of in order to recover them from their errors and to bring them to a conformity to our holy mother the Church to which many of them are inclined but cannot be brought over by mere 

Itinerant preaching."

Quakers at one time, in 1709. The original church was built in 1721.
