A PAGE FROM the Old VeSTRY BOOK; THE 1745 REGULATIONS TOUCHING BURIALS WITHIN THE CHURCH AND IN THE CHURCHYARD

general and perennial need for the successful conduct of parish work anywhere and always-parochial house to house visiting on the part of the clergy and individual pastoral care-a thing not a few of our clergy today would do well to ponder and act upon. 

Notwithstanding the favourable attitude of the Society indicated in Parson Weyman's letter just quoted, there was evidently an adverse decision afterwards towards any present change in the missionary assignment. In July, 1725, acknowledging receipt of the Society's letter of August, 1724, the Missionary to Oxford and Radnor voices his disappointment that "the Society are not yet come to resolution of sending a missionary to Radnor where the people have great need of one to be always residing amongst them that can converse with them in their own British dialect. . . . they desire he may be well acquainted and obliged to speak to them in their own language beuause there are a considerable number amongst them that are strangers to the English."

The Radnor Churchwardens and Vestry had already (April, 1725) sent the Venerable Society an address that "Humbly Sheweth

That we are deeply Sensible of ye great favours and Invaluable Blessings that we have from Time to time & for the space of many years enjoyed by your Appointment of the Reverend Mr. Robert 

Weyman to exercise ye Duties of the Sacred function amongst us in Conjunction with the Care and Oversight of other Churches. We beg leave to acknowledge ye great Piety & goodness to us therein with the utmost Thankfulness & to desire his Settlement wholly and Solely & constantly amongst us in the Relation of a Pastor to the end we may have the Comfort & satisfaction of being led in the Way of Salvation of having the means of grace dispensed to us in our own Language wch is more Edifying to us than any other Can be And by him whose Doctrine Life Conversation & temper, we are all pleased with & must do him so much Justice as to Declare to ye Honble Body that he had already been a Painfull Labourer in God's Vineyard amongst us & to the great increase of our Community amongst a 

Crowd of Gentiles Heathens Infidels & Sectaries of all sorts who will in all likelihood be brought Over in Time to embrace the Christian faith & to an Entire conformity to our Church & that nothing may be wanting on our Parts to Encourage so good a Design we have resolved on building another Fabrick in Stone for Divine worship & for their Accommodation about Eight Miles in Distance from this And earnestly pray that your Body may Indulge us in our

Request above, or at least take Care that we shall be provided with a Minister that shall Officiate amongst us in our own British Language for the Reason afore said."

And they back up their insistence on having a resident parson of their own, free from the care of distant Oxford, and

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley one that can speak and preach in their own tongue, by their corporate assurance to the Honourable Body that "we shall Contribute to his Maintenance and better Support among us as largely and freely as Our Circumstances will admit off." The resolve on "building another Fabrick in Stone for Divine worship about Eight Miles in Distance from this," can refer to nothing but an intended new structure to replace the log church at the upper end of the parish, Montgomery. Although St. Peter's, as we know, was not built until 1744, there is nothing at all unlikely in the plan being mooted at this time to erect a building more worthy than the log church. Furthermore, the same address to the 

Venerable Society that announces the "resolve on building another Fabrick in Stone" is signed not only by the Radnor 

Churchwardens and "Vestry Men" but also by four "Trustees for the building of the New Church"-Thomas 

James, James Anderson, William Evans and James David.

All four of them lived in the "Montgomery" neighbourhood at the "upper end of the parish." When St. Peter's had its first separate Vestry in 1745, James David was a member; 

Thomas James became a member in 1747; William Evans some years later; and James Anderson was in time represented by his son, Captain Patrick Anderson. 

When Parson Weyman went to England in the summer of 1728, he addressed a memorial or report to the Venerable Society. In the course of enumerating the places to which his missionary labours had extended he says "... and to the great Valley which is 30 miles [from Oxford or 

Philadelphia] where they have laid the Foundation of a Church just before I left the Country and is about 8 miles distant from Radnor." 

From this it is quite evident that the four "Trustees for the building of the New Church" had made a beginning and had a visible result to show for their commission; the foundations had already been laid for the "New Church," "a Fabrick in Stone for Divine worship," when Parson Weyman sailed for England in the late spring or early summer of 1728.

It is entirely plausible and highly likely that the founda-

tions laid between 1725 and 1728 are the very foundations on which the present structure of St. Peter's-in-the-Great 

Valley rests. What interrupted the work thus begun, and delayed the completion of the building until 1744 we do not know. While eighteenth-century methods of building were sometimes very leisurely, sixteen to nineteen years is a long time and what kept the congregation worshipping in the log church all that while was presumably something more than any mere disposition to be dilatory on the part of the builders or "Trustees." It was most likely lack of funds. 

And this unexpected lack of funds could easily be accounted for by the "remarkable exodus," to which Mr. Pleasants directs attention in his 

History of Old St. David's, "occurring during Mr. Weyman's term, of the Welsh settlers of Radnor and adjacent territory into what is now Lancaster County. It bears on this history in that it deprived 

Radnor Church of many of its most valuable supporters, and because Mr. Weyman and his successor seem to have endeavoured to retain some supervision over the new territory."

In spite of this long-continued financial discouragement, it speaks well for the faithful tenacity of the congregation in the "upper part of the parish" that they would not be swerved from their purpose and finally accomplished its fulfillment. There is a further confirmatory allusion to this early plan to build a stone church. In a letter to the Secretary of the Society, in June, 1730, Rowland Jones, a schoolmaster (of whom Parson Weyman appears not to have had any great opinion), mentions "Whiteland where they are designed to build a church. The ground is marked out for the length and breadth likewise a church yard where a great many church people has been buried already and well walled in with stones." 

In October, 1730, St. Mary's, Burlington, fell vacant by the death of the Reverend Nathaniel Harwood and Parson Weyman applied to the Society to be transferred to that charge. At the same time, he was careful to explain that "I would not in any wise leave my Flock at Oxon and Radnor, had it been possible for me considering distance of the situation of the two Towns to serve each with Advantage to them and with conveniency to myself." *St. Peter's stands at the border line between Whiteland and Tredyffrin Townships so that it is mentioned sometimes as in one, sometimes as in the other.

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley and again strongly urged that Radnor be separated from Oxford and coupled with Perkiomen, while Whitemarsh and Oxford be joined as one missionary field. 

For nearly two years after Mr. Weyman's removal to 

Burlington, the people of Montgomery and Radnor were without any regular pastor. During this time the Reverend 

Richard Backhouse, then stationed at Chester, gave them such attention as he could. Then, probably in the winter of 

1732-33, the Reverend Griffith Hughes came as the Society's missionary for Montgomery and Radnor, with St. 

James's, Perkiomen. Mr. Weyman's urgent and repeated recommendations for the separation of Radnor from Oxford had at last been heeded.

The coming of Parson Hughes received a cordial welcome from the people of Montgomery and Radnor and the Churchwardens and Vestry of Radnor Church addressed a communication to the Society expressing "our sincere thanks for their favour & great goodness in sending the Revd M. Hughes to be our Missionary who... so well officiates in ye welch tongue . . ."

It is worthy of note that the "Honble Society's most obedient & most obliged Humble Servants" sign their names to this document in two columns. One contains eight names, evidently those of the two Churchwardens and six Vestrymen of St. David's Church. The other, separately bracketed, has the names of seven men, four of whom-Thomas James, William Evans, Joseph Jones and James David-afterwards appear in the Vestry list of St. Peter's. From this arrangement of signatures in two columns, each bracketed by itself, it seems not unreasonable to conclude that the seven whose names form the second column represented the "upper part of the parish" at Montgomery.

As we gather from Parson Hughes's letters to the Society, he was a young gentleman of diverse attainments, much energy, assiduity in visiting, more than a touch of egotism perhaps, and a great capacity for getting over ground. In one of his early reports he says, "My last journey to them completed in all 1105 miles, besides my weekly attendance at Radnor and Perquihoma [Perkiomen], at either of which churches I preach and catechise every Sunday . . ."”

Part of the occasion of his 1105 miles' travelling was his preaching and visiting at Bangor Church, at what is now Churchtown, in Lancaster County, and at Tulpehocken. 

With all his evident missionary zeal, he was not blessed with stability nor an adequate sense of obligation to his duties, and he was solicitous about his health or else made his health an excuse for following his own butterfly inclinations. The next year after reporting his 1105 miles of missionary journeys, he writes the Society suggesting the propriety of his return to England to secure a proper supply of Welsh books for his flock, and then adds "Ye present would be the most proper opportunity if my absence would be Dispensed with for lately on my way to Perquihoma Church I had the misfortune to break my knee pan which continues, tho' upon the mending hand, very weak, so that it is Impossible for one in my present Condition to Serve that Church in a Regular order this Winter. That and severall other hardships which I have with pleasure almost endured in my severall Journeys to preach among the Back Inhabitants hath very much Impaired my health being often obliged in the day to want the Common necessaries of Life and in the night to be contented with the a shade of a large tree for a Lodging. As for my congregation at Radnor it is in a very flourishing condition. And as to my own conduct in General I appeal to my reverend Commissary who is a gentleman equally beloved and admired for his behaviour, Piety and good conduct." 

The sequel to this epistle was that the gentleman took French leave of his flock and was up and off to Barbadoes before the Society had a chance to tell him not to go away from his post. Arrived back in Pennsylvania again from 

Barbadoes, he writes them another letter to say that no doubt they have been as "informed by the hands of Rev. Mr. Commissary Johnson of my being at Barbadoes, the Chief Reason of my going there was unexpected as unavoidable and nothing but an absolute necessity would have obliged me to quit my mission without the Previous consent of the Society. At the Earnest Request of a very considerable number of both Welch and English that live near Tolpahocken, 70 miles from town, I officiated there & at Canistogo, at Sundry times . . . But the fatigue of returning home by Saturday night to take care of the Churches more particularly under my care and the great Difficulty of travelling into so Remote a part of the country with severall Inconveniences too tedious to be mentioned, threw me into a very Ill state of health. My Physicians were of opinion that a Sudden Change of air would be absolutely necessary for me, especially to one of the Leward Islands, and if I found no benefit there to return to England, wch I resolved upon rather than 

Run the hazard of my life by staying

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley 

I had no sooner arrived at Barbadoes but I found great Benefit by the Change of air 

Soon after the Rectory of St. Lucy's

Parish became Vacant, wich by the earnest Request of the Rev. Mr. 

Commr Johnson I was prevailed upon to accept of . . . . I enjoyed it for near three months and then obtained a Lycence from the Honourable James Dotton, Esq., our Commander in Chief, to_return to Pennsylvania for 4 months, where I now officiate at Radnor & Perquihoma at the expiration of which time I design with the Honble. Society to return to Barbadoes and then beg leave to Resign my Mission, praying that the Honble Society will be pleased to 

Supply it with another as soon as possible." ! ! !

He went. Doubtless the Venerable Society were glad to have him go. Certainly some of his parishioners were. It may, perhaps, be consoling to some readers to know that the valetudinarian parson of our own day, who runs away from his parish for an "imperatively necessary rest" whenever he can trump up so much as a toe-ache by way of excuse, had his prototype in the eighteenth century. Unfortunately, nowadays he isn't always so obliging as to provide himself with another parish during his truancy. Parson 

Hughes was the last incumbent to preach to the people of the Barony in Welsh.

So far as the records show, the people of Montgomery and Radnor, whatever their feelings may have been, kept a dead silence over this episode. Not so the congregation at 

St. James's, Perkiomen. A communication to the Venerable 

Society at this time reads. 

"The Humble Petition of Sundry Members of the Congregation belonging to St. James's Church near Perquihoma in The Province of Pennsylvania in behalf of themselves and the whole Congregation. Sheweth 

That your Honourable Society . were pleased to send over a Very Young Welch Gentleman to serve Radnor and this our Church of St. James to whom we regularly paid our Subscription money amtg to about £ 24 or 25. p. annum altho. the said Mr. 

Hughes very seldom came near us and some time in September last (as by Mr. Cummings's letter to the Society may be seen) he thought fit without giving us the least Notice to make a Voyage to the Island of Barbadoes where he has accepted of a Parish and we are now destitute of a Minister and without Your Honourable Society will be pleased to Continue your care to us this congregation will in a very little time dwindle to naught, we being surrounded with Dissenters of every sort. We beg Leave further to acquaint your 

Honours that unless the Gentleman you send has much more solidity and conduct than Mr. Hughes our Religion will be brought into 

Contempt. We shall not trouble your Honours with a Detail of his misbehaviours not doubting but you'll receive that from some able hand; . 

​B

Amongst the thirty-seven signatures to this protest is that of the Honourable William Moore, that staunch old Churchman and Judge, of whom there will be further note in these pages. 

After Parson Hughes left the scene, the congregation at Montgomery and Radnor had no settled pastor until the Reverend William Currie came in 1737. It is a curious coincidence that the minutes of the Venerable Society show that Parson Hughes's letter about his irresponsible holiday in Barbadoes and William Currie's petition for ordination and employment as one of the Society's missionaries came to their attention at the same time.

CHAPTER III  

N the story of our old Colonial churches, it happens time and again that one parson's personality and character so intimately affect the pattern of parish growth that during his incumbency parish history seems mainly a chapter in the clergyman's personal biography. This occurs especially when there has been an unusually long and devoted pastorate, such as Parson Currie's. His ministry to St. Peter's people began in 1737 and lasted actively until the outbreak of the Revolutionary War; after that, almost until the appointment of his successor in 1788, his pastoral care and concern for the welfare of his parishioners had an appreciable influence, to say the least, on the corporate life of St. Peter's congregation. 

At the age of about twenty-eight, his personality then set on enduring lines, William Currie came to a charge that had already passed through the sundry vicissitudes of early growth-the vigorous and devoted missionary labours of Parsons Evans, Clubb and Weyman, followed by the disappointments and irritations caused by the inconsequent goings on of that energetic but flighty "very young Welch

Gentleman" who provoked the candid and unflattering expression of opinion from the long-suffering and irate men of 

St. James's, Perkiomen. Through the years that followed his coming, Parson Currie and his threefold flock together ripened in fully organised parochial relationships and experience.

William Currie was born in Glasgow about 1708. Educated at the University of his native city, his conduct and

sound scholarship won the high opinion of the Faculty and when a distinguished and wealthy Virginia planter asked them to recommend a tutor for his son, they named Currie as a well-qualified and suitable person for the post. After he had spent several years in Virginia and finished his tutorial duties there, Currie went to New Castle in Delaware. Still a member of the Kirk of Scotland, the Presbytery of New Castle licensed him to preach.

At this time the young Scottish licentiate became intimate with the family of the Reverend George Ross, the Missionary of the Society for the Propagation of the Gospel at New Castle. Currie's mind may already have turned in that direction but, at any rate, under the influence of the Rosses, he determined to seek Holy Orders in the Church of England. In the meanwhile he had fallen in love with Parson 

Ross's daughter Margaret, the widow of the Reverend Uri Walter Hackett who, at the time of his death, in 1734, had been the Society's Missionary at St. Ann's Church, Appoquiminy. It is said, on the authority of a statement attributed to a granddaughter, that the Widow Hackett insisted that Currie become a Priest of the Church as a condition to giving him her hand in marriage.

In 1736, when he applied to the Lord Bishop of London for ordination and addressed a petition to the Venerable Society to appoint him one of their missionaries, it is noteworthy that both the Presbytery of New Castle* and the "A Certificate in Behalf of Mr. Curry 

That Mr. Wm. Curry Prob. applying to the Psby of Newcastle in Pensilvania in America in September 1734 And bringing Authentick Credentials, was taken on Tryal for the work of the Ministry, and going through all the parts usuall of Tryal, was by said Psby Lycenced to preach the Gospell of our Lord Jesus Christ, he acquitting himself, in all his Tryals to the very great Satisfaction, of said Psby-and has been for the most part, ever since under the Care of the said Psby. and Subject to them in the Lord, by Carefully observing their appointments, by Preaching in the Vacancys, within the Bounds thereof to the Edification and great Satisfaction of all his Auditors; And now Designing to Leave these parts; And applying to us for his Testimonials.

We do Hereby unanimously Recommend the above named Gentleman to the Divine protection, & Due Regards of any Psby or Christian Society, where Providence may order his Lot, as a Sober Christian, & well Deserving Gentleman, And During his Residence within our Bounds, his Behaviour was very Becoming & agreeable to the Character of a Sound & Orthodox preacher of the Gospell & now is free of any Scandall or Church Censure known to us is by Order & appointment of the above sd Psby Certifyed & Subscribed.

May 15th 1736 By 

Joseph Houston, P. Clk."

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley clergy of the Church of England in Pennsylvania* gave him testimonials of good character, learning, and fitness for the Christian ministry. His prospective father-in-law, who had five days before signed the certificate to the Bishop of London, along with the other clergy of the Province, gave young Currie also a letter to the Secretary of the Society saying, "... The bearer, Mr. William Curry, lived in this Town, as a preacher to a Meeting, above a twelve Month past, in high esteem for his virtue and learning. How far I was instrumental to gain him to our side, I shall not presume to say. You'll find, by his ample

Credentials, how worthy he is for your regard, and how fit for the service of the Society. The Church of Dover in this Government, is now vacant, by good Mr. Frazer's returning to England. I wish Mr. 

Curry may be named his successor. Your assistance in forwarding his laudable design will be an indelible obligation upon, Reverend Sir, Your most humble Servant, Geo. Ross"

Armed with his Church of England and Presbyterian credentials and certificates, Currie went to London, applied to the Bishop for ordination and laid before the Venerable 

Society his petition which ". . . . Sheweth 

That the sd Wm. Currie after Passing some time in Virginia as

Preceptor to a Young Gentleman was admitted a Preacher among those of the Presbyterian Party in Pensilvania in which Station he 

Continued for above a Year last past. That he is Lately Come over from thence to apply to the Lord Bishop of London, for Episcopal Ordination and hath brought with him Testimonials of his good Life and Conversation, which he is ready to produce to this Venerable Board. 

And humbly prays they will be pleased to Receive him into their

Mission. 

William Currie" "Clergy in Pensilvania in Behalf of Mr. Currie

To the Right Reverend Edmond Lord Bishop of London 

Whereas Mr. William Curry of the University of Glasgow, after passing some years in Virginia, as Preceptor to a Young Gentleman was admitted preacher among those of the Presbyterian way in the Terroteres of Pensilvania in which Station he has Continued for a year past, and now having signified to us, Subscribers his just Veneration, for the Doctrine and Government of the Church of England and his Desire of Receiving Episcopall orders. And of Serving the Church in Virginia if the Society de propaganda have no 

Employ for him.

This is to Certify that he hath behaved himself while he resided in these parts, without Scandall or reproach; in good Esteem for his Sobriety, learning and circumspection, among all that Knew him; And how fair his Character was when he came from Virgínia, his 

Licence to preach among the Dissenters, is allowed here as a Sufficient proof. 

Given under our hands at Philadelphia the Tenth day of June Anno Dom. 1736" 

This was signed by the Revs. Archibald Cummings, Commissary; George Ross, Missionary at New Castle; Robert Weyman, Missionary at Burlington; William Lindsay,

Missionary Itinerant in Pennsylvania and New Jersey; and Alexander Howie, Missionary at Oxford and Whitemarsh.

Satisfied with his testimonials, the Bishop of London ordered Currie deacon and priested him soon afterwards; the Venerable Society acted favourably upon his petition and appointed him Missionary to Radnor and Perkiomen. He then returned to America to take up the charge to which he faithfully devoted the rest of his life. A little more than a year after entering upon his new duties, he and the Widow 

Hackett were married (1739).

But, however much Parson Currie, or any other parson, may seem to be the persona, the "person"-the embodiment, so to speak, of a parish's composite personality and its mouthpiece-he cannot stand alone and dissociated from all else in his pastoral capacity. Remember the old nursery rime and its accompanying finger-play, "Here's the church, and here's the steeple; 

Here's the priest, and all the people."

And old nursery rimes have a terse way of putting large truths in few words. For the priest to be a pastor or "person," there must be also the people of the parish of which he is the "person." And there must be likewise the material elements of the physical setting as necessary concomitants. Taken all together they make up the indispensable parts of a single whole, features inseparable from a complete picture. 

So far as we know, Parson Currie could not read the service nor preach in Welsh and, since he makes no particular mention of the Welsh in his cure, or of any insistence on Welsh services, it seems reasonable to assume that by the time of his arrival amongst them most of his parishioners could understand English. But that the congregations at both Montgomery and Radnor were Welsh, and remained predominantly so for a long time afterwards, there can be no doubt. One look at the lists of Vestrymen and pewholders is enough to prove it—almost without exception 

Griffiths, Owens, Evanses, Joneses, Thomases, Williamses, Davieses, Davids, Pughs, Reeses, Howells, Powels and the like.

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley 

The condition of these people is sometimes misunderstood and several passages-one in a letter from the Radnor Churchwardens to the Society, in 1717, and another in Parson Weyman's report to the Society in 1728—are misleading if taken by themselves without reference to the circumstances under which they were written. The Radnor 

Churchwardens' memorial says "we are generaly poor setlers that have newly setled Lands back in the Wilderness and have not yet as much as our own Habitations free from Debts-and in a second place we have built a Church (in hopes of being supplyed with the right worship of God) and have put ourselves in so much debts that in all Probability (according to the times) we shall be several years before we clear ourselves. 

And thirdly the Constitution of our Government is such that we have no Law to uphold any supply for our Teachers, and all other persevations or dissenters being very numerous Amongst us And none of them being under any Obligation to their Teachers but only free gifts. Therefore if we should atempt to bring our Congregation under a Certain Salary it would be a means to scatter Our Congregation..."

This was in reply to a suggestion from the Venerable Society that the people should pledge themselves to an appreciable annual sum for the maintenance of a priest. 

The Venerable Society were ignorant of the intimate details of Colonial life and economy; seemingly they did not in the least understand that in a newly-settled country, while a certain kind of prosperity might be apparent, and indeed very real, actual money to pledge towards a parson's stipend or contribute for church-building was as scarce as the proverbial "hens' teeth." Unlike Maryland and Virginia, which had their "pounds of tobacco," Pennsylvania and the Three 

Lower Counties had no readily convertible exchange of staple commodities as a substitute for currency. The dearth of coinage in circulation frequently caused both the Provincial authorities and individuals serious inconvenience in early days. This same dearth prompted that enterprising and irrepressible Quaker, Charles Pickering (whose lands lay not far distant from Radnor parish) to engage in the somewhat questionable and highly risky adventure of supplying the deficiency by "Quining of Spanish Bitts and Boston money"-for which "Heynous and Grevious Crime" of counterfeiting he paid a fine of "fourty pounds . . . toward

4I ye Building of a Court house," but did not lose his standing in Friends' Meeting nor his eligibility for public office. (A few years later he sat in the Provincial Assembly.)* The 

Venerable Society also seemed oblivious to another fact; "healthy" debts, incident to colonisation, had necessarily been incurred and had to be discharged. Apparently, too, they failed to grasp the difference in economic status between a province of comparatively recent establishment, like 

Pennsylvania, and those of longer standing like Virginia,

Maryland or Massachusetts. All of this had to be explained to them, along with a timely reminder that in Pennsylvania, where there were no taxes for clerical support, as there were in Maryland and Virginia, the Church people would probably balk at a fixed imposition while their Quaker neighbours, without the responsibility of any "hireling ministry,' got off scot free. Hence the Churchwardens' letter with its emphasis on "poverty."

In Parson Weyman's report, he says of his congregations in the Welsh Barony, "The number of inhabitants originally belonging to and frequenting these Churches at first was small, as was their general Condition low in the World. Their employment was chiefly Agriculture and handy Craft Employments 

& their manner of living is to this day Plowing the ground and following the business of the farms, Plantations and trades."

So has it ever been, with few exceptions, in newly-planted British colonies where the land must be tamed and "labour" was scarce. "Quality" must needs put its hand to the plough and owners of land, no matter what their "walk in life," dared not disdain manual toil. Jamestown, in its infancy, furnished a notable exception to this common-sense course, and it cost Captain John Smith many woes and worries, and the bitter experience of following years, to teach the lesson of sound pioneering technique. And because this lesson was at last fully learned, even to-day in old-established British Colonial possessions highly respected "quality," of unassailable social status, may be found engaged in "trade" or commerce. Parson Weyman's early familiarity with well- 

*Portrait of a Colonial City: H. D. Eberlein and C. V. D. Hubbard, v. pp. 14 and 15.

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley stratified rural conditions in England-large land-holdings, working tenant-farmers and numerous farm labourerswhich also the recipients of his communication well knew, probably led him to assume a deprecatory tone in describing the "low Condition" of his Welsh parishioners and their dependence on "Plowing the ground and following the business of the farms, Plantations and trades." It would help to explain their inability to give substantial monetary support. He might have added something about characteristic 

Quaker insistence on having every boy taught some manual trade (whether he was intended afterwards to ply it or not), and that this practice so generally observed by their Quaker neighbours had doubtless had some effect on the Church of England Welsh. One suspects, too, that the worthy parson's tone betrays a tinge of the snobbery that came into England along with the Hanoverian Succession. 

As a matter of fact, far from being poverty-stricken, or anything like poverty-stricken—as one might, perhaps, infer from the two quotations just made-the people of the Montgomery and Radnor congregations were generally speaking in circumstances as comfortable as the conditions of relatively recent settlement would admit, circumstances that would compare more than favourably with those in most other Colonies after a like period of settlement. One could not ask more convincing evidence of their general well-being than the houses they built for themselves in the late seventeenth and early eighteenth centuries. Many of these are still standing to yield their testimony from all the countryside round about. Poverty-stricken colonists, hard bested to wring bare subsistence from a savage wilderness, did not build the substantial stone hendres or farmsteads, virtually the counterparts of the homes they had left in Wales. 

By examining an empty sea-shell, science can accurately describe the original occupant; houses afford an equally reliable index to the manner and lives of those who built them. The "shells" fashioned and dwelt in by these early 

American Welshmen show them a sturdy and prosperous race, quite able to fend for themselves and needing no pity from either contemporaries or posterity because of their

narrow means. A good many of them, indeed, enjoyed not a few of the elegancies and luxuries of life to which they had been used in their old homes. The Welsh of the Barony -whether Quakers or Church of England folk-were not hinds when they migrated from their native mountains. On the contrary, some of them were persons of consequence. 

Although they unavoidably, and voluntarily, gave up certain of the amenities incident to an old civilisation when they set out to establish themselves overseas, they did not live like hinds in the new land, whose wooded hills recalled their beloved Cambria and whose place-names, given they may have been at first from nostalgic affection, nevertheless often indicated more than merely nominal resemblance to namesakes. 

They were tenacious of their cultural heritage and their household gear frequently displayed considerable elegance. "Their tables, abundantly plenished with good food and drinkthey were by no means total abstainers-were well appointed with the best of linen and all other appropriate accessories." It is told of one of the early settlers in the Barony that "the old gentleman had fine linen napkins for each day in the week, and two linen tablecloths for each week."*

In the matter of raiment, old account books attest Welsh love of gay clothing and show that "the colonists in the Barony bought from the Philadelphia merchants the wherewithal to keep pace with the current London fashions. 

The finest beaver hats, silver-mounted riding whips, silk gowns of sundry hues, riding coats with silver buttons, and embroidered waistcoats, along with many other fashionable etceteras of both masculine and feminine apparel made a gathering of" even "Welsh Friends anything but the drab affair so often mentally pictured when 

Quakers en masse are mentioned."*

Church of England people laboured under no deterrent principles or prejudices respecting lively attire, and it is pleasant to think of the Montgomery and Radnor congregations adding a spice of colour to the surroundings as they arrived at church. That the women were duly careful of their finery we might be certain, even without the testimony of local tradition which tells how St. Peter's matrons and maids, who came on pillions (as most of them did), wore 

*The Portrait of a Colonial City: H. D. Eberlein and C. V. D. Hubbard, pp. 62 and 

63; v. also Merion in the Welsh Tract: Thomas Allen Glenn.

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley splash-skirts over their clothing; these they took off as they dismounted and hung over the churchyard wall before they went into church. 

Merely material comforts and elegancies, however, did not monopolise the attention of the Welsh colonists. "Books were to them a necessary part of the machinery of living, and though their libraries may have been small, they were wellchosen. The books were likewise indicative of their owners' intellectual status; the Welsh colonists were well educated. Their handwriting shows a far higher cultural average than in most other parts of the Province during Penn's lifetime."*

Parson Evans's mention of finding devotional or religious books, in Welsh, in the possession of some of his parishioners has already been recorded. Furthermore, "the Welsh have always been noted for their love of music and their excellence in singing. Music and early Quakerism were altogether at variance, but it was hard to repress the Welshman's native impulse to burst forth into his old ballads." It is said that "more than one Welsh Friend was privately admonished that his tuneful inclinations must cease forthwith." "It is pleasant, though," adds

Thomas Allen Glenn, in writing of the Merion neighbourhood, "whilst looking backward to the first settlement, to think that often through the wild woodland . . . there has echoed the burthen of some ancient British war song, chanted ages ago in battle against the legions of Imperial Rome."+

The Church of England Welsh, free of any disciplinary inhibitions, could sing to their hearts' content, and doubtless did. Of the early music at St. Peter's we unfortunately have no record. We know that it was a long time before an organ was installed in the church, and it is not until quite late in the history of the parish that there is even mention of a 'cello or "chapel bass." Whether they had any accompanying instruments or not, St. Peter's congregation could undoubtedly make the welkin ring, as anyone knows full weil who has ever heard a Welsh congregation's spontaneous outburst in some favourite hymn. Knowing the racial makeup of the parish, one is perfectly willing to take the hearty tunefulness of St. Peter's music on faith without documentary proof.

MAP OF TREDYFFRIN, SHOWING ST. PETER'S; DRAWN BY THE ENGINEERS ATTACHED TO GENERAL GREY'S ARMY

Besides the purely Welsh members of the Montgomery and Radnor congregations, there were a few English families from the early years of the eighteenth century, such as the Waynes of Waynesborough and the Moores of Moore

Hall. The Waynes, although they came immediately from 

Ireland, were really a Yorkshire family. Judge Moore, who married the Lady Williamina Wemyss, was the son of the 

Honourable John Moore and the Lady Rebecca Axtell, who had come to Philadelphia from England by way of South Carolina. Of both families we shall hear further in the history of the parish.

There was also at least one Scot in the congregation- 

James Anderson, from the Isle of Skye. His descendants say he ran away from home because his father threatened to thrash him for poaching. On his arrival in America, about 1707, as a seventeen-year old redemptioner, Thomas 

Jerman (or Jarman), the prosperous Quaker miller who had the Great Valley Mill, bought his indentured time and brought him to the Great Valley. Before his term of service had expired, Anderson had won the heart of Jerman's daughter Elizabeth. Father Jerman frowned on the match, so the young couple eloped as soon as Anderson had served his time. Jerman, constrained to make the best of the fait accompli, finally relented, forgave them and helped his sonin-law buy 340 acres on Pickering Creek. Thither, in 1713,

Anderson took his bride. At first they lived in a cave; later, Anderson built a log cabin where he lived until his death. 

Amongst other activities, he is said to have made moccasins for his friendly Indian neighbours. As already mentioned (v. p. 30), James Anderson was one of the four "Trustees for the building of the New Church" who, in April 1725, signed the address of the Rector, Churchwardens and vestry of St. David's, Radnor, to the Venerable Society for the 

Propagation of the Gospel. 

James Anderson's eldest son Patrick, afterwards a Vestryman of St. Peter's, bought the farm from his father about 

1746. When Patrick was a Captain of Militia in the Revolutionary War, he borrowed heavily to pay for the equipment of his company. After the war, the Continental

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley 

Congress failed to reimburse him and, in order to discharge his debt, he deeded a considerable part of his farm to the creditor who had advanced the money for military outfit. 

Throughout the eighteenth century the Andersons, father and son-two of the picturesque characters of St. Peter'swere active in church affairs. "Montgomery" lay at the corner of Tredyffrin Township (Tredyffrin meaning "Great Valley," a name appropriately bestowed). When Parson Currie came to take charge of his flock, he found at the "upper end of his parish" the log church, and the stone foundations for the stone church that was to be built seven years later, within the churchyard at the crest of a rise in the middle of the Great Valley. To the north was the lofty barrier ridge shutting off the Valley of the Schuylkill, its steep hills cleft where the Valley Stream broke through at Valley Forge on its way to the river. 

Three miles to the south of St. Peter's another high wooded ridge hemmed in this well-favoured land. From the elevated vantage point of the churchyard, one could see east and west for twenty miles in each direction to elevated rolling country at the horizon. This "Great Valley," where they had planted themselves, may well have reminded the Welsh colonists of many a scene in their beloved homeland; the natural conditions are strikingly like the country in the Welsh Border counties. There was the same tender, half-mysterious beauty that defies close analysis. To Parson Currie it doubtless recalled to some extent the surroundings of his native Glasgow; it was utterly different from the countryside he had known either in Virginia or New Castle. 

Apart from Parson Currie's semi-annual reports to the Society (required of all the S.P.G. missionaries), his communications to the reverend Secretary in London are not nearly so numerous nor full as those sent by sundry other of his fellow-missionaries in the American Colonial field. 

Nevertheless, we can gather a good deal from them, both of Parson Currie's own life and character and also of the parochial conditions at St. Peter's, St. David's and St. 

James's, Perkiomen, during the years of his incumbency.

With the aid of various other sources, we can fill in a fairly complete picture. 

At the beginning of his ministry to "Montgomery,"

Radnor and Perkiomen, the Notitia Parochialis-this was the title of the semi-annual reports to the Society, which oftentimes didn't get sent-shows that there were about 2000 souls in that part of the countryside committed to his pastoral care. Of these, about 1500 were "Dissenters of all sorts," 450 were of the Church of England (at least more or less nominally so), while some 66 of them were communicants. These figures in the Notitia Parochialis of succeeding years remained more or less constant and one cannot escape the conclusion that the good parson would not have qualified as capable statistician; even a most casual census of the district would have revealed an appreciable increase in population between 1737 and the out break of the Revolutionary War. 

Sometimes, amongst the approximate 1500 "Dissenters of all sorts" in his cure the reverend missionary numbers "Heathens 50 negroes," "Heathens & infidels," "Heathen

Blacks" and the like. Counting in the "heathen," the figures do not by any means always tally with the static 1500 “Dissenters of all sorts"; in fact, these apparently transient or migratory "heathen" make up a quantity markedly variable from year to year. The 50 "heathen" of the 1761

Michaelmas report had increased to 68 just three years later. This enlargement of heathendom seems to have perturbed the Venerable Society; an item in their Minutes reads, "Agreed that the Secretary be directed to get Information concerning the Subject of this Letter from some Neighbouring Clergymen." 

At Michaelmas, 1769, the "Heathen Blacks" had reached the number of 70, but by Lady Day, 1770, their ranks had dropped to 60. By what means the parson compassed this reduction in the "powers of darkness"—whether by poison, massacre, or evangelical persuasion-the records do not disclose. Perhaps a newspaper advertisement of a few

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley years before may give the clue to an occasional source of "heathen" diminution: "Run away from William Moore of Moore Hall, in Chester County, a likely young Negro man, named Jack; speaks but indifferent 

English, and had on when he went away a new Ozenburg Shirt, a pair of striped homespun Breeches, a striped ticking Wastecoat, an old Dimity Coat of his master's, with buttons of Horseteeth set in 

Brass and Cloth sleeves, a Felt Hat, almost new. Whoever secures the said Negro and will bring him to his Master or to John Moore, Esq., in Philadelphia, shall receive Twenty Shillings Reward and reasonable charges. 

William Moore." 

Notwithstanding the abiding presence of "Heathen 

Blacks," "infidels" and "Dissenters," Parson Currie's work was not barren of results. Not only did he preach and teach the Gospel to his own immediate threefold flock, but also to other Church folk for whom the Venerable Society had not so far been able to provide any regular pastoral care. His reports note the conversions and baptisms outside the limits of his own appointed mission field, which show that his labours in this direction also bore good fruit. In the Society's Journals a synopsis of his letter of May, 1739 notes that 'he sometimes preaches at a place called Canastogoe about 25 miles from the place of his abode and there and in his way thither, he hath at times Baptized above 40 Infants and some adults.'

In October, 1741, a petition to the Lord Bishop of London from the "Inhabitants of Towyn," a place apparently identifiable as in Warwick Township, testifies to the spread of Parson Currie's extra-parochial activity. The petition says: "Our Condition like many Others in this Remote Part of the World has been, That we for Several Years have been almost like Sheep 

Without a Shepherd, Except the Revd Mr. Curry of the Parish of 

Radnor and Perkiomy some few Times hath been Pleas'd to Visit us.

But he having enough to do with his own Flock, cannot Assist us as often as our need doth require. 

The petition continues that 

"many of us not Understanding English cannot make that use of his [Parson Currie's] Preaching As, if we, being Welch Men heard one in our own Native Tongue, Which cannot but be a great Grief to 

Us, Besides an Unspeakable Damage and Loss to our Spiritual Im-

provement. So that many of Weak Judgement fall away from the 

Truth, Some to Dissenters, Some to nothing. 

We, being Inhabitants of the Parish of Towyn,* have joyned with one of our Neighbouring Parishes newly Setled at the French Creek, to have wth Your Lordship's Consent and Applause one Minister who can Preach English with Welch setled among us. 

For his Yearly Maintenance We have made up a Sum of Thirty 

Pounds Pensilvania Currency 

There follows a list of subscribers to the stipend of the hoped-for Welsh Missionary for the "Parish of Towyn" and the "Parish at French Creek." In the list from the "Parish of Towyn" occur the names of Thomas James, John Evans, Morris Griffith, James Anderson, Patrick Anderson, Rees Prichard, David Howell, John Hunter, Richard Richardson [Richison] and others-fifteen in all out of twenty-four—who afterwards served on the Vestry of St. 

Peter's-in-the-Great Valley or were closely identified with the parish. Since the "Parish of Towyn" did not materialise,, it is apparent that these "Welsh Men"-who made this last recorded appeal for a Welsh clergyman of their own then threw in their lot with the log church at the "upper end" of Radnor Parish and brought enough strength to that part of Parson Currie's congregation to complete a few years afterwards the stone church whose foundations, according to Parson Weyman, had already been laid in 1728. 

About two years after Parson Currie had entered upon his threefold missionary charge, Whitefield came a-preaching to America and stirred up a spiritual disturbance wherever he went. Parson Currie's disgust at Whitefield's brand of evangelism and his own staunch adherence to Church of England doctrine and principles are best told in his letter of 

July 7th, 1740, to the Venerable Society. "You have here enclosed an acct. of ye state of my two congregations ["Montgomery" and Radnor being counted as one] from 

May 1739 to May 1740. It may perhaps be somewhat surprising to ye Honble Society to find so great a difference between this and ye It was wishful thinking to speak of the "Parish of Towyn" and the "Parish at French Creek." They had not yet been organised even as missions. The inhabitants of those places were exercising their Pennsylvania charter rights in appealing to the Bishop of London for a priest, but evidently they could not assure sufficient support (as in the case of Christ Church in Philadelphia) to maintain a priest of their own, and it was apparently deemed inexpedient by the S.P.G. to establish other missions there at that time. Of "Towyn" in his History of Old St. David's Church, p. 101, Henry Pleasants writes: "It would be impossible to locate this place with accuracy, but the reference .. to a 'neighbouring parish' at French Creek and the identity of the names of certain taxables would indicate its probable situation in Warwick or West Nantmeal Township, in Chester County."

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley last acct I sent you [in which he says that 'the people at Radnor and Perquihoma carefully attend Divine Service every Sunday, and that the congregation at Perquihoma is much increased and consists of a good sort of People']; But did they know how much pains and Labr ye Revd. Mr. Whitefield has lately spent among us to Rob us of our characters and then of our hearers their wonder would immediately cease. This strolling preacher, what by a musical voice, by an agreeable delivery, a brazen forehead, impertinent asseverations, uncharitable assertions and impious imprecations upon himself, if what he says be not true, has raised such a confusion among the people of this province as I believe will not be laid in haste, and (which I am troubled about) has made a very great rent in all the congregations belonging to the Church of England. The generality of my hearers not only run after, but adore him as an oracle from heaven. 

They look upon all he says to be ye immediate dictates of ye Holy Ghost, only because he confidently asserts it to be so, & Imprecates ye most dreadful curses upon himself if what he says be not true.

There is a very large church a-building for him in ye City towards wch all sorts of people have contributed. This deceiver pretends to be ye only true minister of ye Church of England now in all America & yet he has a Criminal Regard for all those who have ever been ye avowed enemies of ye Church of Engd. When he left this Province last he conjurd. all his hearers especially those of ye Church of Engd to leave their own teachers because their Doctrine was 

Damnable, and cleave to his Dear Bror. Mr. Gilbert Tennant & his

Brern. as ye only true Gospel preachers in the whole country. Now this Gilbert Tennant has ever been lookd upon even by ye Discreeter sort of his own Society [Currie, remember, had previously been a Presbyterian and knew how the Presbyterians felt and thought] as a kind of mad man and generally went by ye names of Hell-fire Tennant. However since he was recommended by Whitefield he is followed by all sorts of people and is now in as great repute as his Master, his whole endeavr is to preach men out of yr reason. In it he has been greatly successful for there are sevl. people now raving mad wch was intirely occasioned by ye Damnatory Sentence pronounced agst ym. by this mad Enthusiast and some of his Crazd. Brethren."

The clergy quite generally concurred in Parson Currie's opinion of Whitefield as a peculiarly dangerous tub-thumping mountebank. His "high-power salesmanship" of salvation may have proved an efficacious laxative for moral inertia and spiritual indifference in certain quarters, but its violent unsettling effects practically nullified any hoped-for enduring good to the general public. For a time the emotional tension verged on hysteria; then, directly it was released, the wave of reaction that surged over the Province left the serio-comic aspect of this ephemeral episode of ecclesiastical charlatanism so pronounced that it is extremely difficult to appraise justly whatever (if, indeed, any) residue of abiding benefit may have remained. There is no record

of Whitefield ever having preached in St. Peter's; it is quite likely, however, he may have done so. 

By May, 1741, the tempest had somewhat abated. Those that had gone astray, upset by the irresponsible rantings of the evangelistic disturber, were coming back to their senses. 

Parson Currie writes the Society, "I supply constantly at Radnor & Perqua Excepting one Sunday in every seven weeks when I'm obliged to preach at Philada. [Christ 

Church, then without a Rector] by The appointment of the missionaries till that Congregation can be provided with a minister of their own.

All the people in my congregations who were smitten wth Whitefield are again returnd to their former principles, So that I have the same number of communicants in Radnor I used to have and in Perqua the number is considerably increased. In Radnor there were last Easter upwards of forty and in Perqua about twenty."

But one ill after-effect of Whitefield's mischievous sojourn was that many people had grown "slack in paying their 

Subscription money and Still Continue so." Then the Parson continues, "out of upwards of 60 pounds Subscribed I dont receive twenty from both places and Bills here are a mere drug having fallen from 70 to 35 so that it goes hard with me." His children were now beginning to come and, careful 

Scot that he was, he took timely precaution to provide for his family. "To mend the matter" of diminished income, he goes on to say, "I was obliged to buy a plantation being turned out of the place where I lived before. The Plantation I have purchased cost me 160 pounds one-half whereof 

I was obligd to pay immediately."

When he writes the Society at Michaelmas of the same year (1741), the reaction to the Whitefield incident had come full tide. He reports with evident satisfaction, "I have two flourishing congregations. Some of my hearers were like to be drawn away by Whitefield & his followers but through the blessing of God upon my Endeavours I have not only kept such to their profession but have brought over Several others Since_his departure, to be my constant hearers & two persons who were Dissenters before have recd the Sacrament from me." 

And he also noted that "the Whole Duty of Man is now much esteemed by hundreds, who knew nothing of it before 

Mr. Whitefield condemned it."

After this last-dated letter there is unfortunately an inter-

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley val of three years before we find another of Parson Currie's more detailed accounts of his parish to the Society. Just before Michaelmas, 1744, he writes from "Plymouth" (evidently Plymouth Township near Norristown), where he had bought the "plantation" previously mentioned, "I acquainted ye Society in a former lettr [of which there seems to be no present trace in the Society's records] That ye upper part of my parish at Radnor were about to make an addition to their [log] Chapel, But instead of that They have built a New Stone one for wch they humbly pray ye Venerable Society for a Bible &

Prayer book." 

Thus he records the building of the present stone church on the foundations laid about sixteen years before. 

At Lady Day, 1745, he writes the Society, "My congregation being much the same as when I wrote in September last I have nothing now to add but that the Church in the Valey wch was then in building was Since opened by the Revd Dr. 

Jenny by the name of St. Peter's in the Valey. There I preach once a month to a Large and Regular Congregation who humbly pray the 

Venerable Society for a Bible & Prayer book." 

To which the Society replied: "It is with Pleasure the Society hears of new Churches rising in Pennsylvania, and have ordered a Folio Bible and Common Prayer 

Book for that of St. Peter's in the Valey."

The "Folio Bible and Common Prayer Book" sent by the Venerable Society are still preserved amongst the parish. treasures.

Parson Currie's allusions-they are scarcely more-to the building of the present structure of St. Peter's-in-the- 

Great Valley leave much to be desired. One must take some allowance, doubtless, for the natural habit of a laconic Scot. 

The one occasion on which he conspicuously departs from his usual terse method of reporting is when he launches into indignant denunciation of Whitefield. Fortunately, other sources enable us to gain a more adequate notion of the new building than we could from the Parson's scant mention. 

The earliest known deed to St. Peter's for the land on which the church stands is dated May 30th, 1744; Methuselah Davis (a member of the first Vestry) deeds the ground for the church and churchyard to William Moore, Thomas

James, Morris Griffith, Richard Richison and John Cuthbert, to be held for the use of the congregation, "at the yearly rent of one ear of Indian corn, to be paid annually whenever legally demanded."

It is particularly worth noting that both St. Peter's and 

St. David's were built by private subscriptions on the part of the congregations and there was no recourse to lotteries to raise the necessary funds, as so frequently happened in the case of eighteenth-century church building. 

Although there is an entry, dated 1744, on the fly-leaf of the old Vestry Book (the earliest Vestry record we have) saying "Be it Remembered that this Year St. Peter's Church

Was Built Under ye Care of Rich Richison, Mathah Davis, Jn° Cuthbert and Morris Griffith," that does not mean that the structure was fully finished. So far as we have any record, there were then no pews, no gallery, neither altar nor pulpit, and it is not at all certain that there was yet any paving or even partial paving. In other words, the bare structure of walls and roof had been completed. Nevertheless, at the second Vestry meeting, on December 4th, 1745 (the first recorded Vestry meeting was in April, the same year or the year before), the Vestry acted with respect to burials inside the church, when "it was ordered and agreed on for ye Better Regulating of said Church affairs That Every Grown Person admited to be Buried within the said Church walls Shall pay by their Heirs, Exetrs admrs or friends ye Sum of five pounds to ye Church Wardins then being and the same to be Laid out for ye Use of ye Church at ye Discretion of ye Vestary for the time being, And Every Minour there Buried As aforesaid Shall pay half ye Sume aforesaid in the Manner afore and for the Use as aforesaid. 

This to Continue till Repeald."

This decision was later to cause trouble, as we shall see. 

At the same time, the Vestry ordered-and may we not here trace Parson Currie's hand?-that to prevent "any Evil Report on our Christian Burial place for time to Come, by any pratice not Admited by ye Church of England Where Establish'd, That no persons what So Ever Laying of Vilant hands Upon themselves to ye takeing away of their Life Shall be admited Directly or indirectly A Burial within ye said Church Yard Walls."

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley 

Not long afterward, they ordered "yt ye southeast Corner of said Church-Yard shall be to Bury 

Strangers in."

Soon after the Vestry had acted on the matter of burials, the people of the Welsh Barony fell into a fever of apprehension at the rumours of imminent Indian uprisings "or intended incursions of the French with their Indian allies on our defenceless western frontier, hardly fifty miles west of the Parish." On the General Fast Day appointed in January, 1747, Parson Currie preached an eloquent sermon in St. David's Church on the text "Shall I not visit these things, saith the Lord," Jeremiah, v, 29. This sermon was afterwards printed and sold by Benjamin Franklin. Dr. Sachse records that the congregations held meetings to urge armed resistance and counteract strong Quaker pacifist opposition in the neighbourhood. The Parson wrote A Treatise on the 

Lawfullness of Defensive War, in Two Parts, by William 

Currie, printed and sold by Benjamin Franklin and David

Hall at their "New Printing Office in Market Street, MDCCXLVIII." Dr. Sachse further records* that many members of the congregations signed a compact binding themselves "as christians and most loyal subjects of our most gracious Sovereign 

Lord GEORGE II, by the grace of God &c, &c., to save if possible our lives, wives, children, liberty and our most holy religion from the hands of our most cruel and merciless enemy, who daily approaches us with hasty, conquering and murderous steps. We have, (first imploring the assistance of the Almighty Sovereign, giver of all victories, on our feeble endeavours) formed and by these presents do form and associate ourselves into an independent Company . . ." 

At the outset, the improvised seating arrangements at St. 

Peter's lacked all semblance of uniformity. The congregation sat on whatever the individual members chose to supply for themselves-benches, stools, chairs brought from home, or what not. It is not quite clear whether, at the beginning, they even took up "pew ground"-definite space, for which a definite sum was due the church-on which to camp during service time on whatever seating conveniences 

*MS.S Sketch of The Reverend William Currie, 1736-1776: Dr. Julius F. Sachse, in Collections of Church Historical Society.

55. they themselves had brought. The institution of "pew ground" and the right of holders to build thereon their own pews-a practice derived from rural churches in the Mother Country-was a recognised usage at St. David's and other early churches. Sometimes difficulties arose when owners of pew ground, in building their pews, trespassed on the pew ground of their neighbours, and it took diplomacy on the part of the Vestries to allay bitter feelings thus aroused; of this we find ample evidence in some old church records. 

Trespass and animosity over pew ground, however, seem not to have troubled St. Peter's.

It was not until September, 1749, that the Vestry took measures to provide uniform and orderly seating equipment. 

They then ordered that the space inside the church should be laid out to allow for thirty-two pews, "Twenty six of said to be Eight feet long and Twenty-Eight Inches wide ye other Six to be seven feet Long and twenty Eight inches wide. And the same Disposed of to the following persons and Under ye following Conditions viz: That Every person who has paid ye sum of five pounds or More Towards ye Building of said Church is intitled to one whoal Pew, and all they which have not paid ye Like Sum are to advance it in one Yeare after ye Date hereof and ye said the Said Money to be Laid out and for ye Use of ye said

Church at ye Discretion of ye Vestary and Wardins for ye time being and its Likewise ordered that if any of ye said persons to Whoam any of said Ground is to them aloted shall Neglect or Refuse to Build thereon a Pew within one Year after Date hereof then it shall and may be Lawful for ye said Vestary and Wardins to sell and Dispose of ye said Ground at their Discretion and the former owner shall for Ever after be Debard therefrom, and it is forder orderd that if at any time here after and after ye said Pews are Built that any of ye Owners of said Pews should Neglect to attend ye services at said Church then ye said Pews to be free for any of the Congregation as shall think fit to sitt in and Use the same- More over it is a Greed yt in Case any of ye owners of Pews as aforesaid should Desert from or Remove away from said Church, then and in all such Cases and Immediately after the sole Right title Claim and property to all such Pews shall be Invested into ye hands of ye then present Vestary and Wardins provid that there be no wife or Children Remaining to Use the said Pew. And it is furder ordered by ye Vestary aforesaid-the the Pews aforesaid When Built shall be Nombered As follows, for ye Better Preventing of any Disputes Which Might arise between ye Owners thereofwho have paid as follows." 

Then comes the first list of pewholders (v. p. 142) and the sums by them paid, then or previously, towards building the church.

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley 

For a long time, "pewing" the congregation appears to have been a serious job for the Vestry. Judging from the 

Vestry minutes, there were more applicants for pews than there were pews to accommodate them. While some of the pewholders retained the same seats for a long period, there were others who, for one cause or another, frequently wished to change their seats. This "moving" element kept the Vestry chronically engaged in what appeared to be a continuous game of "Going to Jerusalem" or "Pussy Wants a Corner." The Vestry, indeed, must have felt a measure of relief when a pew fell vacant and they could re-assign it. 

Two such pews, and those the best in the church, became available on March 19th, 1750, when the first trouble occurred over the stipulated charges for the privilege of burial within the church walls. Judge Moore, of Moore Hall, who had been a member of the first Vestry in 1745 and had continued as a Vestryman until 1748, objected on principle, it would seem, to the extra charge levied for intra-mural interment. The minutes of March 19th, 1750, read: "At a Vestary then held it was Made Appear by Several Evidences that Wm Moore Esqre Did Declare and Say that Unless ye

Vestary would Repeal a certain Act by them Passed the 4 day of 

Dec A.D.1745-Where in they Laid a Duty of five pounds on Every

Grown Person's and fifty shillings on every Minour Grave as Should be Made in said Church &c-that he ye said Moore Would Never

Darken the said Church Door-To Remedy Which ye Vestary Calm ly Deliberated thereon: and having the interest of ye Church of 

England Nigh at hart it was therefore Resolved that the Duty aforesaid Should be Continued on ye Graves as aforesaid-And it is furder Resolved by sd Vestary that in as Much as sd Moore has Excluded him Self from said Church, that the two pews to him aloted 

To Witt No 7 and 8 Should be Disposed of for ye Benefit of ye

Congregation And according was the one to Mr. Curry [Parson 

Currie] ye other to Jno Hambryth."

This summary action of the "Vestary" doubtless further incensed the irascible and masterful Judge. From that time onward he and the Lady Williamina, his spouse, went either to St. James's, Perkiomen, or to St. David's, Radnor, where eventually they were both buried at the threshold of the church. 

As recorded in Judge Moore's family Bible, his sevenyear old daughter Margaret was buried in St. Peter's on

July 18th, 1745. This was probably the first burial inside the walls of the new stone church. The Lady Rebecca (Axtell) Moore, Judge Moore's mother, died in 1749; by the terms of her will, she directed that her body be buried in St. Peter's Church-in-the-Great Valley. 

Burial inside the church as early as 1745 would seem to indicate that at that time the stone paving-later, in the days of disfigurement, to be covered up, along with the gravestones, by a wooden flooring-had already been laid. 

One burial, at least, was made beneath the pew of the deceased owner. Robert Powel, one of the first Vestry of 1745 and a valued member of the Vestry for more than thirty years, was buried (presumably by his own desire) under his pew, Number 16. The Vestry minutes of April 9th, 1787, record: "At a Vestry Meeting this 9th day of April 1787 after being

Elected it was Laid before them what the Sakton of said Church Should demand from the Executors of Robert Powel Deceased for taking Down the Pugh & Digging the Grave under said Pew for

Said Deceased & Putting it up that he should have the Sum of 

Twenty Shillings."

Less than three months after the unpleasantness with 

Judge Moore, "At a Vestary. . . . it was ordered that Richd Richison and Jno 

Potter should Agree with some Joiner to Build a Pulpit, Reading Desk and a Communion Table and to floore and Banaster ye same and accordingly they agreed with Jno Wayne for £ 12..0..0-To 

Do & Complete ye same Workman Like."

This early altar, the first of which any mention occurs in the records, stood in its proper place beneath the large round-headed east window. The high pulpit, with the reading desk for the clerk beneath it, stood against the north wall nearly opposite the south door. In the mid-eighteenth century, when the present stone structure was built, ancient ecclesiastical tradition was still strong enough to ensure the proper placing of the altar, pulpit and reading desk, and the use of the south door as the main entrance; it was left for the nineteenth century "improvers and beautifiers" to glorify the pulpit by placing it at the east end above the altar (which latter then often gave way to a mean little

The Church of St.

Peter-in-the-Great Valley table), along with the perpetration of other wanton irreverances and defacements.

The original sacristy was a small stone projection on the north side of the church; the small doorway from the sacristy into the church was just to the west of the doubledecker pulpit and clerk's desk. Even after the nineteenthcentury "improvers" pebble-dashed the outside walls in the age of vandalism, they could not obliterate in the coating over the masonry the traces left by this doorway. As a matter of fact, all the wall spaces and the positions of windows and doorways in the whole building unmistakably indicate the original (and traditional) arrangement of the interior. What the old sacristy looked like may be gathered from an early picture of St. David's, Radnor, where the original sacristy occupied the same position as that at St. 

Peter's. The two churches were originally as like as two peas in all essential points, for St. Peter's was evidently (and quite naturally) patterned after St. David's; the only noteworthy difference was that St. Peter's was considerably larger.

The thirty-two pews at first planned for seating the congregation proved insufficient to meet the need for seats. In 

December, 1750, "At a Vestary it was agreed there should be a Galary Built a Cross ye West End and along the South Side of said Church and Richd Richison and Robert Powel was agreed on to Undertake ye Same and to agree with Workmen to Build and Complete ye Said Gallary the Same to be paid with ye Money Arrising and Now in Arrears for ye Ground whereon ye Pews are Built-But in Case it 

Should prove not Sufficient then ye Remainder To be Made Up by Subscription."

The building of the "Galary" went on at a leisurely rate for, in May, 1752, ".. at a Vestary held at ye Said Church it was agreed that ye 

Building of ye Gallory in ye Church should be proceed on and finished."

Paying for the "Gallory" was even slower than building it. 

In December, 1753, there is an entry in the minutes that "ye said sums [£ 6.0.0.] w paid to Edward Pearce toward

ye Building of ye Gallory," and as late as April, 1754, it is ordered that "Robert Powel & Edwd Pearce the Undertakers of ye Building of ye

Gallory is to pay of all ye Charges of said building now Due thereon they useing their Best Indeavours to Collect ye outstanding Debt to them Due for the purpose: and all ye Lacking or Remainder the 

Congregation is to pay to them in Six Months."

A truly naïve method of financing church improvements, this! The foregoing is the last entry anent payments for the gallery. Let us hope that success rewarded the "Undertakers of ye Building" in "useing their Best Indevours to 

Collect ye outstanding Debt to them Due," and that they didn't have to wait six months for any "Lacking or Remainder."

Before the gallery was paid for, the Vestry ordered that "three Double Pews May be Let to proper persons applying therefor, on ye west End of ye Galary and in the Front Each pew to take two of the Seats: the persons taking any one of the same are to Build thereon in one year after their so taking they paying for Each of said pews in one Year as fore said to ye Wardins for the time being ye Sum of six pounds to be Laid out at ye Discretion of ye Vestory and forther to subject themselves to ye payment of twenty five shillings per anam for the use of ye Minister .. 

In spite of all the provisions about pew-building and renting in the gallery, the final completion of gallery equipment hung fire for some years longer, if we go by Parson Currie's 

Michaelmas report to the Society in 1759. The Society's minutes note his reporting that "about three months before they had put the last hand to the finishing of St. Peter's Church with a large gallery which, with the floor below, is crowded every time he preaches there." 

The Parson must have been alluding to the completed seating arrangements in the gallery, not to the completion of gallery structure. It is impossible to see how the actual building could have dragged on so long; the unfinished construction would have become unsafe.

The stair to the gallery-it is just possible the gallery may have been an afterthought to the original scheme, prompted by necessity-seems to have caused some disturbance of the pew lay-out as first planned before the addition

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley of a gallery was considered. At the same Vestry meeting at which the manner of pew-building and pew-renting therein were determined, it was ordered that "Jno Potter and Robt Tompson is to have yt pew Ground Joyning of ye North Wall of ye Church subjecting themselves as aforesaid Excepting Jno Potter who is to have his share of ye Ground Free In Lue of his half of Pew No 23: which is Discommoded by ye Galary stares."

At the Vestry meeting of May, 1752, when it was agreed that the building of the "gallory" should be finished, "the Said Vestery aprovd of ye Dispousal of ye old Logg Church." Tradition says that a member of the Vestry bought the materials of the demolished building and therewith built his son a house, and therein the son raised a large family of children. The Vestry minutes give no clue to the identity of the purchaser nor the whereabouts of the dwelling he reared with the erstwhile ecclesiastical logs. 

With the fabric of the new stone church completed, and the old log church demolished and removed, it remains to picture the clergyman as he stands in the high pulpit opposite the south door, the clerk in the reading-desk below him. 

The congregation with its varied personnel has already had attention. Parson Currie, from all we can gather a stickler for clerical proprieties, preached to his flock arrayed in bands and a black academic gown of fine Spanish cloth, on his head a full-bottomed powdered wig. Such, during the eighteenth century, was the decorous habit for preaching and saying the choir offices. Indeed, even in the nineteenth century, there were some of the clergy-conspicuous Anglo- Catholics at that-who put off their vestments at sermon time and donned the black gown and bands for preaching, along with the hood to denote the academic nature of sermonising.

To visualise Parson Currie's august dignity, as he looked down over the heads of his people, we have no portrait to help us. Of so many of the Colonial clergy there are portraits, more or less excellent and trustworthy; of Parson 

Currie the only likeness is said to have been a pencil sketch, possibly a caricature, and that seems to have disappeared as

Said Deceased & Puling it up that he should have the Sum of Irventy Shillings
