completely as every vestige of the old log church. Nor have we any trustworthy description of his personal appearance; we must be content to recall his aspect mainly by the help of powdered wig, black gown and bands. At the same time, in limning our mental picture of priest and people, we must not forget Mrs. Currie (of whom there is a striking portrait in the possession of a descendant) and the Currie children. sitting in pew Number 7, one of the two 'aloted" to the clergyman by the "Vestary" in March, 1750, when they had their difference of opinion with Judge Moore.

If we lack data for a clearly defined portrait of Parson Currie as he visibly appeared to his people, nevertheless existing correspondence and persistent local tradition reveal a good measure of his personality and outstanding characteristics. He was undoubtedly possessed of vigour, endurance and conscientious fidelity in the discharge of his pastoral duties. He likewise had qualities that endeared him to his parishioners. Had he not been vigorous and enduring, he could never have coped with the tremendous physical difficulties confronting any missionary attempting to cover a field as extended as the triple cure of Montgomery, Radnor and Perkiomen, with the unbridged Schuylkill between the two former and the latter-and that in all extremes of winter cold and storms or the parching heats of summer. 

Had he not been able to enlist the abiding affections of his flock, there would not have been the many memories of devoted attachment that still endure. With all his other work, he found time to educate his own children and, it is said, sometimes prepared other young men for college.

We know, too, that though wiry and blessed with Scottish tenacity, he was not robust. In his letters to the Venerable 

Society, there are too many mentions of ill health, and over a considerable period, for him to have been a person of Herculean physique. His letters are not those of an hypochondriac or a chronic complainer. His disorder, whatever it was, was real.

Along with his other traits, he had the proverbial Scottish carefulness and there is frequent evidence of solicitude for the comfort and welfare of his family. His letter of

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley 

Michaelmas, 1747, to the Venerable Society furnishes sidelights on the condition of the writer and also on the attitude of some of his people respecting responsibility for mission ary stipend. The Parson says: 

"I beg leave to acquaint ye Venerable Society that the Congregations of Radnor and Perqr have in a great measure withdrawn their Subscriptions ever since I began to officiate one Sunday in every three weeks in St. Peter's Church and the Congregation there being generally poor though numerous makes up but a very small sum for me annually which together with the great expense I am at for ye recovery of my health having laboured under a very ill state for sevl years makes it very hard for me to maintain my numerous family. I therefore humbly pray the Venerable Society to take my case into their Consideration and redress this grievance either by allowing me a Small annuity for officiating at St. Peter's or else enjoining the Congregations to Contribute more liberally to my Support. I might have made this Complaint two years ago but waited in hopes that time would have brought them to a better mind." 

The Society heeded this appeal for financial assistance, for he writes to thank them "for the generous present which I shall in all my future Conduct make it my Constant Endeavours to deserve." His "infirm State of health," however, troubles him the following year and, at Lady Day, 1751, his "ill state of health which rather grows worse than better" leads him to ask eight or nine months' leave of absence as "I am advised by my Physicians that a Voyage to Sea would be of service to me, and having some affairs relating to my Family to Settle in Scotland." This talked-of voyage never materialised, although the Society were willing to grant a leave of absence.

The Parson's complaint may have been some kind of nervous indigestion; his Lady Day report of 1755 tells how 'he is frequently ready to faint during the Service with a Spasmodick pain, from which he is never free, but worst when Speaking in Publick.' If he had been living to-day, perhaps the doctors would have attributed his disorder to allergies or insufficient vitamins-or, possibly, his teeth.

The Venerable Society's minutes record that in the same letter Parson Currie desires, if possible, that "the Society will send him an assistant, to whom he will resign all his perquisites, reserving only for the support of his numerous family the Society's bounty. Mr. Currie has been missionary at Radnor for eighteen years next May, during all which time he never had ...

the least difference with any member of his numerous congregation, for though they may have been very negligent in their subscriptions yet he would never quarrel with them for private interest, but if the Society should appoint an assistant Mr. Currie hopes they will enjoin the congregations of Radnor, Perquihoma and St. Peter's to contribute generally towards his support." 

Entered in the minutes, the curt rejoinder to this suggestion reads: "Agreed as the Opinion of the Committee that Mr. Currie be acquainted that it is not the Practice of the Society to keep Curates for their Missionaries. 

Reesolved to agree with the Committee." 

The Society's minutes of the following spring note that

Parson Currie has reported his health much improved "and that if he should be once able to take a voyage by sea, for which he has obtained leave of the Society, he is persuaded it would be of great service to him. . . . The Rev Mr. Smith, Provost of the

College in Philadelphia, has promised to supply Mr. Currie's place whenever he shall be able to undertake his intended voyage." 

That Provost Smith should be willing to undertake for a season the exacting duties of Parson Currie's three country parishes speaks well for the esteem in which his fellowclergy held the Missionary of Montgomery, Radnor and Perkiomen. The Parson officiated at the ceremony at Moore 

Hall, on June 3rd, 1758, when Provost Smith married 

Rebecca, the daughter of Judge Moore and the Lady Williamina.

Of the Parson's chronic indisposition we hear very little further until March, 1761, when he writes, "Notwithstanding my sore affliction I have been enabled to attend my Churches without intermission ever since my last Letter by ye help of an anodyne taken every morning before I set out." 

Again, in September of the same year, he writes dolefully, "Yet so grievious is my affliction that next to a sense of God's favr through Christ ye prospect of ye happy moment when I shall exchange ye present for a future state gives me ye greatest pleasure." 

But by October, 1765, he reports his health "better this half year than for several years before"; March, 1766, sees such improvement that he is "able with ease and pleasure to

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley attend ye Dutys of my sacred function"; and by March, 1768, he writes with confidence, "Blessed be God, I enjoy a much better state of health than I did some time ago so that I am able to attend my Numerous Congregations more constantly & with a great deal more pleasure now I am sixty years of age than when I was forty."

With this encouraging declaration of fitness ends the chronicle of clerical aches and pains. The removal from his shoulders of a load of worry and anxiety about "place of habitation" in all likelihood had much to do with the Parson's restoration to normal health. 

What this worry was, the letters to the Society plainly show. In his letter of March 31st, 1760, the Parson writes: "I must beg leave to acquaint the Society that although my hearers are many in number especially at Radnor and ye Valley, yet they are become so very careless and lukewarm that I cannot get them to meet on Easter Monday to chuse a Vestry. [This has a very modern sound!] And as to my support among them there are but a very few that give themselves any concern about it and hitherto I have lived upon a place of my own purchasing, but as my ill state of health rendered me incapable of manging it any longer [he apparently had to be something of a farmer as well as a priest] I have parted with it and am now destitute of a habitation for my numerous family and as there is a small Glebe belonging to Perquihoma Church but no house on it, I pray ye Society may enjoin ye Congregation to rebuild ye house or purchase one more convenient, a standing Rule of ye Society they have never yet complied with which if they neglect to do I must petition to be removed to a mission where 

I can have a convenient habitation."

The Venerable Society recognised the indisputable justice of this complaint and their minutes of August 15th, 1760, show that it was "Agreed as opinion of Committee, that letter be wrote to Vestry of Radnor desiring them to build good. house for Mr. Currie. Unless this be done Society will withdraw their Missionary from them."

This communication from the Society produced no immediate result. At Michaelmas, 1763, the worried Parson again writes, "I have the pleasure to acquaint you that my congregations of Radnor and the Valley daily encrease; but that at Perquihoma rather declines as the Dutch buy out the English and settle in their room. 

I have acquainted the Society that the Glebe House is in ruins and have expected orders from time to time to the Congregations to repair it, and if it is not repaired soon I must beg leave to petition the Society for a remove to another Mission where I can have a better

support for my numerous and expensive family having nothing to depend upon here but the Rent of the Glebe which is but Five pounds this Currency Parr. and about Twenty pounds a year for marriages besides the Society's Salary."

Still nothing happened about providing a Glebe House for the Parson's "numerous and expensive family." Discouraged and sorely harassed by the uncertainties of outlook, he writes at Lady Day, 1764, "As I have diligently attended ye Dutys of my Station ever since I was appointed their Missionary I hope ye Venerable Society will permit me to be absent next Fall a few months when I intend God willing to settle two of my sons in ye Colony of Granada where if ye Society would be pleas'd to open a Mission & appoint me their Missionary it would be a great favour to my poor Family as I Could then have an opportunity of providing for them much better than I have here. I have six sons & one Daughter to provide for which 

I have no prospect of doing to any Advantage in my present situation as ye price of Land is high & I have nothing to depend upon but ye Society's bounty & a small perquisite from Marriage." 

To this the Society replied that they had "no thought of appointing a Missionary to Grenada," but that the Parson might "have liberty to go there a few months next Fall as requested, provided he procure a proper substitute." Meanwhile he had written the Society that he had "received ye 

Society's Letter of 23 Feb. 1764 to ye Church Wardens of 

Radnor, which I shall lay before the first Vestry I can get to meet, & report ye Result."

The matter of the Glebe House still hung fire and in 

March, 1765, the Parson writes, "Your favr. of Sept. 19, 1764 I had ye honour to receive and return my kindest thanks to ye Venble. Society for their indulgent offer, but as there is to be no mission opened in Granada and my State of Health continues much ye same I must leave my sons to push for themselves and be content to spend ye small remains of Life yet before me in ye station wherein kind Providence and my generous Benefactors have been pleasd to place me, & I shall endeavour thro. ye help of God as I have always done hitherto that it shall not be spent in vain. *Parson Currie's "six sons & one Daughter" were all children by his first wife,

Margaret Ross Hackett, who lived until 1771. By his second wife he had no children. 

The seven Currie children were (1) John, who became a lawyer, married a wealthy wife and settled in Bethlehem, Pennsylvania; (2) James, who became a physician and practised in Chester County; (3) William, an "erudite and experienced physician and an extensive successful practitioner" in Chester County, "author of several important medical works," had an honourable military career in American service, and subsequently removed to Philadelphia; (4) Richard, who entered the American army, died in 1776. leaving a widow and three children; (5) Alexander, a physician, who went to the West Indies, married, and died there; (6) Ross, who served in the American army, and afterwards removed to Canada; and (7) Elizabeth, who married Dr. Demon, of Reading, Pennsylvania.

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley 

The Society's letter to the Vestry of Radnor &c. I laid before them which they have still under consideration. The congregation of 

Perqua. is willing to comply with it, but that of Radnor is not, alledging that as the Glebe is not situated convenient to them they cannot think of laying out their money upon it but would rather in conjunction with ye congregation of St. Peter's purchase a small 

Glebe betwixt these two churches, but as I am satisfied they are not of ability, the price of land being exceeding high, I have thought of living in ye Glebe at Perqua, towards ye repairing of which & rebuilding ye Glebe house I have subscribed 14 lb of this Currency which is 9 lbs. sterl. unless the other two Churches will either purchase or hire a House for me."

Despairing of getting the Vestries of St. Peter's and St. 

David's stirred up to any united action, he writes in October, "for as ye congregations of Radnor & the Valley are like to do nothing towards providing a Glebe I intend with ye Society's leave to move my family to ye Glebe at Perquihoma, ye House whereof is almost finished by that generous handful of people and my own assistance."

At the end of March, 1766, he writes the Society again, • • ".. I long for an answer to my last letters relating to ye Glebe

House, not being willing to move my family to Perquihoma till I know ye Society's pleasure, which, with their leave, I am resolved to do unless ye congregations of Radnor and ye Valley provide me a 

Convenient Habitation 

At present I live on a little farm I purchased some years ago, which my expensive Family obliges me to fill in order to put my Sons in some way of business." In May, 1766, the Society consented to Parson Currie removing his family to the Perkiomen Glebe House and he seems to have gone there soon afterwards. There is some uncertainty, however, as to just where the Curries lived at various times because the Parson usually dated his letters from "Radnor"-in all likelihood because the official designation of his mission was "Radnor and Perkiomen." At any rate, for some years after the removal, freed from anxiety about "place of habitation"—this constant worry had probably aggravated his bodily ailment-the Parson was vastly more comfortable in mind and body. In his happier and more settled condition we may leave him for a time and look more immediately to the Church in "ye Valey."

In 1750, before the leisurely building of the gallery began, the physical inability of the Parson to be in three widely distant places at one and the same time led to St. 

Peter's Church becoming the birthplace of two Chester

County Lutheran congregations, Zion and St. Peter's, in adjoining townships. The establishment of these two congregations, through the efforts of Dr. Henry Melchior 

Mühlenberg, marks the foundation of Lutheranism in Chester County. In his Journal Dr. Mühlenberg, who then lived at the Trappe (only a few miles from St. James's, Perkiomen), enters under the date of Wednesday, May 2, 1750, on his way home from missionary business at Lancaster, "Journeyed back home with my colleague, Mr. Brunnholtz; on the way baptized a child, reaching Providence on May 3. [HN: An 

English justice of the peace, who was present at the Baptism, said that there was a great many poor German servants, men and women, indentured to the English people in this neighbourhood, and that they wished I would come some time and preach to them, since the poor people did not understand English and for a long time had had no divine service in their mother tongue. The justice said that he and his English neighbours would grant me the use of the English church for the Germans, on condition that I would also preach an English sermon for them. But since they had a regular preacher in the church, I replied that I would not make use of the church for the benefit of the Germans unless they first consulted the preacher himself and obtained his consent to it, so that no dispute might arise between the preacher and his congregation, particularly since I had hitherto been on very friendly terms with the preacher. The justice said that they had already asked him beforehand and obtained his consent. 

So I promised to come there several weeks hence."] 

The next month (June, 1750), he notes in his Journal, "After this I made a trip of 17 miles to the neighbourhood where a great many of our German people are indentured to English people as domestic servants. The English church was opened for me and, as 

I had promised, I preached an English sermon and afterwards a German sermon. The English people were very attentive and much moved, and they desired that I should come frequently. There was weeping among the Germans, as is usually the case when they have been deprived of the Word of God for a long time. The English were amazed at our singing and almost went into raptures over it, for some of the people had fine musical voices and knew how to sing in harmony."

There is no specific evidence elsewhere to identify by name the "English church" where Dr. Mühlenberg preached to the Germans as St. Peter's, but tradition has always attached this beginning of Lutheran missions in Chester County to St. Peter's. Furthermore, the distances noted elsewhere in the Mühlenberg Journals and in other Lutheran missionary memoranda, of that date, all point to St.

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley 

Peter's as the spot where the "great apostle of American Lutheranism" first ministered to his indentured countrymen in the Great Valley. Since Parson Currie, with his other churches to care for, could officiate only one Sunday out of every three at St. Peter's, it was not unnatural that the congregation in the "upper part of the parish" should be ready to work in an extra preaching service when opportunity offered; the eighteenth-century taste for sermons was keener than that of our own day. 

If a fair number of Germans with good voices sang some of the old chorales like "Wake, Awake!" or "Now thank we all our God," in harmony a capella, St. Peter's folk may well have been thrilled. Though not quite so fine perhaps as the Medieval Latin hymns, like "Corde natus" or "Vexilla

Regis," they were more impressive than Welsh melodies like "Tony Botel" in unison, and infinitely superior to the introspective so-called "belly-ache" psalmody, with its personal musings, which English congregations of that period usually had to put up with. 

In his MS. life of Parson Currie, already quoted, Dr. 

Sachse implies that the congregation of German redemptioners continued for some time to have their services at St. 

Peter's and says there is a tradition that the Germans wished to buy the old log church "for their exclusive use, but the Vestry, after due deliberation, refused the request and decreed its removal [1752], that there might be less danger to the congregation [St. Peter's] in case of any sudden Indian attack while at worship."

At the risk of what may seem a somewhat lengthy digression, it is well to point out that the dread of Indian attack was by no means groundless at any time during the period of the French and Indian War. How the people of outlying country districts lived with an ever-present threat of pillage and murder hanging over their heads plainly appears in a letter Dr. William Smith wrote the Archbishop of Canterbury in October, 1755. He says: "Our situation at present is such as never any Country was in. 

The Province is powerful in men, in money and in all sorts of provisions. From twelve to twenty thousand men might be raised on

THE WEST GALLERY; AS LEFT BY THE ALTERATIONS OF 1830 

15 

(Courtesy of the Chester County Historical Society.) Photograph by C. V. D. Hubbard. 

OLD PEW DOORS DATING FROM THE ALTERATIONS OF 1830; Now PRESERVED BY THE CHESTER COUNTY HISTORICAL SOCIETY

an emergency and we could find provisions for six times that number. And yet we have not a single man in arms but our lives and our all left at the mercy of a savage crew who are continually scalping around us and among us. While I am writing this I hear of a large number cut off on our own frontiers. The winter approaches and the King's troops have left this province so that we have a most melancholy prospect. 'Tis extremely distressing to think that we have strength sufficient and yet by the very Government that owes us protection to be restrained from making use of those powers which God and Nature gave us to repel violence and prevent the inroads of injustice and iniquity into the world." 

The Pennsylvania Assembly-"the very Government that owes us protection" indicated by Provost Smith in the letter just quoted-was the one duly constituted body in the Province in whose hands lay authority to create and equip an armed force to repel invasion and protect the lives and property of the people. But the Assembly was completely under the domination of the Quakers, who were astute politicians and had always contrived to control both legislation and the strings of the public purse. And the merest suggestion of military organisation, even though solely for defense, was wholly repugnant to Quaker principles and prejudices and enough to elicit their most rancorous and obstinate opposition.

To obtain appropriate defense measures and relieve the chronic apprehension clouding the lives of all the people, a former Vestryman of St. Peter's-the Honorable William 

Moore-suffered no little annoyance and persecution because of his determined and justifiable stand against the supine pacifism of the Pennsylvania Assembly. The part 

Judge Moore played in trying to secure adequate military protection against the imminent threat of Indian massacres -instigated by the French-is succinctly set forth in an address the late John Cadwalader, Esquire, delivered before the Annual Day Association in September, 1932.* Mr. 

Cadwalader, himself a descendant of Judge Moore, said: "William Moore was an outspoken advocate for a militia law and for arming for the defense of the Province, and very open and pronounced in his views. The Religious Society of Friends. . . . greatly irritated by his activities, thereupon armed themselves for another This paper contains full and illuminating details of the devious, unscrupulous and undignified proceedings on the part of the Assembly in the pursuit of their quarrel with Judge Moore and Provost Smith. For access to this paper the authors are indebted to 

Mrs. John Cadwalader.

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley kind of warfare, not at all, however, of a disarming nature. They procured petitions from disappointed suitors in Moore's own Court and other discontented persons, charging him with extortion and various forms of injustice. Thereupon in August, 1757, the Assembly summoned him to answer these charges, to which Moore replied in a respectful memorial, proclaiming his innocense of the charges but refusing to appear because the Assembly had no right to try him and, if tried at all, he was amenable to the law courts. The Assembly, thereupon, in Moore's absence proceeded to hear the petitioners ex parte and, without other proof, addressed the Governour, charging Moore with oppression, injustice and corruption in office and praying for his immediate removal. This address was published by the Assembly, and to it the Governour replied that he would appoint a day for a hearing, when both sides could appear and be heard, and that no man should be condemned unheard. 

The Assembly which had made the charges dissolved and a new one was elected and convened in October, 1757. Moore, thinking he could not overlook the charges made and published against him by the preceding House, presented to Governour Denny what he called "The Humble Address of William Moore,' the opening sentence of which reads, May it please your Honour: Whereas the late Assembly of this Province, upon a number of groundless and scandalous petitions, most shamefully procured against me by one or more of their members from sundry persons of mean and infamous characters, did on the 28th of September last, present to your Honour and order to be published in the common Gazette, a most virulent and slanderous address, charging me in the bitterest terms with divers misdemeanours and corrupt practices in my office, without exhibiting any other proof thereof than their own unjust allegations founded on the evidence of the said petitioners procured as above and taken ex parte before themselves, who were invested with no legal authority for so doing.' The address then went on to explain the motives behind the 

Assembly's accusations, viz. Moore's efforts with others to have a 

Militia Law passed, and the evil consequences to the rights of freemen if the Assembly were allowed to exercise such arbitrary powers and condemn persons unheard as if they had judicial authority."

To this sturdy and altogether proper defense of his rights and reputation the incensed Assembly retorted in January, 1758, by arresting the Judge on a charge of libel and throwing him into gaol-an wholly unjustifiable, high-handed and illegal proceeding. They also arrested and gaoled Provost 

Smith on a libel charge, as accessory to the publication of the Judge's address to the Governour. Both the Judge and the Provost very properly refused to plead before the Assembly, and they were kept in confinement. At length the 

Assembly grudgingly conceded that the Governour, and not they, had jurisdiction over the Judge.

The upshot of it all was that a trial took place before the 

Governour on August 24th, 1758, at which the Assembly

evinced abundant malice in the prosecution. Judge Moore was fully exculpated, acquitted and retained his seat on the bench for many years thereafter. Since Provost Smith held no civil office, his only recourse lay in an appeal to the Crown through the Lords of Trade and Plantations in England, which appeal he made. When the Lords of Trade and Plantations heard the case, at which both the Provost and the Assembly had counsel to represent them, the Attorney-General appearing also for the Crown, "the decision was a complete reversal and victory for Smith. . .

Not only was Smith entirely exonerated but the Assembly was roundly berated. They were charged with invading the liberties of freemen and of encroaching on powers which belonged only to the Sovereign."

Both the Judge and the Provost were friends of Parson 

Currie. Although Judge Moore was no longer in the Vestry of St. Peter's when this strife with the Assembly occurred, he was still active in the associated cures of St. David's, Radnor, St. Peter's-in-the-Great Valley, and St. James's, Perkiomen. The incident throws an interesting sidelight on the disturbing conditions that then affected the lives of all of St. Peter's parishioners.

The church fabric and its fixed equipment finished, or virtually so [as already explained, Parson Currie's allusion of 1759 must have referred to seating accommodation, not structure], the Vestry in 1755 turned their attention to outof-doors and "agreed with Edward Pearce to put up 16 pannel of post and rail Fence along ye front of ye Graveyard for-£ 2.0.0."-evidently the stone wall at that time did not entirely surround the churchyard. Further attention to the exterior needs occurred in the summer of 1762, when the 

Vestry ordered that "Two Stables should Be built 20 by 16" -sheds to shelter the horses during service time.

So far as we know, there was no provision for heating the church until 1762. Up to that time, it would seem, in bitter weather the people of St. Peter's had either to pray with. blue noses and benumbed fingers and toes or else depend on the inadequate warmth of such footstoves or hot bricks as

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley individuals brought with them. But in August, 1762, the 

Vestry ordered "two Stoves had to accommadate ye Congregation the Same to be had and computed as Soon as may be the same Being Done under ye 

Care and Jurisdiction of Edwd Peirce David Jones Richd Richison and Robert Ralston."

Stoves and horse sheds were apparently paid for much more promptly than gallery-building or pew-making; in November we find "Delevired to Dad Jones to pay for ye stove including 15/- already Paid ye Maker s.30," and "Paid Edwd 

Peirce in part for Building ye Stables-£ 2.15.0."; and, in 

December, "Paid Jn° Rowland toward ye Stove £4.12.6."

Although several later payments are noted for both stoves and sheds, the debts therefor were cleared within a reasonable time. 

But delayed settlements appeared inseparable from pew or gallery building. As late as April, 1765, we find inscribed in the Old Vestry Book, 

"We ye Subscribers Do promis to Pay to Robt Rolston the several sums anexed to our Names, to and towards Paying for ye Building of 5 Pews now in the Gallery Which sums is to be Reamburts out of ye Rents arriseing from said Pews." 

Then follows a long list of names with the amounts of subscriptions. By this date, the clerk, secretary or other person who made entries in the Vestry Book had learned to spell "gallery" although the rest of his spelling may still have been distinctly original.

In May, 1770, the Vestry paid £ 0.18.0. for "a Bason 

Cup & 2 plates," This would seem to indicate the purchase of some communion plate. From the price paid, it must have been pewter. What St. Peter's had done for sacred vessels up to that time we do not know; perhaps, as part of his missionary equipment, Parson Currie had a set that he used. The rumour occasionally circulated that St. Peter's had a silver communion service, presented by Queen Anne, is altogether fabulous. There will be occasion later to mention the sacred vessels subsequently acquired by the parish, and tell what became of them.

The last recorded permanent improvement made at St. 

Peter's before the outbreak of the Revolutionary War was

the building of a stone wall along the front (presumably the road front) of the churchyard. In December, 1770, "John Gronow Received Twenty pounds by order of the Vestry for defraying the Expences of Building the wall on the front of the graveyard &c- John Gronow likewise Received of Subscription money towards the wall three pounds seven shillings & three pence."

Final payment for the churchyard wall was made in November, 1771.

At various times since the building of the stone church, well-disposed members of St. Peter's congregation had bequeathed legacies for the use of the "upper end of the parish." None of these legacies was large, but they had begun to be a real nest-egg for a future endowment fund. 

What happened later in the matter of endowment fund and of other legacies bequeathed in the post-Revolutionary period is a story that belongs to a subsequent chapter of parish history. 

An interesting note in the Vestry minutes of April, 1773, records that it was "Ordered that Jonathan Evans while he Continues to Officiate as

Clerk in St. Peter's Church Shall have the Benefits of the Rents of all the Pews in the Gallery to be Collected by himself." 

This Vestry-imposed self-help in collecting his own stipend may or may not have been an ungrateful task to Jonathan Evans; if the gallery pew-holders didn't like his services, they could be annoying. At any rate, the clerk's position was a duly recognised institution. Doubtless it was the clerk's duty, on the Sundays when Parson Currie was busy at St. David's or St. James's, Perkiomen, to act as layreader and conduct the services at St. Peter's.

About this time Parson Currie had other help than Jonathan Evans in conducting lay-services, probably at St. Peter's as well as at Radnor and Perkiomen. In his letter of 

March, 1771, to the Society, he reports, 66 One of my sons (a hopeful youth) I am educating for the Ministry, and hope my honble Patrons will receive him into their 

Service when qualified for holy orders. 

He is now in the 21st

My son is a great help to me in reading year of his age. 

Prayers and a Discourse to the congregations when the weather is too 

Stormy for me to ride out."

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley 

Although only sixty-three at the time, the Parson was feeling the pressure of age. This is scarcely to be wondered at in view of the arduous, exposed missionary life he had led continuously for more than thirty years in a threefold cure covering a wide extent of territory. Besides the physical strain imposed on a man whose "ill State of health" had continued to plague him for many years, there were his harassing anxieties about "place of habitation," already mentioned, as well as family worries. 

Of these latter, the most poignant seems to have been in 1762, when James Currie, then in military duty, got into serious trouble as a deserter. All we know of this affair, that caused the Parson such evident distress, appears in his letter to "The Revd Mr. Richard Peters in Philada.

As gratitude is all ye Tribute great & generous minds will have in return for ye Benefits they confer I can only beg you to accept ye overflowings of a Heart charged with ye most grateful resentments for your kind Promise to intercede with his Excellency General Monkton on behalf of my unhappy son James who (as I have been informed lately) dishonourably abandoned his Post in ye army under his Excellency's Command in Mortinieo. Should that unhappy youth once ye Darling of both his Parents be obliged to submit [illegible] ye fatal sentence of a Court Martial it would Dr. Sr. [illegible] our gray Hairs with Sorrow to ye Grave.

May gracious [illegible] therefore give success to your Endeavours preserve [illegible] for a Comfort to the distressed & reward this most [illegible] instance of friendship and humanity with the Concert of its Blessings here and hereafter. 

I am Rev. Dr. Sr. with ye greatest Esteem 

Your ever obliged & most humble servant 

William Currie"

There may have been extenuating circumstances. In any event, Dr. Peters's good offices seem to have averted the punishment impending for this grave military offense. James 

Currie was ultimately restored to the bosom of his family and later became an highly-esteemed physician with an extensive practice in Chester County.

Parson Currie, as we have already seen, was not insensible to the political conditions of his time or to the alarms of war that perturbed his people. He felt called to speak out again in the summer of 1763. "In June, 1763, very threatening rumours having again reached Radnor concerning the devastations of the Indians, under Pontiac,

who had at that time advanced as far as Carlisle, Mr. Currie, on the 

Sunday after the arrival of the news, announced his text from Ecclesiastes, vii, 14.-'In the day of prosperity be joyful; but in the day of adversity consider.'

So eloquent was the old gentleman's discourse that the congregation, impressed with a sense of their danger from an attack by the Indians, resolved themselves before leaving the church, into a mutual protective association and, after due deliberation, returned to their homes, carefully cleaned and prepared all the guns and other weapons found in the neighbourhood, and thus made full arrangements to resist the Indians should they approach." "This Home Guard" to protect the families of the countryside "continued, it is said, until Pontiac's Peace and withdrawal in August, 1764."* Presumably this stirring call to arms was uttered in St. David's. Whether the Parson preached a similar sermon at St. Peter's we do not know; like as not, he did. Of one thing we may be certain—he had the hearty concurrence of one of his parishioners, Judge Moore, and probably the equal approbation of Anthony Wayne, of nearby Waynesborough. Politically, these two gentlemen were usually at loggerheads; in a matter of this kind, where readiness for defense and opposition to Quaker "passive resistance" were urgently needed, they were in full accord.

After he had established himself (in 1766) in the Glebe House at Perkiomen, the Parson's materially improved health, and his relief from carking anxiety about an assured dwelling place, brought him but a few untroubled years before a crushing sorrow befell him in the death of Mrs. 

Currie. He writes the Society in March, 1771, "On the third of this instant I buried my wife whose unexpected death is the sorest stroke I ever met with. As my Family is numerous and my congregations contribute next to nothing towards my support I shall find it extremely hard to keep them together. . . . Blessed be 

God your aged servt in the 63rd of his Life performs the Duties of his function with more pleasure & greater ability than when 15 years younger, having received a better state of health. It is part of my daily prayers in private that it may please God to take me to himself before I become superannuated & unfit for Duty but should it be otherwise ordered by ye almighty & alwise Disposer of human affairs I humbly hope my generous Benefactors will not cast me off in Extremeity."

To this the Society replied with expressions of sympathy *Henry Pleasants, Esq., quoted by Dr. Sachse in his MS. Sketch of Parson Currie, in the collection of the Church Historical Society.

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley and a reassuring message of due consideration for his future welfare. In March, 1772, he writes them, "On the 28th of Decr. came to hand your kind sympathizing letter of 20th of Aug., 1771 

The assurances you give me in it of the honble Society's kind and generous Intention towards myself & son afford me no small consolation under my present declining state & I can only say that I want words to express the grateful acknowledgements I am willing to make to them for so much goodness." 

However great the Parson's grief at the loss of a devoted wife, his constant companion for thirty-two years, the first sharpness of his sorrow eventually abated enough for him to take a "realistic" view of the situation. He became convinced it was expedient for him to marry a second time. In his Michaelmas, 1772, report to the Society, he very frankly states his attitude in the matter: "Being extremely destitute and uncapable of managing my numerous Family consisting of Children, Grandchildren and Old Negroes, without a Wife, I was induced to marry again. I made choice of a 

Widow Gentlewoman of my own congregation, who being a prudent religious Woman of a suitable age, fifteen years younger than myself, without any incumbrance & a remarkable good manager, seems every way qualified to render my future life comfortable. And as being far advanc'd in years, I know not but this may be the last opportunity I humbly beg leave to recommend her to the notice of the honble Society that she may have the same indulgence after my decease with other Missionarys' Widows." 

The second Mrs. Currie (born Lucy Godfrey) was the widow of David Jones of the Great Valley. In spite of the 

Parson's visions of a rapidly approaching demise, he was to live thirty-one years longer.

Three years after his second marriage, he was again fretting about his "place of habitation" and the conditions under which he had to live because his three flocks could not agree about joint maintenance of the Glebe House. At 

Michaelmas, 1775, in his semi-annual report to the Society he writes: "The Glebe Land belonging to my Mission consists of 40 acres with a little ruinous House upon it which wants repairs because the Congregation of Radnor declines to assist ye Congregation of Perquihoma alledging that it is too far distant from their Church and yet they are not able to purchase a Glebe any where else, which they ought to do unless they will assist in repairing the Other as it is by means of that only they are Supplied with a misionary. In yé meantime I am obliged to provide a Habitation at my own Expense when

(Courtesy of John C. Winston Co.) WEST END AND NORTH SIDE OF ST. DAVID'S CHURCH, RADNOR, WITH OLD SACRISTY BEFORE ALTERATIONS. THE OLD SACRISTY ON THE NORTH SIDE OF ST. PETER'S WAS OF SIMILAR TYPE

all I receive from my three Congregations including the Rent of ye Glebe which is nine lb. currency or Six lb. Sterl. pr. ann, does not amount communibus annis to above twenty lb. sterl. pr. ann.” 

Why this apathy amongst the congregations, why their indifference or negligence to contribute to their parson's support or show more readiness to cooperate in maintaining his dwelling in good order? The condition seems at first all the harder to understand when we consider what the Parson had written quite cheerfully only six months earlier in his spring report, when he speaks of serving "my three churches in their turn, where I read prayers and preach to large congregations of respectable & orderly hearers with whom 

I live in great Harmony and Esteem."

We must seek the answer in a variety of causes. At this time the whole atmosphere was highly charged with political unrest and great uncertainty about the future; interest centred on the quickly-changing events from day to day rather than on the ordinary course of social, economic and religious life of more normal and orderly times. The Battle of Lexington had been fought and every thing was in a state of boiling ferment. Moreover, the congregations of both St. 

David's and St. Peter's had in the recent past made considerable outlays in the improvement of the church properties. Again, there was a rapidly swelling wave of ill-feeling towards the Church of England amongst those of Whig sentiments; and many in the three congregations were either 

Whig sympathisers or active Whig proponents. Then, too, for a long time the Parson had always complained more or less about his "place of habitation," and the people had probably come to look upon his reiterated complaints as a species of chronic grumbling with no really serious cause back of it; the Parson had made the mistake of weakening his position by repeatedly spreading his complaints over too wide a general field, instead of vigorously pressing only specific and obviously well-founded demands at the psychologically right moment to get attention and definite results. 

Also, the knowledge that in Maryland and Virginia the Church derived its support from a government tax, while in Pennsylvania it had to depend upon the voluntary contri-

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley butions of its members, may well have caused a certain small but enduring undercurrent of dissatisfaction that eventually slowed the hand that loosed the purse-strings. By no means least of all, the Parson was better off than some of his letters imply; witness his will and the inventory of his estate. 

In other words, like a good many others in circumstances that cannot be called straitened, he habitually made a "poor mouth." With all this his people were fully acquainted and could better judge the actual conditions than can we after the lapse of 170 years. To add to the sum total, there may have been also other slightly contributory causes that we cannot at this distance of time even conjecture; enough have been offered to clarify somewhat the situation. 

Between the date of Parson Currie's last-quoted letter and the time of his resignation the seething excitement in the Colonies was steadily mounting and political animosities had become rife in every community. The congregations of 

Montgomery, Radnor and Perkiomen were divided between

Whigs and Loyalists and, so far as we know, the Whigs were greatly in the majority. The outstanding Loyalist amongst the parishioners was Judge Moore. Loyal from the first in his allegiance to the Crown, he so remained to his dying day. While recognising the injustices of which the 

British Government were stupidly guilty, "he was 'most vehement in his disapproval of any attempt on the part of the Colonies to separate from the Mother Country.' During the encampment at Valley Forge, Colonel Clement Biddle and other distinguished officers were quartered at Moore Hall, where they were courteously treated by the master. Any allusion, however, to the right of the Colonies to throw off British dominion would throw the old Judge into a state bordering on apoplexy.' On one occasion, when a party of soldiers, sent to deprive Loyalists of their arms, went to Moore Hall and found the haughty occupant confined to an easy chair, suffering from a bad attack of gout, which did not tend to improve his irascible temper, they discovered a beautifully wrought sword whose hilt was inlaid with gold, silver and precious stones. 

They were about to make off with it, when Judge Moore insisted on having a last look at the prized heirloom. No sooner had he taken it in his fingers than he snapped the blade from the hilt. Holding the hilt tightly in his right hand, he threw the useless blade at the feet of the leader of the party. 'There,' he cried, 'take that if you want to fight, but you shall not rob me of my plate!' 

The rebels he regarded as a rude rabble . . . .' 

*The Portrait of a Colonial City: H. D. Eberlein and C. V. D. Hubbard, p. 149.

Judge Moore died at Moore Hall in the spring of 1783. 

The Lady Williamina died a year later. Both are buried at the threshold of St. David's Church. In all likelihood, Parson Currie took part in the service at the burial of his old friends.

At the opposite political pole in the congregation was 

Anthony Wayne, of Waynesborough.* Prominent in Whig counsels and activities, he was head of the Chester County Committee of Safety, was appointed a member of the 

Colonial Committee of Safety, in June, 1775, and in January, 1776, became Colonel of the Fourth Pennsylvania Regiment. On September 21st, 1777, at Paoli, in an engagement with British troops only a few miles to the south of St. Peter's Church, he was to suffer that reverse known as the "Paoli Massacre." His subsequent distinguished history as a successful commander is too well known to need repetition. He also, like his political opponent, Judge 

Moore, is buried at St. David's. 

Another ardent Whig, and a member of St. Peter's

Vestry, was Captain Patrick Anderson. He had been a captain in the French and Indian War and, in 1774, a member of the Chester County Committee of Safety. Now that the Province was again involved in war, he resumed military service and became senior Captain in command of the Pennsylvania Musketry Battalion, in 1776. Afterwards (1777) he was Captain in the 13th Regiment of the Pennsylvania 

Line. Elected to the Pennsylvania Assembly in 1778, he continued to sit in that body until 1781. When he died in 

1793, he was buried in St. Peter's Church. 

With strong diametrically opposed political convictions dictating the actions of his parishioners—and there were many of them who held no less pronounced opinions-the Parson's lot was not a "bed of roses." The case in which the Church of England clergy found themselves at this time clearly appears in a letter Provost Smith wrote the Venerable Society, after the Battle of Lexington but nearly a year before the Declaration of Independence. Writing from *The Portrait of a Colonial City: H. D. Eberlein and C. V. D. Hubbard, p. 170.

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley 

Philadelphia early in July, 1775, and speaking of Parson 

Currie and the other Pennsylvania clergy, the Provost says: "Their difficulties in their missions are greatly increased by the present alarming state of things, and never were men in a more trying or delicate position. We had hitherto with one consent and one mind kept our pulpits wholly free from everything bordering on the present unnatural controversy. But now our people have all taken up Arms and entered into Associations never to submit to the Parliamentary claim of taxing them at pleasure. We see nothing in our churches but men in their uniforms, & tho' they excuse us on Sundays, yet they are everywhere requesting occasional sermons on the present situation of things. The case of the poor Missionaries is hard. To comply may offend their protectors and those that support them in the Parent Country. To refuse would leave them without congregations every where, and perhaps it is more the wish of some that they should refuse than comply. . . ."

After the outbreak of actual fighting, Parson Currie's position became increasingly embarrassing. By his ordination vows, as a Priest of the Church of England, he was bound to pray for the King and Royal Family when he read the services of the Church, regardless of any political convictions he himself might hold. The many Whigs in his congregations would stomach none of these prayers for a monarch against whom they had rebelled. Dr. Sachse 

"... it is said that at the last service Mr. Currie held at St. David's 

Church in 1776, as during the course of the Litany he read, "That it may please Thee to keep and strengthen in the true worshipping of Thee, in righteousness and holiness of life, They servant GEORGE, our most gracious King and Governour,' a dead silence ensued and instead of the usual response, "We beseech Thee to hear us, good Lord,' a single distinct voice from the gallery answered, 'Good Lord deliver us.'"*

The state of tension may be imagined. It has been said that the doors of his churches were closed against the Parson and that he was forcibly prevented from holding service. There is no evidence, however, to substantiate such a statement. 

Loyalist as he was by conviction-although passive in conduct and held by the obligation of his ordination vows, which he conscientiously obeyed, he saw it was impossible for him to continue to hold public services and, accordingly, he ceased all public ministrations. *The Reverend William Currie, 1736-1776: Dr. Julius F. Sachse; MS. now in, collection of Church Historical Society.

Divisions within his own household sharpened his personal unhappiness and distress; while he himself was unswerving in his fidelity to the Crown, three of his sons were serving in the American army-Ross, as a first lieutenant in Captain John Reese's Company of second Battalion, under 

Colonel Arthur St. Clair; William, as Surgeon in Colonel 

Samuel John Atlee's Musketry Battalion; and Richard, in the First Militia of Pennsylvania. (The last named was invalided home and died in September, 1776.)

Disheartened at the sight of his life's work falling to pieces, and fully conscious of the unalterable current of affairs, on May 16th, 1776, the Parson addressed a letter of resignation to the Wardens and Vestrymen of St. David's, Radnor. Four days later he wrote his resignation to the 

Wardens and Vestry of St. James's, Perkiomen. It is not at all unlikely that he sent a similar letter to the Wardens and Vestry of St. Peter's, but of that there is no record; the 

Vestry minutes of St. Peter's are completely blank from 

April 1776 to 1780. His letter to St. David's Wardens and 

Vestry says: "Gentlemen,

Age and infirmity having rendered me unable to officiate any longer, I take this method to let you know that I shall decline attending your church any more, but though Providence has so ordered that I can serve you no more in public, yet God forbid that I should cease to pray for you in private. No, as I have taken the best care

I was able under an infirm state of health to shew you a good and right way, so while I breathe I will not cease to pray that God may give you his Grace to enable you to walk in it. And as I shall not cease to pray for you, I beseech you, neglect not to pray for yourselves.

Prayer is at all times your duty, but more especially in troublesome times. When deprived of the church, make use of the closet, and there pour out your complaints to him who seeth in secret and will in his own good time reward you openly.

A devout man, though he has but his chamber to retire to, and his doors be shut upon him, yet he lives as it were in Goshen. 

When flashes of judgment burst upon other persons, 'tis calm in the prayer room; when the destroying Angel had overrun every house in 

Egypt with death, when there was nothing but carcasses and crying in each dwelling, there was not one Shriek in all the land of Goshen. 

When a thick darkness dwelt upon the nation, the praying Israelites had light in all their dwellings, and when sad, dark clouds set as it were, on God's countenance and pours down Inundations of Tempests upon a careless, lukewarm and backsliding people, yet even then his face shines in closets of Devotion there he breaks in and reveals his comforts and is so as his Angel was at that time a pillar of light to

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley the one and of cloud to the other. Let the Devotion Chamber be your 

Sanctuary till these troublesome times be overpassed; flee for refuge to the horns of the altar, the throne of Grace, there offer up the Incense of your prayers and let the lifting up of your hands be as the even Sacrifice. Thus, my dear little flock, I bid you heartily farewell and am with great love and affection your faithful pastor till death. 

William Currie" "The letter of the old shepherd," as Henry Pleasants says, "breathes only of affection and solicitude for his flock."

Although he declined thenceforth to officiate in public, he nevertheless continued to baptise, to perform the marriage office, and to bury the dead amongst his parishioners during the following years of conflict and sorrow.

9333333333333333 

CHAPTER IV  

OR the story of St. Peter's Church during the 

Revolutionary War we have to depend almost entirely upon tradition. Between 1776 and 1780, as already stated, there are no entries in the Vestry Book. In Parson Currie's infrequent letters there are only a few scattered allusions from which we can draw inferences. From other sources we know what was going on in the neighbourhood and from sundry unquestioned facts of local history we can surmise, but only surmise, to some extent the condition of the "upper end of the parish" during this troublous period.

Parson Currie's resignation in 1776, as Mr. Pleasants points out in his history of St. David's, "completely paralysed the Church organisation." While the Parson continued to baptise, visit the sick, bury the dead and marry the couples that came to him to have their union hallowed by the Church, his public ministrations ceased as totally as though he had been a thousand miles distant. There is reason to believe that he interested himself to procure the occasional lay services of a young candidate for the Lutheran ministry but, in the absence of all further pastoral direction, parish life came to a standstill and inevitable disintegration set in.

It is said that while a part of the British army, under 

Major-General Gray, lay encamped at Tredyffrin in the

Valley, just before and after the "Paoli Massacre," in September 1777, St. Peter's served as an army chapel for the King's troops. A map made at this time by one of 

General Gray's engineers, and afterwards published in Lon-

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley don (facing p. 45), shows St. Peter's as a conspicuous landmark of the region. At one time or another during the 

Revolutionary conflict, so we are told, both the British and American forces used the church as a military hospital. The sick and wounded of both sides who died there, their fellowsoldiers buried along the west wall of the churchyard; the graves were marked merely by rough stones. 

The story goes that after Parson Currie's resignation. various sectarian ministers, from time to time, held services and preached in St. David's and that on one occasion, in the early stages of the war, General Wayne's chaplain, the Reverend David Jones, then in charge of the Great Valley Baptist Church, in Tredyffrin, was invited to preach. "After finishing the introductory exercises, he climbed into the lofty pulpit, and having announced his text, glanced up in order to reassure himself of the attention of his audience. That one glance, however, completely disconcerted the worthy Welshman. He saw seated comfortably before him several young and active men, previously hidden from so close a scrutiny by the old-fashioned highbacked pews. In an instant patriotism had so completely mastered him that he threw away his sermon and, shaking his finger vehemently at the astonished youths, demanded to know why they did not go into the American army. 'I'm not afraid to go,' he screamed. "They can't hurt me; they may kill me, if they like, and make a drum-head of my old hide, but they'll beat a tattoo that will scare the British out of the country.'

Then in wild excitement he threw off the heavy cloak, which hung around his shoulders, and displayed an American uniform."*

Whether St. Peter's also was sometimes opened to sectarian ministers, or whether it was ever the scene of similar dramatic clerical recruiting for the Continental army, we do not know.

The church building seems not to have suffered any serious material damage at the hands of either American or British soldiery-not even as much as St. David's apparently, and nothing like the profanation of the Lutheran 

Augustus Church at the Trappe, where Dr. Mühlenberg complained that an undisciplined company of Americans, who had little regard for their captain's authority, bivouacked in the building and used part of it for a stable. St. Peter's escape from harm has been attributed partly to British re-

spect for a church whose parson was known to be a staunch Loyalist, partly to American regard because so many of the parishioners were either in the Continental army or in some way active for the American cause. Whether this supposition be correct or not, it is now impossible to say. Whatever be the true cause, and whether the church's immunity came about by chance or by design, we only know that in the Vestry minutes of 1780, when a Vestry election is recorded, there is no allusion to damage. Nor is there any claim for war damage recorded in the County records. The first mention of repairs to the church fabric occurs in the minutes of

Sarah Wistar was obviously for glass intended for minor replacements and evidently belonged to some date prior to 1776, since Edward Pearce died about 1777, and there were no Vestry meetings between 1776 and 1780. These "lites" of Wistar glass would now be almost priceless, could they be identified; they were probably destroyed in the last deplorable remodelling. It was not until 1788 and 1789 that any considerable repairs (beyond the 1785 reshingling of the roof) are noted, and the itemised accounts show that the work then carried out could scarcely be counted as restoration from war damage.

Parson Currie's loyalty to the Crown may, perhaps, have

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley had some effect in saving St. Peter's from desecration at the hands of the British soldiery. It did not, however, save him from pillage of his personal possessions by British foraging parties. In a letter of claims for war losses, on file in the 

County Records at West Chester, the Parson writes: 

"On the 19th day of September, 1777, [this was while General Gray's troops were in camp nearby at Tredyffrin] a Company of Soldiers from the Camp came to my house and robbed me of all my 

Cabbage, Bacon, Cheese and Butter, a bushel of fine Salt & all my fine sheets, Table linnen, fine Shirts-head dresses, stockings & table silver spoons to the value of 20 Pds.

There is the strongest presumption likewise that at the same time they robbed me of 200 Pds. Continental Money in sheets the Money 3 for 1 is 66 Pds. 13. 4. 

And the day following a foriaging party took from me two waggon loads of Oats and do. of Wheat beside several Horse loads of both, a good Cart & Gears all my Waggon & Plow Gears, Collars & Blind Halters & Ropes, 2 Men's Saddles half worn & 3 Bridles all which I judge to be worth 20 Pds.

The truth of all which I will be Qualified to But as to the Continental Money though there is the strongest presumption as they certainly carried of a file of News papers upon which the sheets had been strung some days before, yet as it is possible that some body might have taken them of the file before that day, I do not find freedom to swear to it. 

William Currie."

Later in the struggle he suffered further inconveniences from military intrusion, in addition to personal sorrows and family anxieties. The minutes of the Venerable Society, of 

July 18, 1783, record: "Letter reported from Mr. Currie dated April 30, 1783, hoping bills will be paid; if not his position is deplorable, as war has reduced him to very low circumstances. He has lost not only the most of his substance but likewise his wife and a son and his wife, with whom he lived in his old age. They all died of camp fever and left him in the midst of the camp [this seems to have been during the Encampment at Valley Forge] with one of the American Generals and his suite quartered in his house. He is left with three orphan grandchildren, oldest seven, when parents died. He blesses God that he has been enabled thro. grace in the midst of these difficulties to hold fast his integrity and he will die as he has lived a true son of the Church of England even tho he should have the misfortune to survive it.

The Society's mission being, as Mr. Currie supposes, superseded by the terms of the Peace, he looks upon this to be the last of his correspondence with his noble benefactors in England, and therefore lays hold on this last opportunity to return them his thanks, &c." (He had previously written the Society, in September, 1780, that 'as he lives entirely on credit of money he borrows on

credit of Society he relies that bill will be paid his executors if not presented in his lifetime.')

The Parson's total claims presented to the County for "damages and losses sustained . . . . by military forces" amounted to £106. 13. 4. His "low circumstances" doubtless caused him concern, but they were not so low as to be anywhere near destitution. As Mr. Pleasants says, in his history of St. David's, "It is difficult to reconcile his professions of poverty, however, with the fact that the assessment rolls of Tredyffrin Township show him to have been the owner of seventy-six acres of land and numerous cattle in 1774, and of 200 acres from 1779 to 1781; that he also had considerable land in Montgomery County, and that he left an estate valued at £ 3,116. 4. 9., and a will (dated December 28, 1794, and registered at West Chester in will book K 436), whereby he bequeathed many legacies, including £10 (sterling) to Radnor Church."

Notwithstanding the severance of the American Colonies from the Mother Country, and in spite of the Parson's resignation from his cure in 1776, the Venerable Society continued its support for some years after the termination of hostilities. The Society's minutes of December 17, 1784, record: "From the Rev. Mr. Currie dated Radnor in Pennsylvania, October 1st, 1784, acknowledging the receipt of the secretary's letter of 27 August, 1783, by which he is encouraged (a favour for which he adds he can never be sufficiently thankful) to draw as he now does for his last half year's salary.

Thinking his Mission actually dissolved, he is far from claiming this as a debt, but thankfully accepts it as a bounty. The severity of the last winter has had such an effect on his aged constitution that he has been mostly confined to his house ever since, where though a non-juror he officiates without molestation. 

The churches are sometimes supplied by a wandering Lutheran clergyman, and they live in hopes of being better supplied shortly, as the clergy of the United States are about settling a frame of government for the Episcopal churches in North America. Mr.

Currie was invited to join their convention, but not having taken the oaths to the State, he declined it. They propose to keep as near to the government and worship of the Church of England as their circumstances will admit, but they are at a loss to find out how to preserve the Episcopal Succession. It has been proposed to send one of their own number duly elected to England for consecration, but there are doubts whether such a person would be consecrated or no. 

This question has been proposed to Mr. Currie, who declined giving his opinion till he had consulted his learned brethren of the Society. 

If any of them will be pleased to favour him with an answer he will accept it as a singular favour."

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley 

In the preceding March (1784) a number of the clergy had met at the house of the Rev. Dr. William White, Rector of Christ Church and St. Peter's in Philadelphia, and had addressed a circular letter "to the wardens and vestrymen of the different Episcopal congregations in the state," asking them to "delegate one or more of their body" to represent them at a meeting subsequently to be holden in Philadelphia. At this preliminary meeting it was especially resolved "that the letters addressed to the churches formerly included in the mission of Radnor be included under cover to the Rev. William 

Currie, their former pastor, and that the clergy be desired to accompany them with a letter to the said reverend gentleman requesting his assistance at the proposed meeting."

Although Parson Currie declined to attend the proposed meeting, as appears from his last-quoted letter, he transmitted the communication to his several vestries. In accordance with the request, St. Peter's Vestry took action and the old Vestry Book has the following entry: 

"May ye 15th 1784

At a Meeting of the Wardens and Vestry of St. Peter's Church held this Day Agreeable to the Request of a Committee of the Different Episcopal Churches in the City of Philadelphia in order to Delegate a person out of our body to represent us at a General Consultation held in sd City on the 24th Instant When John Francis was unanimously Chosen for the Above purpose.”

St. Peter's-in-the-Great Valley, therefore, was duly represented from the outset in the councils of what ultimately became the Diocese of Pennsylvania. 

With the return of peace, Parson Currie, so far as his health permitted, resumed his pastoral duties in the three churches of his Mission and the Venerable Society continued his stipend until 1785 when the aging clergyman finally ceased from all further parochial activity. His last draught on the Society was in October, 1785. The extended financial support from the Venerable Society was doubtless a great boon to all three churches in this period of readjustment.

The minutes of the Society note that in a letter of March, 1785, the Parson says: "His congregation desire him to return the Society thanks for all past favours and to assure them that notwithstanding the present

unhappy situation of the members of the Cherch of England in America, they will never cease to pray for the prosperity of the Society for the Propagation of the Gospel in Foreign Parts." 

The resumption of services with some degree of regularity, and the readiness of the people to attend, appear in the Parson's Michaelmas letter of 1785. He writes the 

Society's secretary: "Blessed be God notwithstanding my great age I have been able to attend my former Churches in complyance with ye Society's continuing my salary duly ever since November last, where I have officiated to a crowded Audience & baptized a great number of children, there being no Episcopal Mnr. within twenty miles of me but myself, which services I make it a point to receive no lucrative Emolument [from the people], having made a firm resolution when I laid down my charge at ye Declaration of Independence never to take Wages of Subjects of a Government to which I cannot give my Test of Allegeance.

I have only to add that as I am really sorry that I am an incumbrance to my worthy Benefactors in my present superannuated State so I am cheerfully ready to desist as soon as they shall desire it, as I want but little & that little I shall not want long. Thus with my most grateful acknowledgments and sincere prayers for prosperity and success to ye honble Society and ye whole British Nation I remain with all due respect 

Revd Sir 

Your affectionate Brother & most humbl Servt 

Willm Currie."

On March 4th, 1786, in conformity with an act passed by the General Assembly of Pennsylvania in September, 1783, St. Peter's was incorporated and the church granted a charter by the State Legislature. The incorporators were 

Thomas Bull, Benjamin Thomas, Richard Richison, John 

Ralston, John Rowland, John Francis, Cromwell Pearce, Thomas Wilson, Joseph Rowland, Richard Robinson, John

Evans and Roger Little. In taking the important legal step of incorporation, St. Peter's was more than six years ahead of the sister church of St. David's, which did not receive its State charter until August, 1792.

CHAPTER V Expansion and Nineteenth-Century 

Vandalism

FTER Parson Currie finally ceased from all active ministrations to the three churches of his Mission in the autumn of 1785, there was no regularly appointed priest to care for the congregations until 1788, when the Reverend Slator Clay became Rector. During this period, nevertheless, St. Peter's, St. 

David's and St. James's, Perkiomen, were not wholly without some measure of pastoral attention. 

Slator Clay, the fifth child of Slator and Ann Curtis Clay, was born in New Castle, Delaware, October 1st, 1754.

Bred to the law, he was admitted to the bar in 1779 or 1780. About this time, presumably for his health (which was always delicate), he sailed for the West Indies with a sea-captain friend. A British privateer took their ship and landed him on the Island of Antigua. Thence he took passage on a boat bound for New York, then still held by the British forces. On his way thither, he again fell captive to a privateer-this time an American-and the boat he was on headed for Philadelphia with a prize crew in charge. 

They narrowly escaped shipwreck off Cape Hatteras and eventually were cast on the Island of Bermuda. There the young Delaware barrister remained for some years, during which, according to his son's account, "he taught school there and endeared himself very much to the inhabitants. About this time a great change took place in his religious views and feelings and he determined to devote himself to the ministry of the Church. The people of Bermuda wished him to go to 

England and receive orders of the Bishop of London and then return

and officiate for them as their clergyman. His wish, however, was to return to this country and spend his life here." 

Coming back to New Castle, and learning the pastorless plight of the three churches formerly under Parson Currie's care, Slator Clay served this field, so far as he could in the capacity of a lay-reader, until such time as he could take Holy Orders. Although there is no specific record of such services, in all likelihood neighbouring clergy also occasionally visited the churches, as they found opportunity, and administered the sacraments.

In the meantime, the Reverend Dr. William White, Rector of Christ Church and St. Peter's in Philadelphia, had been elected Bishop of Pennsylvania, had gone to London in November 1786, and been consecrated in the Chapel of 

Lambeth Palace on February 4th, 1787. Returning to

Philadelphia, he could now exercise his Episcopal functions and ordain candidates for Holy Orders. The story of the negotiations and vexatious delays attending the establishment of the American Episcopate is well known and may be omitted as it is not essentially a part of this narrative. 

Suffice it to say that the task confronting the new Bishop was one of enormous difficulty and that Slator Clay subsequently did yeoman service in sustaining Bishop White's efforts for the welfare of the Church. 

On August 14th, 1787, St. Peter's-in-the-Great Valley, St. David's, Radnor, and St. James's, Perkiomen, made a momentous decision. Representatives from the three churches met at Norristown and "agreed to continue in the union for Episcopal Minister to perform Divine service in said three churches as heretofore" and appointed Robert Ralston from St. Peter's, Richard Willing from St. David's and Dr. 

Robert Shannon from St. James's "jointly to apply to the

Right Reverend Bishop White, of the State of Penna., for an Episcopal Minister to officiate in the said churches." 

That Slator Clay was the person they had in mind for the incumbency in making this application appears from the 

Vestry minutes of St. James's, Perkiomen, which record that, "At a meeting of the Vestry and Wardens of St. James's Ch. 

Perkiomen this 8th day of Decemr. 1787 the following recommen-

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley dation in behalf of Slator Clay was drawn up transcribed and sent to our Sister Churches of St. David's Radnor, & St. Peter's in the 

Great Valley for their Concurrence and afterwards sent to the Right 

Reverend Bishop White. 

We the subscribers members of the United Protestant Episcopal 

Church of St. James, Perkiomen, St. David's, Radnor, & St. Peter's in the Great Valley, in the State of Pennsylvania, being desirous of having an officiating minister who is properly authorized agreeably to the Canons of our church. 

And whereas Mr. Slator Clay hath resided amongst us and nerformed the office of a Reader in these churches for more than one year, discharging his duty in that capacity to our satisfaction, We do therefore recommend him as a candidate for Holy Orders, hereby agreeing to accept of him as our minister and make such provision for his maintenance and support as hath been heretofore agreed upon. 

For and in behald of ye Congregation of St. James's 

James Shannon 

Nathan Pawling 

John Jones 

Thos. Read 

John Francis 

John Ralston 

To the Right Revd 

Bishop White." 

For and in behalf of ye Congregation of St. David's 

For and in behalf of ye Congregation of St. Peter's

Bishop White acted favourably upon this application, ordered Mr. Clay deacon and, on February 17th, 1788, ordained him to the priesthood in St. Peter's Church, Philadelphia. Parson Clay immediately thereafter entered upon his duties as the first American Rector of St. Peter's, St. 

David's and St. James's, Perkiomen. In 1786 he had married Mrs. Hannah (Holstein) Hughes and lived at Upper

Merion, where he continued to live for some years after his ordination. It is told of him that while at Upper Merion he had a dog named Mars, of apparently pugnacious temperament, known all over the neighbourhood. On the dog's collar was engraved the identifying couplet, "If I from home should chance to stray, My owner's name is Slator Clay.

In Upper Merion I do dwell, My name is Mars, for I fight well."

Whether Mars ever accompanied his master on his incessant pastoral peregrinations, we are not told; probably he did.

The Parson was a mild and peaceable man, so it would be up to Mars to supply the requisite dash of pugnacity to represent appropriately the Church Militant.
