MEMORIAL TABLET ON NORTH WALL OF ST. PETER'S CHURCH. ONE OF THE ONLY TWO MEMORIAL TABLETS EVER PLACED IN CHURCHES BY ORDER OF THE PENNSYLVANIA LEGISLATURE

Residence at Upper Merion brought Parson Clay into close contact with the Swedish church just across the river from Norristown (Christ Church, Bridgeport), and to the congregation there he gave part of his attention, besides preaching frequently at St. Thomas's, Whitemarsh. With these additional activities, over and above the care of the three churches of which he was Rector, there was ample scope for the evangelistic fervour with which he was filled; with all the territory he had to cover he must have been as much on the roads as a country doctor with a large practice. 

No one of his three regular churches could have seen very much of him-at best, probably once or twice a month on Sundays. Even while his home in Upper Merion brought him within comparatively easy reach of both St. Peter's and 

St. David's, this was the case. When St. James's, Perkiomen, built him a Glebe House near the church, and he moved there at some time after 1790, it was more difficult to reach St. Peter's and St. David's, and the people of St. 

David's seem to have felt they had but small share of their 

Rector's attentiion, although their esteem for him in no wise diminished. 

Tradition says that Parson Clay always came hastening to church on horseback, wore neither surplice nor black gown and bands but conducted service in coattails, and "omitted most of the ritual in order to have a longer time for his always extemporaneous sermon. Tradition also tells that he was a strong "temperance" advocate—“temperance" being the misnomer of that day for prohibition. 

When he first went to his three churches, as related by one of his parisioners, it was then the country custom thereabouts to "set out liquors at funerals. He immediately announced he would attend no funerals where they had liquor, and soon broke up the habit. 

He also was accustomed to omit from the marriage service the words 'serve and obey.' He said he did not want to make the women tell lies."

Until his death in 1821, Parson Clay remained Rector of the three churches, but for a long time prior to that he was increasingly dependent upon assistance, both clerical and

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley lay, for the services at both St. Peter's and St. David's. 

Part of that assistance was supplied by his son, the Reverend Jehu Curtis Clay (afterwards Rector of the Gloria Dei, in 

Philadelphia) and by the Reverend Samuel C. Brincklé, who subsequently became Rector of St. Peter's and St. 

David's. St. James's, Perkiomen, in whose Glebe House the Parson lived (and in whose churchyard he is buried) was less dependent upon supply ministrations. Such an ardent Evangelical was the Rector, and such importance did he attach to constant preaching and exhortation that actual pastoral care, in the shape of regular parish visiting and the cultivation of close personal contacts with the individual members of his widely scattered flock, became an altogether secondary consideration. It could scarcely be otherwise, considering his views upon homiletical obligation, coupled with the extent of territory he felt it incumbent upon him to cover. Notwithstanding the deficiency in the workaday and less spectacular side of parochial care, he was greatly beloved and respected and Bishop White, in his Convention address of 1822, paid a marked tribute to the deceased clergyman's "zeal in his holy vocation." 

In May, 1821, just a few months before Parson Clay's death, the Vestry minutes of St. Peter's record, "A Committee appointed consisting of Cromwell Pearce, John G. 

Wersler and Walter Kerr, to procure and have placed up in this church a Marble Stone with the proper subscription. agreeable to the resolution of the Legislature of Penna to the memory of Daniel Evans deceased." 

This "Marble Stone with the proper subscription" (facing p. 93), a beautifully lettered tablet, they placed on the north wall of the church above the door of the still-existing old Sacristy and just west of the old high pulpit. In its own words, the Act of the Pennsylvania State Legislature tells the story of how the tablet came to be there: "An Act authorizing certain Trustees to sell and convey a plantation or tract of land in Chester County, being the Joint property of the Anna Baptist Congregation, in Vincent Township, and the congregation of St. Peter's Church in Tredyffrin township.

Whereas it hath been represented to the legislature, by the trustees of the Anna Baptist Congregation in Vincent Township, Chester

County, and by the Wardens of the Episcopal Church of St. Peter's

in Tredyffrin township in the County aforesaid, That Daniel Evans, late of the township of West Whiteland, in said County, by his last will and testament, dated the twenty-seventh day of August, seventeen hundred and seventy-five, did give and bequeath unto the said churches, a plantation or tract of land, in the township of West Whiteland aforesaid, whereon he then lived, after the death of his wife, Sarah (who is since dead), containing one hundred and fiftyseven acres more or less, the said Anna Baptist Church to have two thirds of the rents and profits thereof for ever, towards the support of the ministry of said Church; and the said Episcopal Church of 

St. Peter's the other third part of the rents and profits, for and towards the support of the Ministry of said Church for ever: 

And as it has been represented by the said trustees and wardens that it would be much more to the interest and advantage of the said Congregations, if the legislature would pass a law authorizing them to dispose of the said plantation, and invest the monies arising therefrom in some productive fund, for the use of the said Churches; 

Therefore Section I Be it enacted by the Senate and House of Representatives of the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania in General Assembly met, and it is hereby enacted by the authority of the Same That as soon as may be after the passing of this act, the Elders of the Anna Baptist Congregation in Vincent Township, and the Wardens and Vestry of the Episcopal Church of St. Peter, in Tredyffrin Township, in the County of Chester, shall each of them appoint two persons or trustees, who are hereby authorized and empowered to sell and dispose of the said plantation or tract of land, with its several rights, privileges and appurtenances as soon as the same can be conveniently done, either by public or private sale, for the best and highest price that can be gotten for the same, and to give assurances and conveyances valid in law, to the purchaser or purchasers, and his or their heirs forever, and the monies arising therefrom to place at interest upon mortgage or other sufficient security, paying or causing to be paid annually two thirds of the interest arising therefrom, to the elders for the time being of the Anna Baptist Congregation aforesaid and the remaining one third to the Wardens and Vestry for the time being of the Episcopal Church of St. Peter aforesaid, for the support of the Ministry of the said 

Churches respectively, and upon the death of the said trustees or their successors, or either of them, the executor or administrators of the said trustees, or their successors, shall assign and transfer all bonds, specialities, mortgage, and other securities taken in trust as aforesaid, to such person and persons as the said Elders, Church Wardens and Vestry shall respectively appoint to succeed the trustee or trustees to the deceased:

Provided Always that in the case of the dissolution of the corporation of the aforesaid Churches, that then all the neat proceeds of the said sale, in whatever manner the same may be vested, shall descend to the legal representatives of the aforesaid Daniel Evans, deceased, agreeable to the intestate laws of this Commonwealth, then in force.

Section II. And be it further enacted by the authority aforesaid 

That within twelve months after the sale aforesaid, the said elders of the Anna Baptist Congregation aforesaid, and the Wardens and Vestry of the Episcopal Church aforesaid, shall cause to be erected a tablet of marble, in their respective Churches, dedicated to the

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley memory of Daniel Evans, deceased, with appropriate inscriptions of his donations to said churches respectively. 

Simon Snyder, Speaker of the House of Representatives 

P. C. Lane, Speaker of the Senate 

Approved, february the tenth in the year of our Lord, one thousand Eight hundred and seven [Governour] 

Thomas McKean."

It is said that the tablet in St. Peter's and the corresponding. tablet placed in the "Anna Baptist" Church are the only ecclesiastical memorial tablets ever set up in the State of 

Pennsylvania by order of the Legislature.

The bequest from Daniel Evans was part of St. Peter's endowment which, though not large, had been growing by little and little over a period of years from legacies left by members of the parish. The story of the endowment fund for a long time is, perhaps, edifying as an example of loose management to be shunned; it is scarcely a source of satisfaction now to the successors of the early Vestries. Certainly without thought of dishonesty-they would have been shocked and justly indignant at any such imputation-the early Vestrymen were curiously simple-minded and careless in their treatment of church funds. Most of them, it would seem, had at best but rudimentary conceptions of the responsibilities of stewardship attaching to the investment and husbanding of bequests. To some of them, apparently, a legacy to the church was a goose-like kind of creature that just took care of itself, automatically came forth semi-annually and laid its "golden egg" of stipulated interest, and would go on doing so till the crack of doom, without further ado on their part. For a considerable period of parish hisory there is no record of any audits save for petty current bills; after the endowment accounts began to be audited, there is not much evidence of any wisdom displayed in placing the principal where it would yield even a barely respectable return. At one time, indeed, the favourite method of investment seemed to be for the Vestry to lend its members or friends the church funds on notes or other insufficient security. In one case, at least, a member of the Vestry borrowed church money in this way and then proved unable to meet his obligations. The Vestry at last voted their con-

sent to compromise with their fellow-member debtor and get back what they could, but there is grave doubt whether they ever recovered any appreciable part of the loan-or any of it at all—and so that much of the principal was frittered away. Other sums, too, evaporated in a comparable manner, thanks to slipshod finance-consciousness and that strange corporate irresponsibility which even now attacks vestries. 

This deplorable laxity and laissez-faire indifference continued with little betterment, or serious and effectual attempt at betterment, until the early years of the twentieth century when a group of more financially-competent Vestrymen resolutely took matters in hand and brought order out of chaos. While they could not retrieve all of what earlier parishioners had bequeathed to the church, and the church had lost, they put the invested funds on a business-like and secure basis of administration and bent intelligent efforts towards building up the endowment. There will be further reference to this timely financial reform and rehabilitation. 

After the death of Parson Clay in 1821, St. Peter's was without a Rector until September 1823, when the Reverend Samuel Crawford Brincklé, who had already been in charge of St. David's for some time previously, gave up his mission work at St. John's, Concord, in Delaware County-which he had carried on till then in conjunction with the Rectorship of Radnor-and became Rector of St. Peter's as well as of St. David's. Into both churches he infused new life and roused the people from the complacent apathy into which they had fallen during the Revolution. His assiduous pastoral care brought results that no amount of mere preaching could ever effect. It will be recalled that nearly a hundred years before, Parson Weyman had written that the people required a priest "to reside amongst them and to visit them from house to house as well as to preach to them for frequent conference with them." 

This cardinal requirement Parson Brincklé fulfilled. 

St. Peter's and St. David's did not exhaust his energy and missionary zeal, for at West Whiteland (now Glen Loch) he started services and eventually, through his untiring

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley labours, St. Paul's Church, West Whiteland (in Chester 

County), was organised in 1828 and a stone church was built and consecrated by Bishop White in 1829. 

To Parson Brincklé, it seems, must be credited the organisation of the Sunday School at St. Peter's. He sought out and prepared numerous candidates for confirmation, and in sundry ways he endeavoured to create new interests that would foster a community of spirit and a sense of corporate pride in both parishes. His solicitude extended also to minor aspects of parochial well-being and seemliness, and tradition points to him as the first to restore the time-honoured custom of decking the churches with evergreens at Christmas. 

It is unfortunate that a rectorship marked by so much spiritual awakening, and noteworthy advancement in other ways also, should have been marred by the first organised vandalism wreaked on the church fabric-vandalism which, we fear, the Parson not only countenanced but also probably instigated as a laudable undertaking indicative of progress. 

In the record book of St. David's Church he wrote: "St. Peter's Church was put in a complete state of repair the present year 1830. It was plastered in the interior, pews made new, and the pulpit placed in the end of the Church. They are making preparations to erect a new vestry-room, to be attached to the north side of the Church."

An unblushing, in fact a prideful, chronicle of wanton defacement!

Whether they ever built the "new vestry-room, to be attached to the north side of the Church" to replace the old Sacristy, we do not know. St. Peter's Vestry Book is singularly silent about any repairs, changes or new building at this time. The Parson 's record, however, is explicit enough and fixes the date when the pulpit was exalted at the expense of the altar; the old high pulpit with its sounding board, and the clerk's desk beneath, removed from their traditional position opposite the big south door; and the high pews, that had been the subject of so many entries in the ancient Vestry Book, destroyed to make way for low-backed pews with doors (facing p. 69), all with panels and mouldings typical of the period. The Parson does not say so in his

entry of destruction, but from the picture (facing p. 53) of the interior, prior to more recent alterations and vandalism, one surmises that at this time also the rounded window-heads were changed to rectangular heads. It was a period when the architecture and traditions of the recent past commanded but little of reverence or appreciation, a period when a nation-wide mania for indiscriminate "improvement" (socalled) and "modernisation" of houses, churches and other buildings well nigh wrecked the best handiwork of former generations and nearly ruined irreparably some of our choicest monuments. While there was much that was unquestionably excellent and æsthetically sound in the tastes and styles of the Greek Revival age, Greek Revival principles had to be applied with reticence by discriminating and scholarly masters of their art; left to interpretation by the untutored multitude, the results were too often characterised by stupidity or vulgarity. We must remember, too, that in spite of whatever merits or refinements we may justly ascribe to the third decade of the nineteenth century-and there were many such-it was also the day of crinolines, high-falutin language, stilted speech and obvious affectatious, oftentimes accompanied by an ill-concealed coarseness and a moribund sense of cultural values.

In September 1832 the railroad was opened through Radnor and Paoli, bringing the outside world immeasurably closer to both churches and potentially broadening their contacts. In December of the same year (1832) Parson 

Brincklé resigned the rectorship of St. Peter's and St. 

David's to become Rector of Grace Church, in Philadelphia, to the great regret of his erstwhile parishioners who felt that his ministry amongst them had been of priceless service. 

His successor as Rector of the two churches was the Reverend Simon Wilmer, of the Diocese of Maryland. Mr. 

Wilmer, however, remained but six months, resigning his charge in July 1833. He is remembered as the first incumbent since the Revolution who used a surplice. Parson 

Brincklé had sometimes donned black gown and bands, but had often conducted service in coattails, like his predecessors. 

The next clergyman was the Reverend William Henry

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley 

Rees, sometime Rector of the Church of St. James the 

Greater at Bristol, in Pennsylvania, but more recently from the Diocese of Maryland. He was the son of John Rees, a farmer in the neighbourhood, so that he was familiar with the conditions of the parishes. Parson Rees was a diligent pastor and, besides maintaining the regular services at the two churches and keeping up a flourishing Sunday School, he held frequent services at Reeseville and "at school houses and other places on other days." He was evidently blessed with exceptional energy for he established and conducted a "select school for boys" at his home on the Lancaster Pike just west of the present village of Berwyn.

It was during his rectorship (in June, 1834) that the Vestry of St. David's appointed a committee "to co-operate with the Vestry of St. Peter's Church relative to purchasing a parsonage." This was evidently a revival of the agitation to purchase a glebe started in 1832 by St. Peter's. St. 

Peter's Vestry Book records a meeting of the congregation on Easter Monday of that year and has the following entry: "... and further that they be a committee to enquire for real estate for sale, that may be purchased by this Church, and report their information to a meeting of the congregation to be called for that purpose."

After the lapse of nearly two years, the joint committee of the Vestries of St. Peter's and St. David's had been unable to arrive at any satisfactory arrangement about buying a Rectory for the two churches; therefore, in April, 1836, they were discharged. At the same time, Parson Rees resigned the rectorship of St. Peter's in order to give his undivided attention to St. David's, and the connection between the two sister parishes was formally severed after 136 years of joint history.

St. Peter's then united with its offshoot, St. Paul's, West 

Whiteland, under the charge of the Reverend William 

Hilton, who became Rector July 24th, 1836, and remained until April 1st, 1841. The disadvantage under which the parish laboured in not having a Rectory and Glebe must

ΙΟΙ have been apparent to the Vestry for the minutes of April, 1841, record: "... Resolved that a committee of Five be appointed to Procure a

Property Suitable for said St. Peter's Church, Namely John G. 

Wersler, Lewis G. Bull, John L. Phillips, Jacob H. Jones & Cromwell Pearce."

Meanwhile, in September 1838, Parson Rees had resigned from St. David's to become Head Master of the 

Mount Vernon Institute, a Church school at West Chester,

Pennsylvania, and St. David's Vestry had called the Reverend Willie Peck from the Diocese of Maryland. Parson 

Peck came to his new parish at the end of October (1838). 

He is said to have been "a man of striking figure, six feet four inches in height, and of strong personality. He had served with General Jackson in the battle of New Orleans, January 8, 1815, as a soldier from Tennessee, and also at 

Toronto under General Pike."

In 1840 St. David's and St. James's, Perkiomen, again joined forces under one Rector but, in October, 1841, Parson Peck's wish "to be more convenient to Radnor Church" impelled him to accept a call to St. Peter's (now vacant by the resignation of Parson Hilton) in conjunction with St. 

David's. St. David's connection with St. James's, Perkiomen, again terminated and St. Peter's and St. David's once more were under the same Rector.

This renewed union of the sister churches stirred St. 

Peter's Vestry to fresh action about a Rectory and Glebe and at Easter, 1842, the committee reported that they had purchased a glebe and rectory for the Church. The property consisted of comfortable buildings, together with fourteen acres of excellent land, a fine spring of water and every convenience of buildings. It was situated at the twenty-mile stone on the Lancaster pike, within sight of the Church.' 

Furthermore, the Vestry minutes of Easter Monday record that Cromwell Pearce, Jacob H. Jones and John L. Phillips "be and are hereby appointed a committee to take charge and superintendence of the Glebe and Rectory recently purchased of Charles I. Davis for the Church, the said committee to provide for the keeping of the fence and buildings in suitable repair, and propose to the Congregation for their acceptance or rejection any improvements on the said Glebe and Rectory."

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley 

The joint arrangement with St. David's lasted only a short time for the Easter Monday minutes in 1844 record a resolution "that a committee of three be appointed to meet a similar committee of St. Paul's Church, West Whiteland, to make arrangements in relation to the selection of a Pastor of said Churches," and state that the committee consisted of John G. Wersler, Daniel Beaver and Thomas Llewellyn. Although the minutes make no record of Parson Peck's resignation from St. 

Peter's, in his history of St. David's Mr. Pleasants says that the final separation of St. Peter's from St. David's took place in 1844. In 1845 Parson Peck resigned the rectorship of St. David's and removed to the Diocese of Mississippi; he died shortly afterwards of yellow fever at New Orleans. 

The next Rector of St. Peter's, the Reverend W. H. 

Woodward, remained until September 1847, and in December of that year the Reverend W. L. Suddards accepted a call to the parish but kept the rectorship for only about three months, resigning to take Grace Church, Philadelphia.

His successor in the rectorship of St. Peter's was the Reverend Thomas W. Winchester, who came in September, 1849, and stayed until his death in February 1858.

In 1850 was published An Historical Sketch of St. Peter's 

Church, Great Valley, Chester County, Pa., written by 

Parson Winchester. In this brief sketch, calling attention to the weakened condition of the old parish, the author says: "The congregation at the present time is small. It may be easily accounted for by saying that the children of the old mother church have gone out from her until she sits like the prophet city that was full of people, solitary. Yet notwithstanding her own desolation, the venerable mother may look out over the land and say, as she sees the church spire pointing heavenward or hears the tolling bell: All these are my children, they have gone out from me, and although I have lost my strength, yet it has only gone out from me to build up the waste places of Zion. 

St. Peter's may truly be called the mother of churches, if the name does not belie the sex. St. Mary's Church [Warwick], of which the Rev. Dr. Bull was so long rector may be said to be a branch from St. Peter's.-Mr. Bull's own family were for years connected with that congregation The members lived distant from the church and as their own neighbourhoods felt the need of divine service, they came together with their neighbours and erected church buildings. This was the case with St. Andrew's, West 

Vincent-some of the steadiest members of old St. Peter's left it when

this new congregation was organised. So was it with St. Paul's, W. Whiteland, and St. Peter's, Phoenixville; and Trinity, West

Chester, and indeed it is not to be doubted that nearly every Episcopal Church in Chester county, has received or drawn some nurture from the venerable congregation, which has given until it is left impoverished for want of a sufficient number of people to fill its courts." 

This parenthood of numerous other parishes in Chester County is a significant fact that ought not to be overlooked. 

It speaks volumes for the vitality of St. Peter's which had then survived adverse conditions and many misfortunes at the time this sketch was written, and was to live on in spite of serious blows and discouragements that were to follow in later years.

Parson Winchester's narrative continues: 

"If any apology were needed for the present small band of worshippers one might be offered in the fact that the old inhabitants have passed away, and many, very many of their posterity have left the graves of their fathers and gone forth into other places to seek other homes. Yet notwithstanding all the combined circumstances to fret away her strength, a good hope is indulged that the day will yet dawn upon her ancient and time-honoured walls; and up from her courts shall swell a multitude of voices in one glad song of praise to Him who first put it into the hearts of the early founders of old St. Peter's to build there on that beautiful spot a temple to 

His name.

St. Peter's has been the recipient of several liberal bequests since its organisation. Those of her children who have drawn comfort from her during life, did not forget the nursing mother in the hour of dissolution. Lands and money have been devised and bequeathed to the congregation some years ago. Some of the money unfortunately, was not well secured and thus a portion of it has been lost from the pious uses which the donors intended. The present vestry to guard against similar misfortunes have very wisely purchased a valuable small property on the Lancaster Turnpike at the 20th mile stone and about 1½ mile south of the church, as a rectory for the minister. 

They have now by repairs and the addition of a new building, provided a most comfortable and pleasant home for their clergyman. 

There is fourteen acres of excellent land, a fine spring of water and every convenience of out door buildings. There is here sufficient land to keep a clergyman with proper industry, although other means may fail him entirely.

This little history of the church of St. Peter's has been compiled mainly from information preserved by Col. Cromwell Pearce, a grandson of Edward Pearce, who it will be observed was one of the wardens of the church. Edward Pearce may indeed be said to be the architect of the building, as he seemed to be the lever which reared the pile. His son Cromwell, who is the father of Col. Pearce, when a boy of 12 years of age remembered, and so stated to Col. 

Pearce, that he was present at the erection of St. Peter's working and assisting his father at the time."

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley 

The Rectory and Glebe, so warmly praised by Parson Winchester, both as a highly satisfactory home for the Rector and as a wise investment on the part of the Vestry, turned out to be a source of anxiety and care, and eventually a very bad investment. Between 1845 and 1853 sundry entries occur in the minute book to indicate the Vestry found the Rectory and Glebe not altogether a source of unmixed joy, to say the least. Finally, on March 28th, 1853, "On motion of Daniel Beaver [one of the committee on whom the care of the Rectory and Glebe repairs had fallen] it was resolved that the delegates of this parish to the Convention be authorized to obtain the authority of that body to dispose of the parsonage & glebe belonging to the parish" and, the Convention evidently having assented to the proposed sale, on November 1st, 1853, it was "resolved that John G. Wersler have authority to dispose of the parsonage & glebe . . for a sum not less than $2300 (provided the charter gives the power so to do)."

It was apparently with a sense of relief that at "a meeting of the vestry of St. Peter's Church, Great Valley, held 

March 22nd A.D. 1854 for the purpose of appointing a person to convey the parsonage & glebe to the purchaser Patrick McGouch John G. Wersler was appointed to convey the said parsonage & glebe to Patrick McGouch the purchaser." 

A note in St. Peter's Parish Visitor (vol. ii, No. 1, 1938) says that the property was sold subject to a mortgage and adds, "That debt to St. Peter's seems never to have been paid." This statement presumably was made on the strength of persistent local tradition; and tradition was fully informed respecting the "unique financing" that once prevailed, a laxity of corporate management to which allusion has already been made.

In June, 1850, the Vestry Book records a resolution "that the thanks of the Vestry of St. Peter's Church are due, and are hereby tendered, to Mrs. Harriet V. Evans for her kindness and liberality in presenting a communion service to said Church." 

This is the third reference in the church records to communion vessels. The first is in 1747 when an entry in the 

Vestry Book, after stipulating Reese Prichard's stipend as

Sexton and fixing his recompense for digging and filling in graves in the churchyard, ends by directing that "He is to Cleane and Sweep ye Church and take Care of ye Vessels thereunto belonging." 

Of what happened to these particular "Vessels thereunto belonging" there is no record. Again, in May 1770, occurs an entry: 

"Recd of the Vestry for a Bason Cup & 

2 plates 

Benjamin Thomas" 

18, 6.

Benjamin Thomas, then a member of the Vestry, was evidently giving a receipt for eighteen shillings, sixpence the Vestry were paying to reimburse him for the purchase of pewter communion vessels and a christening bowl. Since the records made no further mention of sacred vessels, it is reasonable to assume that the pewter set Benjamin Thomas had bought continued in use until Mrs. Evans gave the communion service recorded in 1850.

Although the vessels entrusted to Reese Prichard's care met a nameless fate, we do know what became of the eighteen shillings, sixpennyworth of pewter mentioned in the Vestry minutes of 1770. Discarded at the time of Mrs.

Evans's gift, their subsequent history affords a commentary on the shocking carelessness about church property all too common in the mid-nineteenth century; likewise on the way in which unchecked rumour can distort the merest shred of fact into the most fantastic misstatements.

For a long time there has been current report that St. 

Peter's once possessed a silver communion service given by Queen Anne, but afterwards taken into private custody and that the holders, or their heirs, have unlawfully declined to return it to the parish. This story has found its way into print on more than one occasion; backed by the sanction of cold type, many people fully believe it, plus all the fictitious circumstantial details gradually attached through verbal repetition. That this claim is wholly fabulous-both in respect of any such royal gift ever being made and also its wrongful retention in private hands-will shortly appear. 

If either St. Peter's or St. David's had ever received

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley silver vessels as a mark of royal favour, either directly or through the Venerable Society for the Propagation of the Gospel in Foreign Parts (which would have been the logical channel for such gift because of the Society's responsibility for the maintenance of the missions), we may be certain the gift would have been duly recorded. At the time of Queen 

Anne's death (1714), St. David's had not yet been built nor even named, and the people who afterwards formed St. 

Peter's congregations were still worshipping in the old log chapel (described in the first chapter) or in some temporary substitute, if that primitive structure had already been "burned by design." Queen Anne did not give silver communion services to nameless log chapels in backwoods missions that had no responsible parochial organisation and were oftentimes without the care of a regularly designated missionary priest. Under the circumstances, such gifts would have been inappropriate and embarrassing, even grotesque. 

When well-established congregations had actually built substantial church structures and effected responsibly-functioning parochial organisations-like Christ Church in 

Philadelphia, Trinity Church in New York, or some of the older parishes in Maryland and Virginia—then, and not until then, might they reasonably hope Queen Anne would send them silver chalices and patens by way of encouragement and as tokens of royal interest and beneficence. (Little in that direction, of course, could they look for from 

George I.)

In Queen Anne's time, if the Venerable Society's "Mission of Radnor" had ever received a gift of church plate from any source whatever, St. David's would assuredly have claimed and got it (and rightly), for St. David's was the first part of the "Mission" to build a substantial stone church with a dedicatory patronymic and have functioning Wardens and Vestrymen. And St. David's has never claimed possession of any royal gift of communion plate. St.

Peter's, notwithstanding its separate and large congregation, was simply the "upper part of the parish" and nameless until its people built the present stone church in 1744 and elected their first Wardens and Vestry-thirty years after am~.

Queen Anne's death. There seems no sufficient reason to believe that Anna Regina either could or did employ supernatural agencies to make the parish a posthumous gift of silver communion vessels three decades after her demise, or at any subsequent time. Calm appraisal of the factual conditions and dates just presented ought finally to lay the ghost of that recurrent fairy-story about St. Peter's lost Queen 

Anne silver.

Now, let us examine the slender thread of sober fact that has been stretched and stretched to weave an elaborate tapestry of myth-a myth that eventually sent St. Peter's 

Vestry on a wild-goose chase from which they got only their pains for their trouble. Under date of April 11th, 1887, there is an entry in the Vestry minutes: "The Secretary stated that the old communion service, the property of St. Peter's Church, donated by the Queen Ann Society for the Propagation of the Gospel was now in the possession of Mrs. 

Hugh Davids of Haverford College. 

The following Resolution was adopted.

Resolved that the Vestry deem it desirable that the Old Communion Service, now in the hands of Mrs. Davids, should be restored to the Church, and the Secretary is hereby instructed to communicate with her; to ask whether she would be willing to return the same to the custody of the Vestry."

The Secretary was then Dr. J. Wilks O'Neill. At the next recorded meeting of the Vestry, April 12th, 1890-three years later the minutes say: "Dr. O'Neill, who was appointed to enquire about the old Communion Service, reported that the family of Mrs. Hugh Davids held the Service and refused to give it up, claiming that it was brought over by an Ancestor who was formerly Rector of this Church." 

The following letters and extracts complete the picture and thoroughly elucidate the story. First, a letter from Mrs. 

Richard Davids to Dr. O'Neill, of March 21st, 1887, says: "Yours of the 19th instant just received. My interest in Old St.

Peter's consists in Hugh Davids, an ancestor of my husband's, having been the pastor there many years ago. In the summer of 1863 or 

4 my mother-in-law, the late Mrs. Rebecca Davids visited the church with Mr. L. H. and was shocked to see the old communion service on the floor of a wardrobe or closet. She knew that a font was required and offered to give one in my son's name on the condition that she should receive the service. Mr. H. brought the subject before the Vestry who unanimously accepted the offer. 

Yours sincerely 

Eliza P. Davids."

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley 

This letter is quoted from a transcription; the original is not to be found. Mr. "L.H." was doubtless the Rector, Reverend Samuel Hazlehurst, the transcriber evidently mistaking the "S" for an "L." Hugh Davids (or Davis) was first minister of the Great Valley Baptist Church and was never in charge of St. Peter's. The next "exhibit" is a memorandum for the Vestry minutes, written on a loose sheet of torn paper and never entered in the Vestry Booka common occurrence in the keeping of the church records at that period. It says: "At a meeting of the Vestry of St. Peter's Church in the Great 

Valley on the 28th March, 1864, on motion it was unanimously resolved that the thanks of the Vestry be presented to Mr. Benjamin 

Davids for his liberal and beautiful present to the Church of a Baptismal Font of Picto [sic-it should be Pictou] granite. 

Resolved that the Rector be requested to communicate the above resolution to the members of Mr. Davids's family."

The next link in the chain is a letter of June 9th, 1913, written by Richard W. Davids to Dr. Prevost, then Rector: 

"My dear Dr. Prevost:

I find I can give you but little [information] about the pewter we have that once belonged to St. Peter's Church. 

The plates were made by John Townsend and bear his name and marks with the date 1748; the basin was made by John 

Townsend and R. Reynolds having their combined mark, the former was mentioned as a pewterer in 1769 and later, the latter in 1767. 

I believe the two of the same name were identical. The chalice has no maker's mark.

My records show a note, that the Church minutes mention as of 

May 7, 1770, that two plates, a basin and cup were purchased for 18s 6d. Futhey & Cope's History of Chester County, p. 277, speaks of the communion set presented by Queen Anne not now being in the possession of the Church; but I think I gave you these notes when at our home. I am sending you these data so you can make use of them in your remarks. I shall hope to be at the Church next 

Sunday with the pewter. 

Yours very truly 

Richard W. Davids."

Mr. Davids had been invited to attend the Annual Day celebration and bring the pewter for exhibition. On the outside of the envelope containing this letter is written-apparently in Dr. Prevost's hand-"In whose possession Old St. Peter's pewter set now rightly remains."

Last of all are two extracts from a letter written in

SOUTH SIDE OF ST. PETER'S CHURCH IN 1941

October, 1929, by Dr. Prevost, then Rector emeritus. He says: "He [Mr. Davids] told me he would gladly let me photograph the pieces but could not part with them. His story, which I afterwards corroborated from other sources, was: During the Rectorship of the 

Rev. Mr. Hazelhurst, his mother who was collecting pewter, saw these lying about Old St. Peter's, and she bargained with the Rector that if he would let her have these pieces of pewter, she would give him a stone font. The bargain was made and the stone font is still in Old St. Peter's.

I might mention that the old tradition of a Queen Anne Set belonging to Old St. Peter's, in all my research work I find nothing that Old St. Peter's had such." 

Dr. Prevost thus completely reversed his confident statement about a Queen Anne silver communion service, printed in the diocesan paper fourteen years previously. The "Queen

Anne silver communion service," we see, was transmuted in the lapse of years to plain pewter (fashioned by London pewterers nearly half a century after Anna Regina had passed to her rest); it was rescued from neglect and oblivion by a lady collecting pewter; and St. Peter's Vestry unanimously voted to swap it for a stone font ! 

The year 1850 seems to have been a time of "taking account of stock," so to speak, pulling loose ends together and putting things more or less in order. Besides acquiring a new communion service by gift from Mrs. Evans, publishing Parson Winchester's history of the parish, arranging for new horse sheds and making sundry repairs, the Vestry decided it was expedient to make some regulation about burials in the churchyard. An entry in the Vestry Book reads: "Whereas Strangers and others who have not contributed to the support of the Minister, or assisted in erecting the wall around the graveyard of St. Peter's Church, Great Valley, have been in the practice of burying the bodies of their Friends, or acquaintances, in said graveyard, and it is right and proper that those Persons should assist in defraying the expenses aforesaid, Therefore Resolved that all Persons who do not regularly contribute to the support of the Minister, or who have not contributed the sum of $2.00 to the 

Repairing of the Church and the wall around the Church and Grave Yard, shall pay for the privilege of burying their Relations, Friends or acquaintances in the Grave Yard the following sums, except in the cases of such Persons who have not the ability to do so." 

Then follows a schedule of burial tariffs: 

20 years or over.... $5.00 

15 ΙΟ to 20 years. $4.00 99 99 99 15 $3.00 

5 years to 10 years.. $2.00 

Under 5 years..... $1.00

IIO 

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley 

The required sum was to be "paid at the time of interment"; the Vestry did not intend to have the church imposed upon by "dead heads" in a double sense. Perhaps Judge

Moore would have disapproved this measure, too, had he been alive, but the Vestry tempered the rule with consideration for them that "have not the ability" to pay and left broad discretionary powers to the Warden or Vestryman to whom application was made to judge of the applicant's capacity to meet the fee.

In yielding to their urge to furbish up, the Vestry now began to let their zeal outrun wisdom. In 1851 they passed a resolution "that the church be repainted throughout," which was all right enough, but in April, 1852, they gave way to a deplorable modernising mania and resolved "that a committee of two be appointed to ascertain the cost of erecting a vestry room 12 ft by 14 ft [a wretched little kennel!] at the east end of the Church."

The mere suggestion of appending such a structural monstrosity to deface a really fine and well proportioned building shows to what a low ebb architectural appreciation, reverence for the past, and all sense of æsthetic fitness had fallen in the middle of the nineteenth century. Whether anything further than "ascertaining the cost of erecting" this brutal blister of uglification was ever done the records do not say. It really didn't much matter for worse was to follow.

In 1856 the Vestry, in corporate ignorance, folly and vulgarity, perpetrated their crowning act of criminal stupidity and did all that in them lay to ruin the church structure. 

Of this sorry episode Dr. Sachse writes:* 

"The Vestry of the year (1856) under the inspiration of the Rector and some people who had but recently come into the parishpeople who forced themselves into prominence (for the time being) in the church for whatever prestige was to be derived therefrom, but had no ties of former associations with, or sentiment for, the old building or its history-at once commenced to agitate remodelling, as they called it (destruction would have been more appropriate). 

So the venerable grey walls were pebble-dashed and whitewashed.

The old vestry [or Sacristy] to the north was torn down, weakening the church walls so much as to require the three iron rods that now disfigure the building to keep the walls in place; the present exten- 

*Sachse MS.: in the Collections of the Church Historical Society, Philadelphia.

III 

sion at the east end was then erected, badly marring the original symmetry of the building

The picturesque round-top windows with their iron-bound oaken shutters were replaced by the present square unsymmetrical openings; the large chancel window was walled up. 

The south gallery was torn out; the moss-covered shingle roof was replaced by the present red tin eyesore; the now squatty square windows were hung with green slat blinds; the pure white of the walls and vaulted ceiling gave place to the dark dull grey that now, one might almost say, repels the stranger as he enters the church. 

The result of this remodelling of the venerable church is that no one, knowing or having seen the original building, would for a moment recognise it as 'St. Peter's of ye Great Valley.'

The effect of this desecration was that some of the older members, who for years had the good of the parish at heart and had borne the burden and heat of the day, were driven away or lost their interest. Those who were the cause of the improvements (?), as might have been foretold, left almost as quickly as they came."

Dr. Sachse appears quite positive about the change in the shape of the windows at this time; nevertheless, there is considerable reason to believe that this particular item of change was made at an earlier date, as already indicated. 

In any event, the wanton disfigurement of 1856 was farreaching enough to justify every bit of Dr. Sachse's bitter resentment and indignation.

For the indignities heaped on the old church, Dr. Sachse lays part of the blame on Parson Winchester. Let us hope, for the sake of that gentleman's credit, that he was not culpable to the extent Dr. Sachse believed him to be. Just how much influence the Parson could exert at that period is a matter of question. At this juncture the State Legislature granted an amendment to the parish charter considerably reducing the number of Vestrymen. This change gave an opportunity for two or three determined and headstrong members to dominate the whole body (including the Rector) and force action as they wished. Moreover, it had been the long-standing tradition for St. Peter's Vestry to order the temporal affairs of the parish as they deemed best and, oftentimes, to assume the attitude of an employer towards the clergyman. This condition becomes understandable when we remember the history of the parish-the long periods during which the people, represented by the Vestry, had to shoulder all the responsibility of keeping the parish going when there was no Rector and they had to depend on such

IIThe Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley supplies as they could get; and, when there was a Rector, having usually to share him with one or two other parishes, so that it not infrequently happened he could come to them but once or twice a month. The spirit of independence and self-sufficiency thus engendered tends to minimise the force of clerical counsel in matters temporal. Parson Winchester may, indeed, have instigated the alterations and mutilations, as Dr. Sachse believed (and Dr. Sachse lived nearer the time of this calamity than we)-he may even have urged them strongly-but all circumstances considered, one cannot help feeling that the major blame for the vandalism must be laid squarely on the shoulders of the Vestry. Furthermore, Parson Winchester's relation to the parish in 1856 is not quite clear and, therefore, his ability or right to exert influence one way or the other may be open to question. He had resigned the rectorship in 1854 and there is no record to show that he withdrew his resignation. Although he continued to minister in the parish until his death, in 1858, it is impossible to tell from the records whether he remained as Rector or merely as a paid (and not very munificently paid) locum tenens.

Parson Winchester tendered his resignation at a Vestry meeting on April 20th, 1854. At the same meeting a resolution was recorded in the minutes "that Daniel Beaver & John Tucker be a committee of this vestry to meet a similar committee of the Vestry of St. Paul's parish, to enquire into the expediency of increasing the salary of the Rector of the two parishes."

This was immediately after the sale of the Rectory and Glebe to Patrick McGouch and the Rector, if not actually turned out of doors and homeless at the moment, was at least under the necessity of finding another dwelling which the church was not paying for. Up to this time the arrangement by which St. Paul's, West Whiteland, shared the same 

Rector with St. Peter's had continued in effect; when St. 

Peter's sold its Rectory and Glebe, the Vestry wished St. 

Paul's to share the necessarily increased obligation for clerical support.

Subsequently the Vestry passed a resolution "that if the above Committee cannot make an agreement with the above parish, then this church will retain Rev. Mr. Winchester." 

This is followed by an entry in the Vestry Book: "The committee appointed at the last meeting to confer with St. 

Paul's parish for the purpose of increasing the salary of the Rector, Report that they could not make an agreement." 

Both the foregoing entries are undated. 

At a meeting of St. Peter's Vestry, October 2nd, 1854, it was unanimously resolved "that the Revd Mr. Winchester be invited to officiate in this parish every Sunday morning, & at any other occasions that he may think proper; & that for each time that he officiates, he shall be paid five dollars by the treasurer of this parish. 

Resolved that this agreement terminates on the 31st December 1854."

As both dated and undated entries are oftentimes written in the old Vestry Book apparently at random, there is no way of knowing certainly whether this last entry preceded or followed the two undated entries in point of time. In any event, it looks as though St. Peter's Vestry were engaging a locum tenens for three months, not treating with a Rector.

The relationship appears ill-defined and anomalous. 

Nor does the next entry on the subject clear matters very much. At a meeting holden in June, 1856 "for the purpose of taking into consideration, the propriety of a union with St. Peter's Church, Phoenixville, it was resolved-that we agree to relinquish for the relief of Rev. Mr. 

Winchester, our present rector, as well as our own parish, a portion of the services; viz. that morning and evening services be held alternately at each church, at the sum of $300. a year paid him quarterly, by each of the said churches; the year to commence on the 1st July 1856."

This resolution does speak of the Parson as "our present rector," but in the light of the previous excerpts from the Vestry minutes, and the fact that we have no record of the Parson's withdrawal of his resignation, his exact statuswhether Rector or locum tenens-is difficult to establish. 

For the remaining two years of his life, whatever his technical relation may have been, Parson Winchester continued to

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley minister to St. Peter's in the Great Valley, St. Peter's, Phoenixville, and St. Paul's, West Whiteland. When he died, in 1858, he was buried in the churchyard of St. Paul's 

West Whiteland. 

An entry in the Vestry minutes says it was resolved on 

December 1st, 1856, "that work or materials contributed to the repair of the church buildings or grounds shall not be considered an equivalent to the payment of the sums chargeable for making interments in the graveyard according to rules made Apr. 8th, 1850."

While the prevention of free burials in the churchyard was obviously the purpose of this resolution, the most interesting thing about it is its intimation that the members of the congregation contributed material and labour to the remodelling then undertaken. This doubtless materially reduced the actual cash outlay required.

On April 5th, 1858, the Vestry appointed a committee to confer with the "vestry of St. Peter's Church, Phoenixville, in relation to calling a rector." A little later, with the concurrence of the respective vestries, they "agreed to call Rev. 

Henry B. Bartow in place of Rev. Thomas W. Winchester (dec'd)." At the Vestry meeting of April 14th (the committee to consult with the Phoenixville committee had evidently acted promptly), it was "reported that Rev. H. B. Bartow declined to accept the call, on account of the insufficiency of the salary-It was resolved that the vestry should elect another gentleman as rector of the parish, whereupon Rev. Samuel Hazelhurst was unanimously elected; & a committee appointed to notify him of his election, which he accepted." 

Parson Hazlehurst remained Rector of St. Peter's-in-the- 

Great Valley and St. Peter's, Phoenixville, until 1868. It was during his rectorship that the Vestry swapped the old communion service for a stone font. It was also during his rectorship that the first mention of music occurs in the church records. On April 30th, 1866, it was "Resolved by the Vestry that Twenty five dollars be appropriated to Miss Eliza S. Jones performer on the Melodian."

When the "Melodian" was acquired and placed in the church, we are not told. We can surmise, however, that

what prompted the Vestry to procure a melodeon (even if they didn't know how to spell its name) was that St. David's had got one a few years before. What supplied the instrumental accompaniment prior to this, if they had any, we are not told. It is not at all unlikely they had a bass viol or a 'cello (often called a "chapel bass"), just as they had at 

St. David's where Jesse Brooke played his bass viol in church to accompany the singing until about 1855 when they got a melodeon. 

We know that Miss Eliza S. Jones got her "twenty five 

Dollars for her severses performing on" the melodeon; there is an entry in the Vestry minutes to that effect. Let us hope Miss Jones never had an embarrassing experience like that which befel one of her feminine contemporaries while fulfilling the same duty at the Union Church, at Audubon, just across the river. The melodeon, be it said for the benefit of those not familiar with the creature, was a small, keyed wind-instrument "the bellows of which were operated by an uncovered crank-gearing worked by the player with a foot-treadle. On the occasion alluded to, the first verse of the hymn was successfully performed, but as the second verse progressed a mysterious paralysis seemed gradually to overcome the instrument; it struggled bravely, but fitfully, and in the seventh line silence fell upon it, much to the wonder of the amazed congregation. Silence was broken in a few seconds by the whispered voice of the organist-perfectly audible, however-which said [to a male relative in the choir]: 'My dear, my petticoat is caught in the treadle! Get it out!' The good lady's lingerie was cut out with a penknife and unwound, and the hymn brought to a triumphant conclusion."

Though quickly achieving popular favour, the melodeon was somewhat of a novelty about the middle of the nineteenth century; perhaps that is why the scribe in the Vestry Book indulged in arbitrary spelling.

It was the year following the advent of the melodeon that William M. Sullivan, Jr., was elected to the Vestry. Mr. 

Sullivan was a man of dominating temperament and for more than thirty years proceeded to dominate the Vestry, the congregation and often the parson. In modern parlance he would have been called a "lay Pope." Doubtless he had 

*Portrait of a Colonial City: H. D. Eberlein and C. V. D. Hubbard, p. 566.

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley the interests of the parish sincerely at heart, but his methods of manifesting his solicitude during the period while he "ruled the roost" were often disconcerting to his fellowmembers of the congregation and sometimes disruptive. His personality comes so conspicuously to the fore during the remaining years of the nineteenth century that it is well to mention his membership in the Vestry at this point. 

When Parson Hazlehurst's rectorship ended in 1868, he was followed by the Reverend A. E. Tortat who remained 

Rector until 1873. Resigning in ill health, he was succeeded by the Reverend Edmund Christian whom the Vestry called for six months, apparently "on approval." The minutes record that "It was resolved by the vestry of this Church to accept Mr. Christian as pastor of this Church for the term of Six months from April 1st 1873 Ending October First 1873 at the usual Salary $500.00 pr. year.” 

One can understand a "priest-in-charge" or a locum tenens being engaged for six months, but Rectors or pastors are not "hired" by the year or half-year. This extraordinary proceeding can be explained, perhaps, though not excused, by the fact that union again with St. Paul's, West Whiteland, seems to have been under consideration. Just why, where or how trouble was brewing it is impossible to say at this distance of time and lacking specific data-one can hazard only guesses-but, at any rate, on August 30th Mr. Christian wrote the Vestry requesting a testimonial respecting character and conduct to satisfy the Bishop and Standing Committee of the Diocese. The answer to this request appears in the Vestry minutes of two days later, September. Ist, 1873, which speak for themselves. Mr. Sullivan was then 

Secretary of the Vestry, so the entries are his. "The following communication [the request for a testimonial] from Revt Edmund Christian Rector was Presented and ordered to be placed on the minutes.

After due consideration of the communication it was unanimously Resolved That it is to the interest and advantage of this Church to again unite with St. Paul's Valley Church said Church having Expressed their wish for such reunion.

Resolved that in view of this union with St. Paul's Church together with the fact that St. Peter's Church is not prospering under
