30th inst."

Thus was the "six-months-on-approval" Rector summarily "bounced." The Parish Visitor (vol. ii, No. 1, 1838), in an historical address by Father Yeakel (then Priest-in-Charge of St. Peter's) on occasion of the Annual Day celebration, notes that "Mr. Sullivan had no time for this clergyman.'

Mr. Sullivan's addiction to that abomination, the split infinitive, recurring again and again in the minutes, shows it was obtruding itself to the detriment of the King's English long before Mr. Hoover so successfully popularised it. 

The next priest to hold the rectorship of St. Peter's-inthe-Great Valley and St. Paul's, West Whiteland, was the 

Reverend William R. Stockton. He came in September, 1873, and remained until October, 1878. To quote the 

Parish Visitor again (vol. ii, No. 1), "Mr. Sullivan met his match in Mr. Stockton who dared to find fault with the conducting of the financial affairs of St. Peter's. So he got into trouble . . .”

Let the Vestry minutes continue the narrative. On March 

19th, 1877, at what was professed, presumably, to be a general congregational special meeting, the minutes say: "We the vestry have consulted togather in regard to the one Thousand dollars held by the County of Chester and concluded to still leave the same in the hands of the commissioners for another year at 5 pr cent the rector of said Church W. R. Stockton whished to have the money but we the vestry of said church think the investment is not satisfactory.

Therefore we the undersigned resolved to leave the same as it is. [Here occur five signatures, one of which is that of Wm. M. Sullivan, Secretary.]

The business of the meeting was conducted with peace and harmony in due form and adjourned . . .” 

Now, evidently, the "fat was in the fire." The minutes for the annual meeting of the congregation for the election of 

Vestrymen, April 5th, 1877, record: "In accordance with a letter or request by W. R. Stockton, rector of said church, dated Phoenixville, April 3, 1877, charging the

Vestry with not having notified the congregation of a special meeting held March 19, 1877, also of doing business in a loose and illegal manner, Charging the Vestry with having loaned money without

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley proper authority and on insufficient security, after the reading of the letter it was resolved to lay the same under the table, after which John Supplee and David R. Young was appointed to audit the accounts of the Treasurer, which was done and found correct. ..." 

Laying the Rector's letter of protest "under the table" did not quell the commotion and the next indication from the minutes is that the Rector had got the upper hand, for a time at least. At a special meeting, November 23rd, 1877,

Parson Stockton was in the chair and a resolution was adopted .. first that no money should be loaned belonging to the church, only on real estate, first mortgage . . . . ."

This meeting, also, we are assured in the minutes was conducted "with peace and harmony"-an assurance of which one is inclined to be skeptical. If peace and harmony existed on the surface, it was not for long. The storm broke out afresh at a special meeting of the Vestry, called at a most unusual hour on the morning of June 10th, 1878, holden "to consider the matter of Rector, wether Mr. W. R. Stockton be continued as Rector or wether it would be advisable to make any change for the good of the church. the meeting was called to order at 5½ A.M. Vestrymen present 

John Supplee, Benjamin Steward, Samuel Acker, John Rambo and 

Wm. M. Sullivan. it was unanimously resolved to by all the members present that the Revt W. R. Stockton be Notified that his services will not be required at St. Peter's Church, Great Valley, after July 1st 1878. from this date his pay will cease. it was moved and seconded that W. M. Sullivan be a committee to notify said Revt W. R. Stockton of his removal and the Proceedings of the meeting. adjourned at 6½ Oclock in peace and harmony." 

Thus was a second Rector unceremoniously "bounced." 

Query: Did anyone at the time think of dubbing Mr. Sullivan "Billy Bounce"? There is no record of the meeting between "Billy Bounce" and the "Revt" Mr. Stockton.

Parson Stockton was not the kind of man to be intimidated; notwithstanding the discontinuance of his "pay" on July 1st, he appears to have stayed on until he got ready to leave in October. After that, he continued on as Rector of St. 

Peter's, Phoenixville.

After Parson Stockton's departure, the parish was with-

out a rector for some years and depended upon temporary supplies. From 1880 to 1882 the Reverend DeWitt C. 

Loop was in charge of the parish; then the Reverend 

Thomas J. Taylor followed him, ministering to both St.

Peter's-in-the-Great Valley and St. Paul's, West Whiteland. 

In 1884 the Reverend Henry J. W. Allen became Rector, serving both St. Peter's and St. Paul's. Prior to this, frequent dependence on clerical supplies and consequent lack of pastoral care had seriously weakened the parish, and this weakness had become aggravated to such an extent by the removal of parishioners to other neighbourhoods that in 1885 the Convocation of Chester appointed a Committee "to examine by personal inspection into the needs of those parishes requiring aid from the Board of Missions, or which are in a dormant state, and report the same as soon as possible to the Presiding Officer." St. Peter's was one of the parishes causing special anxiety. 

With reference to St. Peter's, the Committee reported: 

it has been for many years practically a church without a congregation. The succession to the farm estates in its neighbourhood has not yielded a fresh supply of churchmen, and at present the Presbyterian and Methodist bodies command the support of the immediate vicinage. A dwindling congregation is an evil which tends to exaggerate itself. Of those who remain some, beginning to feel a weakening of the tie of fellowship, grow lukewarm and depart to satisfy elsewhere a craving for a more vigorous church life. The church, however, still has a faithful few, who will not desert their post of duty.. 

Your committee, while saddened by the want of interest among the people, which must include many families who in former times were worshippers at this church, and fully aware that grave causes have contributed to produce the present condition of things, and that the remedy is not easy to find, yet are inclined to believe that the interests of this ancient church might be very materially promoted by a simple expedient which, in fact, may be considered part and parcel of our civilisation. It is, in one wordadvertising-letting it be known what and where St. Peter's is; publishing its existence, its history, its site, or the way to get at it. 

Being within a radius of twenty miles from the city, it is within easy access of an increasing number of people from the city-churchmen who have or will have their summer homes-sometimes permanent homes-in the country adjacent. It has come to the knowledge of your committee during the past summer that either the existence of the church or the way to reach it is a mystery to many who would gladly give it the aid of their presence at its services." 

In their diagnosis of the parish ailments, the committee might have named quite plainly, without fear of contradic-

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley tion, one major cause of trouble-the atmosphere of bickering, friction and unrest that had prevailed in the parish for more than a decade, in large measure (if not entirely) attributable to the arbitrary and imperious behaviour of one of the Vestrymen who, whatever may have been the goodness of his intentions, was exceedingly injudicious in translating them into action. 

One of the "faithful few who" would "not desert their post of duty" was Mrs. Elizabeth Shee, a granddaughter of

Richard Richison, one of the original Vestry in 1745. In spite of her advanced age, she had conscientiously come all the way from West Chester with great regularity, oftentimes performing alone the duties of an altar guild, and always attended by her faithful dog. At her death, she left a tall-case clock and other furniture to the church, and from her remaining slender means bequeathed a substantial portion to the endowment fund, to be paid to the church upon the death of her dog. In other wards, the dog had a life interest in the income to ensure proper care as long as it lived. Mrs. Shee was buried (1886) in the churchyard, as she had expressly desired and, some years later, when her faithful old dog died too, it is said to have been buried at the feet of its mistress. The rumour of a canine burial in the churchyard seems to have scandalised some unco-conventionally-minded persons. But why should the dog not have been buried there? It was one of God's creatures, as well. as we, and it certainly possessed some God-like virtues that humans might do well to cultivate more assiduously than they often do. 

Parson Allen laboured consistently over his two parishes, but the conditions at St. Peter's grew more and more discouraging. The following entry from the Vestry minutes of

April 25th, 1895, disclosed the state of parish decay and the termination of Mr. Allen's rectorship: "At a special meeting of the Vestry of St. Peter's Church, Great 

Valley, held April 25th 1895 there being present Henry Curry, Devault, Benjamin Stewart, Valley Forge, George Fulmer, Devault, Edward Furlong, David Young, Pickering, Wm. M. Sullivan, Warren Tavern, Rev. H. J. W. Allen Rector in the chair.

The question of the cleaning of the Church was taken into con-

sideration, the walls want roofing and dashing, the out-buildings want renewing, after an examination by the Vestry it was unanimously agreed upon that Rev. H. J. W. Allen's services be dispensed with for the present and do the necessary repairs. David R. Young, Wm. M. Sullivan & Geo. Fulmer were elected committee on repairs. 

On motion the meeting adjourned."

Thus was a third Rector "bounced." The Church was closed and the income was to be applied to repairs! This dictatorial effrontery leaves us gasping in sheer bewilderment. 

The closing of St. Peter's was an high-handed and illegal proceeding in which the Vestry, dominated by one man, had usurped powers that did not belong to them. So far as there are any records, there is nothing to indicate that the congregation were consulted, and the Vestry acted flatly in opposition to the Bishop's expressed wishes. The shutting-up of the church so shocked the people of St. Peter's Church, Phoenixville, that the Phoenixville Rector and Vestry petitioned the Bishop to entrust the care of St. Peter's-in-the- 

Great Valley to them. Bishop Whitaker's reply to the 

Vestry of Phoenixville says: "October 25, 1895. 

I am in receipt of yours of the 21st enclosing preamble and resolutions adopted by the Vestry of St. Peter's Church, Phoenixville. 

I regret as sincerely as you can that services at St. Peter's, Great 

Valley, have been discontinued, but it is evident from your communication that your Vestry has entirely misapprehended the reasons for such discontinuance. You speak of it as being a 'mistake to close up country churches in order to concentrate efforts upon city parishes.' 

Such a course would certainly be a great mistake, but there is nothing of the kind in the present case. 

St. Peter's has not been closed for the purpose of diverting money or energy towards any other point, but because the Vestry determined to close it, and took that step in spite of all my efforts to the contrary. 

St. Peter's has received no missionary money for years, and the services that have been maintained have been paid for from the income of invested funds. 

In reply to your petition to the Bishop of the Diocese to entrust St. Peter's Church, Great Valley, to the care of the Rector and 

Vestry of St. Peter's, Phoenixville, I can only say that it [St. Peter'sin-the-Great Valley] is a corporation with the same rights and privileges which other organised parishes possess. 

I am glad to know of the interest taken by your Vestry in the work of the Church, and thank you for the offer you have made for the maintenance of services at St. Peter's [Great Valley]. I am not at present in a position to accept that offer, but it would be most gratifying to me to have some arrangement made by which services

IThe Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley may be resumed at St. Peter's and the church restored to its former position and usefulness in the diocese. I shall be glad at any time to receive any suggestions as to the manner in which this end may be brought about; and am, Very faithfully yours

O. W. Whitaker" 

There are no entries in the minute-book of the Vestry between April 25th, 1895, and September 24th, 1899. The church was closed and deserted for four years.

CHAPTER VI Epilogue ROM reading the minutes of the special Vestry meeting of September 24th, 1899 (the first recorded minutes since April 25th, 1895, when 

Parson Allen was "dismissed" and the church closed), one might readily imagine the parish had been functioning uninterruptedly and not been dormant for more than four years. In spite of this apparently almost casual tone of the minutes, however, one can read between the lines that the entries reflect a great deal that had been happening in the meantime to resuscitate the old parish. 

The scandal of the closed church, and the knowledge of how and why it had been closed, had provoked interest in various quarters, along with a determination to reopen St. 

Peter's and a resolve to repair, so far as possible, the incalculable damage wrought by suspending in 1895 the life of the parish. The result of these influences had been to enlist the active support of the Reverend Edgar Cope, Rector of 

St. Simeon's Church, in Philadelphia, and to acquire three new Vestrymen, men whose personality, broad outlook and devotion to the welfare of the Church at large held great promise for the successful revival of healthy parochial existence. The two remaining members of the old Vestry were the now venerable David R. Young and William M.

Sullivan "who held on to the old Minute Book" (v. Parish 

Visitor, vol. ii, No. 1, 1938); the new Vestrymen were R. 

Francis Wood, William R. Reeves—of St. Peter's Church, Phoenixville-and John Morton Fultz, who proved a most efficient Secretary.

In the reorganisation, Mr. Cope was elected Rector. In

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley accepting the call, he agreed to be present himself at St. 

Peter's one Sunday and one-week-day in every month and to have one of his curates present the other Sundays and one day in each week during the month. The Reverend Robert 

Windley was the curate who served chiefly at St. Peter's during Mr. Cope's rectorship and it is recorded of him that "he did most of the ministering and was a real visitor in the parish." Also, the finances of the parish were thoroughly gone into and put in sound condition, with proper safeguards and control in responsible and competent hands. 

Likewise, the status of the parish in the Diocese was restored and St. Peter's again had representation in the Convention.

As an "outward and visible sign" of St. Peter's renewed lease of life, the sundry repairs undertaken four years previously were finally completed and, at the same time, other changes then deemed desirable were carried out. It is a source of much regret that in making these changes Parson Cope and his collaborators seem to have "had no reverence for antiquity" and very questionable architectural taste although, in justice be it said, the current architectural taste and knowledge of architectural propriety left much to be desired. The pulpit, it is true, was removed from its usurped position at the east end of the church (where it had been put in the alterations of 1830), and the altar was restored to its rightful place of dignity. Also, the semi-circular heads of the windows were restored to correspond with the head of the south door (which fortunately had never been altered). Otherwise, though doubtless actuated by the best intentions in the world, they succeeded in completing the ruination of the church fabric, a process begun in 1830 and ruthlessly continued in 1856 with deplorable ignorance and utter disregard for either ecclesiological tradition or architectural fitness.

Although the restoration of the semi-circular window heads was entirely commendable, the application of external brown stucco voussoirs, like eyebrows topping the window openings, was both meaningless and hideous, and the glazing -four huge panes of glass to a window-was barbarous

Epilogue 

125 and altogether destructive of scale. The Vestry and Sunday 

School addition of 1856 (when the east window was blocked up) was allowed to remain at the east of the church, which was a blunder; and the erection of the Parish House (for which the corner-stone was laid October 12th, 1901), tacked on to the 1856 addition, did not help the general composition. If there was any part of the old gallery still left in the church after the 1856 wave of destruction, it disappeared at this time. Another act of vandalism was to throw out the entire lot of pews-though not the original structures, the 1830 pews were well designed and reasonably in keeping with the church-and substitute offensive taffycoloured seats of grotesque lines. The net outcome of the misguided "improvers'" endeavours-from 1830 to 1901was to bequeath a horrid legacy entailing on their successors a full-sized task of restoration. 

One of the worst things the enterprising "modernisers" did was to cover up the old stone paving with wooden flooring. In making this "improvement," they completely destroyed the old paving and spread a thick layer of cement in which they laid the floor joists. In doing this they obliterated all traces of the graves of the Lady Rebecca Moore (Judge Moore's mother), Margaret Moore, Robert Powel and others. That the wooden floor was a part of the lamentable programme seems indubitable. In the summer of 1942 the sexton told the authors that as a young man he well remembered the old stone floor and gravestones and that the wooden floor was laid when the 1830 pews were ripped out; he was not born till long after 1856, so that this act of vandalism cannot be blamed on the "improvers" of that date.

With the reopening of the church and the renewal of pastoral effort, parish life revived and began to prosper. 

Internal agitation, however, soon broke the calm. At a meeting of the Vestry, immediately following the annual meeting of the congregation for the election of Vestrymen, April 13th, 1901, a petition was presented from the congregation (with 67 signatures) for a "resident Rector." This petition was apparently inspired and engineered by Mr.

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley 

Sullivan; the summarised parish chronicle published in the 

Parish Visitor (vol. ii, No. 1, 1938) says "the signatures of communicants and others have been questioned." On May 

4th, at a continuation of the meeting adjourned from April 

13th, the Vestry minutes record: 

upon motion "After considerable discussion of this matter of Mr. Wood, duly seconded by Mr. Young, it was 

Resolved: That in the judgement of the Vestry, the time has not yet come to elect a resident rector.

After the above motion had been passed, Mr. Sullivan withdrew from the meeting."

Before the resolution was passed, Mr. Sullivan had made a vigorous speech in defense of the petition. The parish chronicle, just quoted, says: "Then Mr. Sullivan left the meeting 'as mad as a bull' and drove away, leaving Mrs. Sullivan to walk home" to Warren Tavern, several miles distant.

Mr. Sullivan was right in his advocacy of a resident rector, but erred in judging the time ripe for taking that step and he was very injudicious in his manner of trying to carry his point. He no longer controlled the Vestry and was not in a position to eject the Rector. Besides, Mr. Cope was not the sort of person to submit to ejectment. Both Mr. Cope and his curate (Mr. Windley), who chiefly ministered to the parish, fully recognised the difficulties and disadvantages of "long-distance ministrations" but, all things considered, it was the best arrangement that could then be hoped for. As a sidelight on the problem of country transportation at that time-the church was three miles from the railway stationthe minutes of the May 4th meeting, immediately following the note of Mr. Sullivan's abrupt departure, record that "Mr. Wood kindly offered to lend Mr. Windley his bicycle to enable him to get to and from the Church. 

It was moved by Mr. Wood, and duly seconded and carried, that Mr. Windley be authorised to hire a team on rainy Sundays for three months to take him to and from the Church" to the station.

No one can justly impugn Mr. Sullivan's sincere devotion to St. Peter's and his good intentions for the welfare (as he

Epilogue 

127 saw it) of the parish; he laboured under the handicap of an impetuous and dominating temperament, and his concern for the material condition of the church property and corporate funds often caused him to lose sight of spiritual conditions. and needs. Impatient of opposition and not readily tolerant of any point of view that differed from his own, he nevertheless ultimately accepted majority decisions and continued to work for the church's good with those who had opposed him. Even after his explosive departure, "mad as a bull," from the Vestry meeting of May 4th, he not only remained on the Vestry but also served on the building committee for the new Parish House. When he died, after a long illness, in January, 1903, the Vestry passed a resolution (spread upon the minutes), the complete sincerity of which cannot be questioned. It reads:

The Vestry desire to place upon their minutes a record of the death of their fellow-member, William M. Sullivan, which occurred on the 23rd day of January, 1903, after an illness of many months' duration. Mr. Sullivan became a member of the Vestry in 1867, and 

Accounting Warden in 1882 and served continuously until his death. 

During this long period, he gave much time and labour to the service of St. Peter's, and the present good condition of the Church building is chiefly due to his intelligent care and devotion to the work. His care of the endowment fund also has resulted in its being turned over to his successors intact, notwithstanding the long years of depression during which so many securities were diminished in value.

His responsibilities as a Vestryman and steward of the church property he took seriously and was conscientious in the discharge of what he conceived to be his duties. Better that he should have been insistent and annoyingly aggressive at times than complacently indifferent as too many of our Vestrymen and Church folk too often are in both city and country parishes.

In May, 1903, Mr. Cope resigned the rectorship of St. 

Peter's. The Vestry minutes read: 

"The Vestry, in accepting the resignation of the Rev. Edgar

Cope, as Rector of St. Peter's Church in the Great Valley, desire to express to Mr. Cope and to put upon the Parish Records, their deep appreciation of his services to St. Peter's during the past three and a half years.

To him are mainly due the reopening of the Church and the maintenance of services there, and the gathering in of the congregation which now worships within its walls.

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley 

The placing of the endowment fund upon a safe and lasting foundation was also largely owing to him. 

And to him also is owing the chief credit for the erection of the Sunday School building and for obtaining the funds to pay off the indebtedness incurred in this work. 

We wish for Mr. Cope a long and happy life, and every success in the great work in which he is engaged." 

Following Mr. Cope's resignation, the Reverend W. H. 

Burbank, then Rector of St. Peter's Church, Phoenixville, became Rector also of St. Peter's-in-the-Great Valley and remained in charge of the parish until his removal to Kansas in 1908. Immediately after that the Vestry invited the 

Reverend Horace A. Walton, Rector of the Church of the 

Good Samaritan at Paoli, to accept in addition the rectorship of St. Peter's. Mr. Walton, however, felt unable to assume the care of a second parish and declined the offer. 

Bishop Whitaker then suggested that St. Peter's should again unite with St. Paul's Church, West Whiteland (Glen 

Loch), in calling a rector to take charge of both parishes. 

Through the negotiations made at the Bishop's instance, the two parishes jointly called the Reverend James Comfort to be their Rector. He accepted the call but his rectorship was of brief duration; he resigned in October, 1909.

The Vestries of St. Peter's and St. Paul's then united in calling to the joint rectorship the Venerable Jules Louis 

Prevost, M.D., sometime Archdeacon of Alaska. Dr. Pre vost accepted the call and entered upon his duties as Rector of the two parishes in the spring of 1910. Dr. Prevost's rectorship lasted until 1924 and was a blessing to both parishes, to whose people he greatly endeared himself. It was a period of steady growth at both St. Peter's and St. 

Paul's and there was marked progress both temporal and spiritual. True to St. Peter's missionary tradition, Dr. 

Prevost in conjunction with his parochial duties carried on missionary work among the Italian quarrymen and their families in the nearby village of Cedar Hollow and also in other places.

It was early in Dr. Prevost's rectorship that the question of enlarging the churchyard was first mooted and a committee of the Vestry was appointed to explore the possi-

Epilogue 

129 bilities. It was not, however, until 1918 that anything definite was accomplished in that direction. Then the Charles 

Warner Company and the Knickerbocker Lime Company (controlling the adjacent limestone quarries) paid the church $8,000.00 and offered some additional acreage for the privilege of closing the old Phoenixville road to the west of the church property. This was done without cutting off access to the church by the old road from the south; this road became merely a spur from the Church Road and ended at the churchyard gate. Additional acreage apparently did not materialise.

Dr. Prevost from the time he entered the parish had always been keenly interested in St. Peter's history and he so continued to the end of his life. In March, 1913, he proposed to the Vestry an institution that has proved of great and enduring value. The Vestry minutes record that "The Rector stated he had been considering having an Annual Day, consisting of Service in the morning; luncheon; and addresses in the afternoon. 

It was upon motion, duly seconded: 

Resolved: That the Vestry heartily co-operates with the Rector in his suggestion in having Annual Day Services, to be held about the middle of June in each year."

These Annual Day services and meetings were designed to foster a more general interest and just pride in the history of the parish; to bring together the friends and former members of St. Peter's and others who, for one cause or another, felt concern for the preservation and maintenance of the old church; and to form a bond whereby their united action might contribute to the benefit and usefulness of this ancient monument of churchmanship in the Welsh Barony. 

Out of the Annual Day observances grew the St. Peter's 

Annual Day Association, an organisation that has consistently and with telling effect directed its energies to the upkeep of the church and restoration of the fabric to its pristine condition. Dr. Prevost was president of the Association until 1936 when Arthur King Wood, a devoted and loyal

Vestryman, succeeded him and retained the post until his death in 1938. Two other Vestrymen also, Richard Haugh-

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley ton and David Seaboldt, have been particularly active in furthering the aims of the Association. The value of its influence in behalf of St. Peter's cannot be over-estimated. 

For twenty-five years Mrs. Isaac S. Young was Secretary 

Treasurer of the Association and laboured devotedly to further its aims.

In 1925, much to the regret of the Vestry and congregation, Dr. Prevost resigned the rectorship of St. Peter's in order "to take up other work at the request of the Bishop." 

He nevertheless retained his affection for St. Peter's and a deep concern for the welfare of the parish, continuing to officiate there whenever circumstances permitted and remaining Rector Emeritus until his death in September, 1937. 

After Dr. Prevost's resignation, St. Peter's was closed for several years, and then came under the care of various Priests-in-Charge, among them Fr. Warren Randolph 

Yeakel and Fr. Charles H. Urban. The Reverend Thomas 

A. Meryweather was Rector for a brief period but he was soon taken away for other duties in the Diocese. The Reverend Caleb Cresson, V, took charge in September, 1939. 

As one looks back over the long and varied history of this old Colonial parish, it is impossible not to be profoundly impressed by the inherent vitality displayed in the face of discouraging drawbacks. In spite of long periods when there was no Rector and the congregation were entirely without pastoral care; in spite of frequent serious losses by removal of parishioners to other neighbourhoods; in spite of almost continuously insufficient care because of having to share one priest with one or more neighbouring parishes; and in spite of the strength inevitably drawn away from the parent church at the founding of each new mission, St. Peter's has lived on in abiding usefulness, through the blessing of God and the steadfast loyalty of those who have unremittingly cherished the foundation laid by staunch Welsh Churchmen more than two hundred and forty years ago. With such a background, and with conditions becoming increasingly favourable, the future holds a bright prospect.

Note As this book goes to press, the work of restoration has only begun. 

The profits from the sale of this parish history will be devoted to the cost of restoration. The returns from this source, however, will be far from enough to meet the total expense and it is the earnest hope of the committee in charge of the work that those whose forebears have at some time had a connection with the parish, and other interested persons also, may feel impelled to contribute to the fund required to complete the undertaking. So far, they have been encouraged by one generous gift. May they hope that others likewise will be moved to make some offering according to their ability? 

Contributions may be sent to the Accounting Warden, St. Peter's 

Church in the Great Valley, Paoli, Pennsylvania.

NOTE 

This book brings the parochial chronicle to 1940. It is meet, however, that there be some note of parish life since that date, in accordance with the earnest wish of one of the most active Vestrymen and faithful workers, David E. Seaboldt. It is, indeed, owing in no small measure to Mr. Seaboldt's untiring efforts that the spiritual life of the parish has continued to function in times of stress and in the face of manifold obstacles and discouragements-this in contradistinction to the usual attitude of the Annual Day visitors, with its emphasis on the past and its indifference to present opportunities for service and the obligations incumbent upon the parish. 

Valuing the historic associations of St. Peter's as highly as anyone, Mr. Seaboldt, nevertheless, has consistently acted on the conviction that the church "has a part to play in the life of the community, that future generations may find inspiration within these walls to think highly, act nobly and live usefully."

A number of years ago, at Mr. Seaboldt's instance the Church 

School began holding its sessions in the morning, before the morning service, instead of in the afternoon, with gratifying results in at-

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley tendance. During long periods when clerical ministrations were confined to monthly visits, Mr. Seaboldt, as a lay-reader licensed by the Bishop, maintained the services every Sunday without interruption, thus following the precedent of his eighteenth-century predecessors in the parish. It was at Mr. Seaboldt's instance, too, that a number of week-day services were started at such times as the people of the neighbourhood could attend; likewise a weekly meeting of the Young People's Fellowship, with encouraging response. 

At this writing there are 100 communicants in the parish; the 

Church School numbers 55; there is an active Women's Guild numbering 16; and the Young People's Fellowship has a membership of 15.

There is unquestionably a field for extensive and useful work in the neighbourhood and, now that a priest has been appointed who can devote more time to the parish needs than could his more recent predecessors, there is every prospect, under God's blessing, of extending the scope of parish usefulness. 20202020 20 2020 2020 

Through the generosity and courteous consideration of Mrs.

Richard W. Davids, the pewter Communion Service and Christening 

Bowl, exchanged by the Vestry in 1864 for a stone Font (facing p. 

117), have now been restored to the possession of St. Peter's Churchin-the-Great Valley since this history was written. 

Mrs. Davids, hitherto its rightful legal owner, has unselfishly and at her own suggestion, given back the pewter to the Parish. For this gracious act, the members of the Parish express their sincere appreciation and heartfelt gratitude. 

July 15, 1944.

Gravestone Inscriptions in the Churchyard 

153 

Elizabeth Ralston, wife of Robert Ralston, Esq., d. Dec., 1797, aged 

75 years 2 months 9 days. 

Farewell my husband dear 

My children and my friends 

In hope to rest in Jesus Christ 

When all my pains will end.

Sacred to the memory of Thomas Wilson who departed this life 

April 17, 1826 in the 87th year of his age.

Here lie the remains of Elloner Wilson, wife of Thomas Wilson, who departed this life, Sebt. 18, 1823, in the 80th year of her age. 

Then why lament departed friends 

Or shake at death's alarms, Death's but the servant Jesus sends 

To call us to his arms. 

Mary Wilson, wife of Thomas Wilson, jr., d. Nov. 11, 1801, in her 26th year. "My Race is Run my Life is done 

And I lye in the Ground 

Intomb'd in clay until the Day 

I hear the Trumpet sound."

MARBLE SHAFT, SIDE NO. I 

Cromwell Pearce b Aug 13 1772 d April 12 1852 a devoted member of the

Protestant Epis Church a faithful officer military and civil and an exemplary citizen of the Republic

SIDE NO. 3. 

This memorial is erected by his son L.G pearce.

SIDE NO. 2. 

He filled the civil office of High 

Sheriff and Associate Judge of Chester Co. with approved fidelity and acceptable unpartiality.

SIDE No. 4. 

Colonel of the 16th Reg't U.S. In fantry in the war of 1812 The 

Battle fields of Little York, Fort George, Williamsburg abd 

Lyons Creek attest his bravery.

The Church of St. Peter-in-the-Great Valley
